# 西力東漸 - 中葡早期接觸追昔

[]

Palgrave Studies in Comparative Global History

Series Editors

Lucio De Sousa

Tokyo University of Foreign Studies, Tokyo, Japan

Manuel Perez-Garcia

Shanghai Jiao Tong University, Shanghai, China

This series proposes a new geography of Global History research using
Asian and Western sources, welcoming quality research and engaging
outstanding scholarship from China, Europe and the Americas. Promoting
academic excellence and critical intellectual analysis, it offers a rich
source of global history research in sub-continental areas of Europe,
Asia (notably China, Japan and the Philippines) and the Americas and
aims to help understand the divergences and convergences between East
and West.

1. Introduction

James Masaki Fujitani¹  

(1)


 

 


Email: james.fujitani@nottingham.edu.cn

The Traditional Story of the Embassy

Before discussing the argument of this book, it is important to know the
story of the 1517 Portuguese embassy to China. What follows is the
standard account of the events, which was first formulated in 1934 by
Tien-Tse Chang in his foundational work, Sino-Portuguese Trade from 1514
to 1644. Subsequently, it was adopted by major historians such as
Charles Boxer and Armando Cortesão, thus becoming the mainstream
scholarly view today.

The traditional narrative notes that the embassy came within the
momentum of the Age of Exploration. The Portuguese had been rapidly
expanding across the globe during the late fifteenth and early sixteenth
centuries. After arriving in India in 1498, and after conquering the
Sultanate of Melaka (near Singapore today) in 1511, they wished to make
contact with what was rumored to be the largest and richest nation in
the region: a place even further to the east called “China.”^([1]) For a
few years, the Portuguese captains sent ships to the Middle Kingdom.
While these sailors did succeed in trading there, they were unable to
conduct diplomacy. The Chinese officials told them that if the King of
Portugal wished to have official relations with the Emperor of China,
then he would have to send an embassy to give tribute.^([2]) This
message slowly made its way back to Europe.

In 1516, King Manuel I of Portugal ordered Captain Fernão Peres de
Andrade to transport an ambassador to China. Andrade arrived in 1517,
and, after some initial challenges, he succeeded in sending diplomats to
meet with the provincial council. The council decided to temporarily
accept the Portuguese ambassador while they consulted the Court.^([3])
Having accomplished his mission, Andrade then departed and returned back
to Portugal. All in all, he is considered to be the most successful of
the captain majors to China.^([4])

A second captain arrived in 1519: Fernão Peres’ younger brother, Simão
de Andrade. He was a violent and aggressive young man who offended his
hosts in numerous ways: building a fortress on a coastal island,
purchasing Chinese children as slaves, repeatedly disobeying the
law.^([5]) Tien-Tse Chang believed that Chinese sources such as the
Mingshi (明史) and the Yueshan Congtan (月山叢談) confirmed the dark
legends about him.^([6]) Because of his recklessness, the Ming officials
began to distrust the newcomers and suspect their intentions.

Nonetheless, in 1520, the Portuguese embassy received permission to
proceed to the Ming Court. The chief ambassador was Tomé Pires, an
apothecary and erudite cosmographer.^([7]) Another member was Cristóvão
Vieira, who later wrote a detailed record of the events.^([8]) Vieira
said that in Beijing, the Chinese officials were furious about the
Portuguese diplomatic letters, considering them to be false
translations.^([9]) To make matters worse, they also began to receive
reports about Simão de Andrade and his misbehavior.^([10])
Furthermore, hostile ambassadors sent by the Sultan of Bintan (the
former Sultan of Melaka) arrived in China, denouncing the Portuguese for
having seized their lands. Finally, the Ming Emperor himself, who had
shown some grace to the westerners passed away.^([11]) At that point,
their tribute was refused, and they were dismissed from Beijing.

In Guangdong, a battle broke out between the Chinese and Portuguese: the
so-called Battle of Tamão.^([12]) The local officials had asked the
Portuguese merchants to leave during the deceased Emperor’s mourning
period. But they wanted to continue to trade and refused to depart. The
Guangdong navy thus surrounded them and began a long war of attrition.
The Portuguese ships were only able to escape when a typhoon arose in
September 1521, breaking up the Ming blockade.^([13]) Thus ended the
1521 embassy: a diplomatic failure and a military defeat.

Again, what just preceded is the mainstream scholarly understanding of
the 1517 Portuguese embassy. In this book, I will generally refer to it
as the “traditional narrative.”

Interpretations of the Embassy’s Failure

The 1517 embassy was the first “modern” contact between Europe and
China. Certainly there had been relations between the two regions long
before—Greek merchants traveling down the Silk Road, Byzantine envoys in
the tenth century, the famous Marco Polo in the thirteenth century, and
many others. But none of these previous interactions had been durable.
In contrast, the Portuguese embassy of 1517 created a relationship that
has proven long-lasting. From 1517 up until today, Portugal and China
have been aware of each other and have remained in dialogue.
Furthermore, through Portugal and its port at Macau, the Spanish also
came into contact with China, then the Dutch, then the English. In
short, the 1517 embassy was the beginning of all modern Sino-European
relations.

But the experience ended in failure. This was not only the first
European embassy to see the Ming Emperor, but also the last. It set a
negative legal precedent which the Ming Court would uphold until the
very end, rejecting not only subsequent Portuguese requests to give
tribute, but also the requests of all other European countries.^([14])
Furthermore, the affair ended with the first recorded military battle
between Europe and China: the battle of Tamão. Accordingly, for over a
century, historians have debated over what went wrong. Why did the Ming
court reject the embassy? What were the causes of the conflict?

There are three major explanations. First of all, many historians have
argued that the cause was European colonialism. Afonso de Albuquerque
had attacked the Sultan of Melaka and conquered his territory.
Consequently, the Ming officials, who viewed Melaka as a vassal state,
looked upon the Portuguese with distrust.^([15]) Their fears were only
confirmed by Simão de Andrade’s aggressive fortress-building and
slave-trafficking. In rejecting the embassy, they were thus rejecting
Western imperialism. This perspective has become particularly prominent
in Chinese historiography. In 1934, Tien-Tse Chang explained the violent
attitude of Andrade as follows:

  What actually accounts for this matter-of-course attitude of the
  Portuguese? The answer must be largely sought in the Portuguese
  conception of their relationships to the countries they visited. The
  Portuguese believed they had come to Asia to “discover.” […] As the
  early Portuguese authorities, merchants and sailors looked upon the
  countries they had visited as the fruits of their discovery, it is but
  logical that they considered it their right to enjoy such fruits in
  whatever way they desired.^([16])

Similarly, in his comprehensive history of Sino-Portugese relations,
Huang Qinghua wrote that:

  The Portuguese policy of overseas invasion, expansion, and pillaging,
  as seen in the unchecked daring and rebellious bravado of Portuguese
  men such as Simão [de Andrade], their rampaging across Chinese
  territory, their violence, abuse, and deceitful behavior […]
  determined the failure of the Portuguese embassy and the termination
  of Sino-Portuguese relations.^([17])

The story of the 1517 embassy thus becomes an early episode in the
tragic history of colonialism and anti-colonialism.^([18])

A second explanation for the failure takes the opposite perspective.
Some historians have instead blamed Chinese isolationism. The Ming
Dynasty was Han-centric and inward-turned. It looked down on other
peoples as barbarians and it limited contact with them, which inevitably
led to tension with the Portuguese. In his history of Ming foreign
relations, Chao Zhongzhen wrote:

  The Portuguese came to China, and at that time their main goal was to
  develop trade with China. For the Portuguese, developing foreign trade
  was urgently necessary in order to develop economically. But China’s
  situation was the opposite. It was still in a natural economy, a
  feudal great power with a hegemonic position. It had no strong need to
  develop foreign trade. The Ming Court always promoted the policy of
  the “Maritime Ban.” […] These sorts of contradictions between China
  and Portugal made it difficult to avoid a clash.^([19])

This historical perspective implicitly places the 1517 embassy within
the story of the “Great Divergence”: the rise of Europe and the decline
of China.^([20]) The Ming Empire was trapped in a traditional mentality,
unable to look forward toward the dawning global economy. When it
excluded the Europeans from its international system, it unwittingly
invited them to create their own rival system. Jin Guoping has
forcefully written:

  The arrival of other nations that were unknown to China created a new
  international reality. But China continued on, a prisoner to [its
  former] policies, which had long become outdated and
  anachronistic.^([21])

From this perspective, the struggle between the Portuguese and the
Chinese in 1517 already pointed toward the chaotic nineteenth century,
and the collapse of the tribute system.

A third major explanation avoids blaming either side. Misunderstandings
were simply inevitable, for the two parties had never encountered each
other before and their cultures were very different.^([22]) Again, Jin
Guoping has pinpointed this problem:

  Perhaps it would be better to consider these frictions and accidents
  against the background of cultural and civilizational conflicts. From
  that point of view, these peripeties of the embassy of Tomé Pires were
  unavoidable.^([23])

Similarly, the historian of Ming international relations, Wan Ming,
wrote that “due to linguistic barriers on both sides, differences in
rituals and customs, and a lack of mutual understanding, the clash
became unavoidable.”^([24]) She points out numerous cases in
which miscommunication caused tension, noting, for example, that upon
his arrival in Guangzhou, Fernão Peres de Andrade fired off a cannon
salute to greet his hosts. But the Guangdong officials misunderstood
this, seeing it as an act of aggression.^([25])

To summarize, there are three major explanations for the failure of the
embassy: (1) European colonialism (2) Chinese isolationism (3) Cultural
misunderstandings. It is clear that these are not simply
micro-explanations for the immediate circumstances of 1517. These are,
in fact, the great metanarratives of the entire history of Sino-European
relations.

The Approach of This Book

This book seeks to revise the traditional narrative of the embassy, as
well as the three explanations outlined above. A revision is certainly
overdue. Since the publication of Tien-Tse Chang’s book in 1934, there
have been many important historiographical breakthroughs which have
profoundly changed our understandings of both Portugal and China.

On the Portuguese side, historians have come to a different view of
Iberian colonialism. They have questioned idea that the sailors and
soldiers in Asia were direct agents of imperialism. For example, Sanjay
Subrahmanyam has noted that a large portion of Portuguese colonial
society was actually “made up of footloose and free-wheeling traders and
mercenaries, renegades.”^([26]) Such colonists often had different goals
than the Crown and even competed against it.^([27]) Furthermore, the
degree to which these people were culturally European has also been
questioned. Serge Gruzinski has argued that the Portuguese often
linguistically and culturally integrated into the local Asian
communities.^([28])

Views of the Ming Empire’s foreign policy have also changed. In the last
thirty years, scholars have increasingly celebrated China’s rich history
of international relations, with particular focus on the Maritime Silk
Road.^([29]) Scholars such as Chao Zhongchen, Wan Ming, and Cheng
Wing-Sheung have shown that while the empire did hold to a restrictive
foreign policy, this policy was not rigid^([30]): “The Ming policy of
the Maritime Ban had periods of both tightening and loosening,” wrote
Chao Zhongchen.^([31]) Furthermore, historians have succeeded in
identifying the specific imperial agencies that handled international
affairs.^([32]) It is now possible to analyze each office, each faction,
and observe how they collaborated, and also how they competed against
each other.

In addition to studies on Portugal and China, there has also been much
new research on the broader international context of the time. Studies
on Early Modern Southeast Asia in particular have surged into view, with
Anthony Reid calling the sixteenth century an “Age of Commerce.”^([33])
Southeast Asia was the trade hub of the world, the crux for between the
Indian subcontinent and East Asia.^([34]) Studies on Central Asia have
developed as well, albeit more slowly. Long neglected, the region’s
vibrant kingdoms and their historical significance are now being
recognized.^([35]) Knowing how the Ming Empire related to these other
regions helps to understand how it related to Portugal as well.

This book thus seeks to bring together these more recent understandings
and apply them to the case of the 1517 embassy. Methodologically, it
adopts the approach of “Connected History”: it crosses over national
boundaries and joins together long-separated scholarly
traditions.^([36]) In doing so, it also tries to correct an imbalance in
the traditional narrative. The reader might have noticed that Tien-Tse
Chang did not give equal weight to both sides. He quietly took the
perspective of the Portuguese officers: Fernão Peres de Andrade, Simão
de Andrade, and Tomé Pires. While this approach is effective in
highlighting the strengths and weaknesses of the Europeans, it sheds
little light on the motivations of the Chinese. This makes it difficult
to understand the failure of the embassy, for it was the latter who
decided to cut off relations, not the former. Even today, the story of
the Ming officials’ decision-making process remains largely untold.
Accordingly, this book seeks to redress the equation. It gathers
numerous Chinese sources, including several previously unstudied pieces,
in order to reconstruct the viewpoint of the imperial court.

Broadly speaking, this book develops an argument that the great
historian John Wills briefly sketched out in 2010:

  There was no conclusive rejection of the embassy until after the death
  of the [Zhengde] Emperor on April 19, 1521, which was followed by
  major political changes, as scholar-officials turned against eunuch
  influence at court. […] With the temporary ascendancy of Grand
  Secretary Yang Tinghe and the general turn against eunuch influence, a
  decision to reject the embassy and forbid all relations with the
  Portuguese […] was a foregone conclusion.^([37])

Following Wills, the following chapters focus on the competition between
the Grand Secretary Yang Tinghe and his political rivals. When his
rivals held power, the Portuguese embassy was able to advance its
diplomatic agenda. But when Tinghe eventually triumphed, he crushed his
opponents and drove the Europeans away.

This argument is spread out over thirteen chapters. The first five
introduce the political context of the Ming Empire in the late fifteenth
and early sixteenth centuries. Chapter 1 introduces Yang Tinghe himself:
his personality, his philosophy, and his role as chief of the
Secretariat. The second, third, fourth, and fifth chapters introduce his
rivals: the Supreme Commander of Guangdong-Guangxi, the Foreign Trade
Eunuchs, the Minister of War Wang Qiong, and the Zhengde Emperor
himself. These figures not only clashed with Yang Tinghe on a personal
level, but also promoted alternative, non-traditional diplomatic
policies.

The second half of the book then turns toward the Portuguese, arguing
that their initial successes in China were largely due to the protection
that they received from the rival factions. Chapter 6 focuses on
Portuguese shipping under the trade policy of the Guangdong-Guangxi
Supreme Commander. Chapters 7 and 8 show how the ambassadors received
support from the Foreign Trade Eunuchs and from Wang
Wiong, respectively. Chapter 9 then shows how they joined the personal
entourage of the Zhengde Emperor himself in Nanjing. In contrast,
Chapters 10 and 11 describe their tense interactions with Tinghe’s own
faction under the Ministry of Rites in Beijing.

Chapter 12 is the climax and turning point of the book: it describes how
Yang Tinghe seized power after the death of the Zhengde Emperor, and
subsequently punished his rivals. It was within this political upheaval
that he also rejected the Portuguese embassy. Chapter 13, however,
argues that it was not Tinghe who called for the naval battle in
Guangdong, but rather the Guangdong officials themselves, who wished to
safeguard themselves during this sensitive transition time.

This book thus takes the approach of microhistory, putting aside the
gigantic meta narratives to focus on the detailed
human interactions. Nonetheless, I hope that it will not only offer a
deeper understanding of 1517 embassy, but also of the broader Early
Modern world. The following pages will sketch the portrait of a global
community that cannot be reduced to a simple rivalry between East and
West, one that swarmed with numerous groups, each with its own
objectives and aspirations. When the Portuguese came to the heavenly
court, they in fact entered a bustling forum in which they found both
admirers and detractors, allies and enemies.

Notes on Transliteration and Use of Chinese

- All historical Chinese names and titles are written using traditional
  characters (繁體), but transliterated using pinyin.

- For Chinese people, the characters are given once, at the first
  occurrence of the name. When abbreviating names, I have adopted the
  classical Chinese style of calling people by their first name (名)
  rather than by their last name (姓).

- For the names of Chinese books and documents, the Chinese characters
  are also given at the first occurrence. The titles are written out as
  pinyin transliterations, but an English translation can be found in
  the bibliography. For clarity’s sake, the bibliography also writes out
  the pinyin with a space between each character.

- For the titles of Ming government officials, I have tried as much as
  possible to follow the nomenclature of Charles Hucker.^([38])

- In Chinese documents of the time, the Portuguese were called the
  “Folangji (佛郎機),” that is to say, “the Franks.” However, for both
  clarity and simplicity, I translate “Folangji” as “Portugal.”

- For the coordination of Chinese and Western dates, I have relied upon
  Keith Hazelton’s precious open-access work, A Synchronic
  Chinese-Western Daily Calendar 1341–1661 AD.^([39])

- All translations are mine, unless otherwise indicated.

2. The Decline of the Secretariat


(1)


 

 



The Secretariat’s Struggle for Power

The Senior Grand Secretary (内閣首輔) is the protagonist of this book.
He was the leader of the Secretariat (内閣).^([40]) This was a group of
about three to eight individuals, called Grand Secretaries (内閣大學士),
who collectively ran the government in the name of the Emperor. The
Senior Grand Secretary was first among them, and his power was akin to
that of a Prime Minister.^([41])

Nonetheless, the grand secretaries had a curious status. Originally, the
founder of the Ming dynasty, the Hongwu Emperor (洪武帝, reigned
1368–1398), followed the tradition of the Song and Yuan dynasties,
appointing one powerful civil official: the Grand Chancellor
(丞相).^([42]) However, over the course of his reign, he grew
increasingly jealous for power and feared that his chancellor might
challenge him. In 1379, he thus dismissed him, executed him, and
abolished the office altogether. He himself took direct control of civil
authority. However, this made for more work than one person could
handle. Accordingly, his son, the Yongle Emperor (永樂帝, reigned
1402–1424), appointed secretaries to help with the burden.^([43]) They
accompanied him throughout the day, recording what he said, writing out
his decrees, and then executing them. They did not have any power of
their own—they were just his assistants.

Over time, the secretaries gained more and more de facto power. From the
beginning, their direct access to the emperor already gave them
considerable informal influence. Indeed, for minor decisions, the
emperor often allowed them to take action without asking him.^([44])
This trend intensified over the course of the fifteenth century, as the
successive rulers withdrew further and further from direct governance.
In the process, they delegated more and more decision-making power to
their subordinates. By mid-century, the secretaries had become largely
autonomous officials, able to independently formulate policy in the name
of the emperor (票擬).^([45]) But this authority was an informal,
without any explicit basis in the law.^([46])

Efforts were made to give the grand secretaries more dignity, to give
them honors that were commensurate with their real authority. By the
mid-fifteenth century, a cursus honorum was established: aspiring
officials necessarily had to be one of the top three highest scorers in
the imperial exam (一甲). They then entered Hanlin Academy (翰林院), and
after several years’ training there, they entered one of the Six
Ministries (六部), which were technically the highest civil institutions
of the empire.^([47]) Finally, from the ministries they were promoted
into the Secretariat.^([48]) Nonetheless, even after becoming
secretaries, they continued to concurrently hold an honorary position as
a minister, for it assured their place among the highest-ranked
officials.^([49]) However, this rank did not come from their status as
secretary, but rather from that of honorary minister.

Lacking clear legal power, the secretaries tried all the more to enhance
their cultural power.^([50]) They asserted that their policies were
wiser and more just than those of their rivals, for they had spent
decades studying the Confucian classics, the histories, and the laws.
For example, the funeral eulogy of the Grand Secretary Li Xian (李賢),
who passed away in 1467, praised him in the following way:

  According to each matter, he advised and exhorted appropriately. As to
  his character, he rejoiced in reading books. In both work and free
  time, his hands always held a scroll. He loved discussing
  Neo-Confucianism (性理之學).^([51])

The secretaries thus distinguished themselves from their rivals through
their erudition. They contrasted themselves to the eunuchs, who were
often illiterate; to the military officers, who only had a basic grasp
of Confucianism; and even to the lower scholar officials, who were
younger and less well-read. Nobody else had so many years of both study
and experience.

Because of that, in their memorials, the secretaries often made show of
their wisdom. For example, when the young Zhengde Emperor (正德帝,
reigned 1505–1521) did not wish to attend his daily lectures, the Senior
Grand Secretary Liu Jian (劉健) reasoned with him:

  From ancient times, there were no emperors who did not invest in study
  lectures in order to perfect their virtue. The Shangshu (商書) says:
  “to learn the lessons of the ancients is the way to attain this.” The
  Book of Poetry (詩經) says “I will learn to hold fast the gleams [of
  knowledge], till I arrive at bright intelligence.” […] Mengzi (孟子)
  says: “Suppose the case of the most easily growing thing in the world;
  if you let it have one day's genial heat, and then expose it for ten
  days to cold, it will not be able to grow.” We planned for [your
  lecture] today, but we are now exposing it to cold, and we do not know
  for how many days!^([52])

Liu Jian grounded his advice firmly in classical wisdom. Indeed, the
Shangshu, the Book of Poetry, and the Mengzi all belonged to the
Confucian Four Books and Five Classics. Jian compensated for his lack of
political power by highlighting his intellectual power.

In the later fifteenth century, as factional rivals began to gain more
influence, the Secretariat gradually lost its de facto authority. The
problem was that the emperors increasingly distanced themselves from the
scholars. Indeed, during his entire reign, the Chenghua Emperor (成化帝,
reigned 1464–1487) only formally met with the Secretariat a single
time.^([53]) And this only happened because the junior Grand Secretary
Peng Shi (彭時) begged the Emperor. The court records say that on
January 25, 1472, Shi said:

  [Your highness] has not yet deigned to show his face to us for a
  single meeting. We, your lowly servants, have heard the sentiments of
  the people. [The people] are discussing it with agitation! Tomorrow
  morning, after your highness has finished the audience and returned to
  the palace to rest, we ask to see you: first, to ease your heart, and
  then also to calm the people's talking.^([54])

The Emperor conceded. The next day, he saw the secretaries in person.
But rumors said that the meeting was brief and insubstantial. A Qing-era
source records an anecdote:

  [Senior Grand Secretary] Wan An bowed, cried out “Long live the
  Emperor!” and then wished to leave. [Junior Grand Secretaries] Peng
  Shi and Shang Lu had no choice but to follow. They all bowed and
  withdrew. The eunuchs made fun of the [secretaries], saying, “Those
  fellows often said that the emperor didn’t summon them to see them.
  But when they got to see him, they only yelled ‘long live the
  emperor!’” For a while they laughingly called them the
  “long-live-the-emperor grand secretaries.” After that, the emperor
  never summoned them again.^([55])

The Hongzhi Emperor (弘治帝, reigned 1487–1505) was similarly
uninterested in his secretaries, and only met with them a few times over
the course of his reign.^([56]) In 1492, one court inspector pleaded
with him on their behalf:

  Sometimes, please meet with the high officials and consult with them
  on matters. Open up communication channels and put their advice to
  action.^([57])

By the end of the fifteenth century, while the secretaries continued to
oversee the routine functions of the government, they no longer had full
control over the administration. They had lost the true source of their
power: their connection with the Son of Heaven.

The Education of the Young Prince

In the 1490s, the scholar officials were despairing over the present
crisis, and they turned their eyes toward the future. They hoped to
educate the young prince, Zhu Houzhao (朱厚照), and teach him to love
both Confucius and the Secretariat. In 1494, one of the towering
officials of the era, the Minister of War Ma Wensheng (馬文升), thus
appealed to the Hongzhi Emperor with a request:

  I have heard that the imperial prince is extraordinary in appearance
  and wise in character. As of now, his mind and abilities are still
  pure. They have not yet been subjected to temptation. […] So I beg of
  your highness: choose pure-hearted, faithful, and erudite palace
  officials […] to hold him and support him.^([58])

Wensheng specifically wanted the young prince, only three years old at
the time, to learn the way the scholar, carefully citing specific
Confucian virtues:

  Teach him not to be permissive, teach him to have standards [儀]: in
  order to nurture a heart of benevolence and justice [仁義] in him.
  Teach him to be respectful, teach him to distinguish: in order to
  nurture a heart of propriety and wisdom [禮智] in him.^([59])

Wensheng did not want the prince to be taught other forms of morality,
pointedly saying:

  As to the teachings of the old Buddha, most of all, it is not good for
  him chant such words with his mouth or to see the statues with his
  eyes, which would lead his heart’s desires astray.^([60])

The Emperor reacted to the request in his usual fashion: he warmly
welcomed the idea and approved it. And then he put it aside and
apparently forgot about it. No action was taken on Ma Wensheng’s
memorial.^([61])

Two years later, in 1496, the old Minister of War thus stepped forward
again and reminded the Hongzhi Emperor of his earlier request, asking
now to appoint preceptors for the prince. The Emperor again acquiesced,
and the Senior Grand Secretary Xu Pu (徐溥) as well as the Minister of
Personnel (吏部尚書) began recruiting. They selected twelve Hanlin
officials. Among the team was an upcoming thirty-seven-year-old man
named Yang Tinghe.^([62])

Introducing Yang Tinghe

It was no coincidence that Yang Tinghe was selected, for he had already
gained a reputation as a promising young official. According to one of
his biographies, the Grand Secretary Li Dongyang (李東陽) praised him in
the highest terms:

  His character and reputation are both preeminent. He has many years of
  achievements in compiling records—not of the common sort!^([63])

Born in 1459, Tinghe came from a family of scholars. His father was a
government schoolmaster.^([64]) He himself was a boy genius who had
passed the regional exams at the age of twelve, and then the
Metropolitan Exam at the age of nineteen, finishing as one of the top
three candidates.^([65]) By all accounts, he was a person of great calm
and poise. The Mingshi (明史) says that “his bearing was elegant, his
personality was tranquil and meticulous.”^([66]) He was a defender of
ancestral rituals and Confucian ideals. In one of his first recorded
memorials as a grand secretary, he described his own principles, saying
that he acted “in accordance with the laws of the accumulated reigns up
till now, from the ancient ancestors, via classical studies.”^([67])
Accordingly, he bore the great hopes of his elders: he was a
next-generation scholar who could guide the future emperor.

No record says precisely what Yang Tinghe and the young Zhu Houzhao did
together in the Spring Palace, but they must have formed a relationship
of trust. In 1505, the thirteen-year-old prince ascended the throne, and
became the Zhengde Emperor—the name by which he will be called in this
book. Among the team of twelve that had originally been assigned as
tutors, Yang Tinghe now emerged as the most favored. In 1507, he was
promoted to be a junior member of the Secretariat.^([68]) In an official
memorial, he admitted that this honor was due to his previous
relationship with the Emperor:

  Your servant, although humble and inept, has been blessed to receive
  the caring recognition of the sacred emperor. An old servant of the
  Spring Palace, elevated into the Secretariat…^([69])

The Emperor also affirmed this relationship, saying to Tinghe:

  From the time of the Spring Palace, I have carefully observed you.
  Then from the beginning of my inheriting the throne, I elevated you to
  the Secretariat, and continuously promoted you, my old tutor.^([70])

Nonetheless, despite his favored status, Yang Tinghe was not exempt from
the troubles of the time. During the early Zhengde years, the
Secretariat fell into even greater disarray as one of the most powerful
eunuchs in the history of the Ming dynasty, the eunuch Liu Jin (劉瑾)
seized control of the government.^([71]) Jin looked jealously upon the
secretaries. According to one collection of scandalous stories, he
pushed the adolescent Emperor to rid himself of all the old officials
who had served his father, saying:

  During the Hongzhi years, all the court’s power was held by the
  scholars of the Secretariat. The court itself only had an empty
  name.^([72])

Thus, Liu Jin forced each of the veteran officials to retire,
one-by-one. This included the Grand Secretary Liu Jian and the Minister
of War Ma Wensheng—the people who had originally mentored Yang Tinghe.
In the end, only one of the Hongzhi-era grand secretaries remained: the
venerable Li Dongyang. But he could only survive because he was a
personal friend of Liu Jin, and because he was willing to follow the
eunuch’s lead.^([73]) Later, many scholars at the court criticized him
for this. One official wrote a harsh memorial, saying:

  Recently, Liu Jin monopolized power and disordered governance. Li
  Dongyang just cared for his own life. Although a high official, he
  could not stop the danger early. Li Dongyang just submissively obeyed
  [Liu Jin], and carefully followed orders.^([74])

Many years afterward, in the preface to his own collected writings, Yang
Tinghe admitted that he agreed with such criticisms:

  In many cases, we disagreed. When dealing with major issues of benefit
  or harm, if one has knowledge, then one must speak. And if one speaks,
  then one must follow through! […] If one does not keep control, then
  things get worse with each day, and the nation is increasingly
  degraded.^([75])

That is to say, Tinghe disapproved of Li Dongyang’s passivity. He wanted
the Secretariat to play a stronger role.

It was inevitable that Liu Jin would eventually target Tinghe. One day,
as the grand secretary gave the daily Confucian lecture, the Zhengde
Emperor grew bored. The seventeen-year-old monarch turned to Liu Jin and
complained: “these meal-time lectures, why do they have to overflow with
so many words?” Jin then sent Tinghe away from the capital, down to
Nanjing.^([76]) In his place, the eunuch appointed his own allies,
people who followed his directions, such as Grand Secretary Jiao Fang
(焦芳).^([77])

Eventually, Yang Tinghe did return.^([78]) And eventually, after Liu Jin
was executed in 1510, and after Li Dongyang retired in 1512, Tinghe even
rose to the highest level of leadership. At fifty-three years of age, he
became the Senior Grand Secretary, the most powerful scholar official in
the empire.^([79])

Although this book will often focus on Yang Tinghe, it is important to
note that he had a team supporting him. As senior Grand Secretary,
Tinghe chose his allies carefully. He filled the Secretariat with loyal
friends: the junior secretaries Jiang Mian (蔣冕), Jin Gui (靳貴), Fei
Hong (費宏), and Mao Ji (毛紀).^([80]) His adherents usually held four
out of the five seats of the Secretariat, giving him considerable
strength to push through his policies. (The fifth seat, however, was
secretary Liang Chu (梁儲), a bitter rival.) His other great ally was
the Minister of Rites (禮部尚書), who oversaw court ritual and, to some
extent, foreign diplomacy. In this book, the Ministers Liu Chun (劉春)
and Mao Cheng (毛澄) will play particularly prominent roles.

The ascension of Yang Tinghe as Senior Grand Secretary marks the
beginning of this book. In many ways, the failure of the Portuguese
embassy is but an episode in the bigger story of how he tried to restore
the power and dignity of the Secretariat.

3. The Supreme Commander and Foreign Trade in Guangdong


(1)


 

 



Introducing the Supreme Commander

This chapter focuses on one rival to the Secretariat: the Supreme
Commander of the Guangdong-Guangxi super province (兩廣總督). This
official was sometimes also called the “Viceroy,” for he ruled the
region in the name of the Emperor.^([81])

The position developed gradually over the course of the fifteenth
century.^([82]) It was not part of the original government structure set
up by the founder of the Ming dynasty. As mentioned in the previous
chapter, the Hongwu Emperor was jealous for power. He feared that the
provinces might break away as autonomous states, and so he dispersed
the authority of regional officials, dividing it among “Three Offices
(三司)”: the Administration Commission (佈政司), the Military Commission
(指揮司), and the Surveillance Commission (按察司).^([83]) The Three
Offices checked each other’s actions. For major decisions, they were
expected to ask the court for approval.

The problems with this structure soon became apparent.^([84]) Having to
constantly negotiate with each other, the Three Offices could not make
decisions efficiently. Furthermore, they could not realistically consult
the court on major decisions either. Many emergencies required urgent
action. In the distant provinces, letters took weeks to reach Beijing,
and by the time a response came back from the court, it was already too
late. Thus, in the mid-fifteenth century, the court began to reform the
system. It sent more powerful scholar officials to the provinces
dispatching grand coordinators (巡撫) to oversee civil matters, or
military superintendents (提督軍務) to wage wars.^([85]) But to some
extent, this multiplication of officials only dispersed authority even
more. And so finally, toward the end of the fifteenth century, the court
created the position of the supreme commander: one single figure vested
with full authority, combining the powers of both grand coordinators and
military superintendents.^([86])

The Secretariat and the Supreme Commander on Foreign Trade

The supreme commander both helped the grand secretaries and challenged
them. On the one hand, he relieved them of many responsibilities, taking
care of distant regions that were far outside their reach. But on the
other hand, he was very independent.^([87]) He had the right to control
finances, hire and fire officials, and deploy armies, all without asking
for permission from the court.^([88]) He could disregard or even disobey
the Secretariat’s orders, and there was little to stop him.

The Secretariat and the Guangdong-Guangxi Supreme Commander perpetually
disagreed on the question of foreign trade. The Secretariat supported
the official tribute system.^([89]) This famous system called on foreign
kings to come visit China and show their loyalty to the Son of Heaven.
When they arrived in Beijing, they would humbly offer their tribute to
the Emperor, and in return, the Emperor would shower down his generosity
upon them. The system expressed the highest moral values: subordinates
 practicing piety (孝) and superiors practicing magnanimity (慈). It was
not meant to be a vulgar vehicle for trade and money-making. Indeed, the
court made laws specifically to ensure that tribute fleets did not
become merchant convoys: embassies had to present tally slips as
identification,^([90]) and they could only send two tribute ships, with
a maximum of fifty personnel, once every three years.^([91]) With its
high ceremonialism and lofty ethical ideals, the system fit well with
the tastes of the Secretariat and the Ministry of Rites.

In contrast, the supreme commanders of Guangdong-Guangxi were displeased
with the tribute system, for it benefitted the court more than the
provinces.^([92]) In order to receive the visiting embassies, the
commander had to equip a full naval squadron to defend the islands where
tribute ships docked. He had to maintain a Foreign Trade Bureau (市舶司)
and a guesthouse named Huaiyuan Station (懷遠驛), with interpreters and
staff.^([93]) And he had to entertain his guests, giving them gifts and
throwing banquets for them. All of these services cost money and did not
bring any clear advantage to him.

Instead, the supreme commanders preferred a different type of exchange:
private foreign trade. Private trade could generate much more tax
revenue, since the Foreign Trade Bureau could collect customs duties on
all incoming ships: both Chinese ships traveling abroad, and overseas
ships coming to visit. This could help pay for provincial army
expenses.^([94]) Indeed, the military budget for Guangdong-Guangxi was a
perpetual challenge. The provinces were home to a rich diversity of
ethnic groups.^([95]) During the Ming era, this was viewed as a burden,
since these groups often revolted or protested against the
government.^([96]) Furthermore, Guangxi province was poor and could not
pay for the armies on its own. That was why it was paired with
Guangdong, which had a rich coastal area which could help shoulder the
expenses. That coastal area thus had to produce significant revenue,
enough to support two provinces. In 1529, the Supreme Commander Lin Fu
(林富) explained why he wanted to allow foreign trade:

  The entire province of Guangxi completely relies on Guangdong for
  supplies. […] I have examined the matter: in the past, when foreign
  ships came, both public and private supplies were abundant, and
  foreign goods filled the warehouses. In a month, tens of thousands of
  silver taels could be earned. It helped the country by supplying the
  army, which relied on it. And nobody lacked supplies, neither the
  government nor the people.^([97])

In contrast, the Secretariat disliked private foreign trade. To put it
bluntly, such commerce was illegal. At the beginning of the dynasty, the
Hongwu Emperor feared that it would foster criminality and corrupt the
simple morals of the people, causing them to lust after exotic goods. He
was already struggling to control the oceans, with Japanese pirates
ravaging the southern coastline.^([98]) His solution was to impose the
notorious Maritime Ban (海禁).^([99]) This set of laws, which was
steadily expanded over the fifteenth century, declared that no private
ship would be allowed to sail overseas.^([100]) More precisely, the 1505
Ming law code (which Yang Tinghe helped to compile^([101])) declared
that Chinese citizens who sailed abroad to trade would be caned one
hundred times.^([102]) Those who traded in illegal items were to be
strangled to death.^([103])

Nonetheless, despite the Secretariat’s pronouncements, in the 1470s, the
supreme commanders of Guangdong-Guangxi began to welcome foreign
merchants.^([104]) They did so very quietly. In this book, I will call
their system, “the island trade system,” for it took place outside the
city, on the islands at the mouth of the Pearl River delta.^([105]) In
1521, one official described the situation in this way:

  [Foreign] adepts of dirty profit falsely claim to give tribute. They
  come and go without ceasing. And the officials take advantage of their
  authority and cover it up, not forbidding it.^([106])

That is to say, foreign merchant ships arrived and falsely declared that
they were tribute ships. They were thus allowed to wait on the islands
while their identification documents were being checked. One
conscientious supreme commander, Min Gui (閔圭), gave further details in
1493:

  In the coastal areas of Guangdong, many people are privately dealing
  with [foreign] ships. The ships come in an unbroken stream and,
  without waiting for the examination of their tally-slips, [the
  foreign] ships start selling their merchandise.^([107])

In other words, during the waiting period, while their (false)
identification documents were being checked, the merchants quickly sold
off their goods. Even though their tribute request was inevitably
rejected, it mattered little. When they were ordered to leave on the
next monsoon, they happily complied, for they had already finished their
trading. For its part, the Guangdong administration was also satisfied,
for it had received its customs dues. The same pattern was repeated each
year.

By the end of the fifteenth century, island trade had eclipsed official
tribute trade in popularity. Even foreign kings preferred to use the
island system, for it was faster and simpler than the ritualistic
tribute system.^([108]) Indeed, the court records say that during the
entire Hongzhi era, there were only two countries from all of Southeast
Asia which continued to give tribute in Guangzhou: Siam and
Champa.^([109])

Toleration of Foreign Criminality

The Secretariat hated the island trade system not only because it was
illegal, but also because it made the coastline more dangerous.
The supreme commander simply could not maintain security on the trade
islands. He himself was based on the border between Guangdong and
Guangxi, at the city of Wuzhou (梧州). From there, it took a few days to
reach Guangzhou, where the Three Offices were seated. From Guangzhou, it
would be another three days of sailing down the Pearl River to reach the
fortress of Nantou (南頭).^([110]) And from the fortress, one could sail
out to the islands, which were scattered across the river delta. Due to
this distance, the high officials never went out on location. Only navy
ships and the government trade agents ventured out to meet the
foreigners.^([111])

Furthermore, the coastal officials did not enforce law and order. They
were local folks, who cared little for the strict rules made up in
Beijing. The Joseon scholar Choe Bu (崔溥), who shipwrecked in Zhejiang
in 1488, gave a vivid portrait of them. During his one-month adventure,
he met various people along the seaside. Some feared him and avoided
him.^([112]) Others pressured him for bribes.^([113]) He even heard a
rumor that some naval officers had planned to kill him and then ask the
court for a reward for capturing a pirate.^([114]) There were also some
kind-hearted folks who offered him food and shelter.^([115]) In
responding to him, all of these people tended to improvise, rather than
follow an official protocol.

Accordingly, along the coast, smuggling and black market trade went
largely unchecked.^([116]) It was a dangerous world, largely outside of
Ming authority.

Chen Jin and Wu Tingju against Yang Tinghe and Liu Chun

Two individuals who are of particular interest for this book are the
Supreme Commander Chen Jin (陳金) and the Administration Commissioner Wu
Tingju (吳廷擧). Chen Jin was a veteran of border warfare. Born in 1446,
he started his career in Yunnan, and slowly worked his way up the ranks
till he finally became the Yunnan grand coordinator in 1500.^([117]) He
tried to reform provincial finances, for example, streamlining the
management of the silver mines in 1504.^([118]) The court encouraged
him, granting him a dual position as both Grand Coordinator and
Assistant Minister of Revenue in Nanjing (南京戶部右侍郎).^([119]) When
he became supreme commander of Guangdong-Guangxi, in 1505, he again
received a dual appointment to Revenue.^([120])

Wu Tingju was Chen Jin’s most capable subordinate. Born in 1462, he was
both a highly reputed military commander and a capable poet.^([121]) By
all accounts, he had a distinct personality. The Mingshi says:

  He had a face like a peeled squash [i.e. flat and beardless]; he wore
  tattered clothes; he cared not for ornament. In his speech and
  behavior he had absolute confidence and nobody could outdo
  him.^([122])

Like Chen Jin, he specialized in finance. The court thus granted him a
dual position in two of Guangdong’s Three Offices: he was both
Administration Commissioner, which gave him power over the provincial
budget, and Surveillance Commissioner, which allowed him to lead armies
into battle (左布政使兼按察副使).^([123])

In 1509, Chen Jin and Wu Tingju proposed a controversial law.^([124]) As
mentioned, the supreme commanders had long taxed foreign merchants
without telling the Secretariat. But in that year, Jin decided to break
the silence. The most precise summary of his memorial comes from the
early Qing compilation, the Tianxia junguo libing shu
(天下郡國利病書)^([125]):

  The governing officials and the Censor Chen Jin propose: they wish to
  take one third of the goods of ships from Siam, Melaka, and Jilan as
  taxes. The Ministry of Revenue deems that the precious goods should be
  sent to the capital. Crude goods can be sold, and the money kept [in
  Guangdong] to supply army salaries.^([126])

This was a radical request. Chen Jin was effectively asking the court to
partially lift the Maritime Ban. He wanted foreign merchants to be
allowed to trade, though Chinese merchants would still be banned from
going abroad.

Surprisingly, the Secretariat approved Chen Jin’s request. (This
happened under particular circumstances, which will be explored in the
next chapter.) For the purposes of this chapter, it can be noted that
Yang Tinghe doubtless was not happy about the decision, but that in
1509, he was but a junior secretary and did not have the power to impose
his will. 

However, as soon as Ting he did have the opportunity, he moved to
abolish Chen Jin’s trade policy and reimpose the Maritime Ban. In 1512,
he became Senior Grand Secretary, and in 1513, Liu Chun became Minister
of Rites.^([127]) Tinghe and Chun were old friends and close allies,
whose hometowns were nearby: “Because Liu Chun is Yang Tinghe’s fellow
countryman, Tinghe has long personally supported him,” later observed
the Emperor.^([128]) Together, the two began to exert pressure on the
Guangdong administration. In 1514, Liu Chun recruited the help of a
mid-level official in Guangdong named Chen Boxian (陳伯獻). Boxian
complained that the situation on the Guangdong coastline had become
intolerable:

  The various trading goods found in Lingnan originate from the various
  [foreigners] of Melaka, Siam and Java. These products are but pepper,
  sappanwood, elephant tusks, tortoise-shell and such things. They are
  not things people need every day like cloth, silks, vegetables and
  grain. Recently, the administration has been permitted to levy
  proportional taxes [on ships’ cargoes] and there has been open trade.
  This has resulted in thousands of evil persons building huge ships,
  privately purchasing arms, engaging in evil activities upon the seas,
  illicitly linking up with the various [foreigners] and bringing great
  harm to the region. This should be stopped at once.^([129])

Boxian reprises the concerns of the Secretariat itself: i.e. that the
island trade system was corrupting morals and fostering crime. The hand
of Yang Tinghe and Liu Chun can be glimpsed in the subsequent handling
of his memorial:

  The memorial was sent to the Ministry of Rites which proposed: "The
  regional inspector and other officials should be instructed to
  strictly prohibit [foreign] ships from coming in violation of the
  frequency determined for tribute missions. Those ships in violation
  should be stopped and sent back, and officials should not be permitted
  to levy proportional taxes on them, as this will give rise to
  troubles. Any evil persons who continue to collude with the
  [foreigners] should be punished." This was approved.^([130])

That is to say, Liu Chun (the Ministry of Rites) took Chen Boxian’s
general complaints, and made a specific policy recommendation: to stop
the taxation of foreign ships, to restore the Maritime Ban, and to
reestablish tribute trade as the sole legal form of exchange. Yang
Tinghe, in the name of the Emperor, approved the recommendations.

By this time, both Chen Jin and Wu Tingju were no longer in Guangdong.
Both had been demoted a few years earlier by the Eunuch Liu Jin. It was
thus their successor, the Supreme Commander Zhou Nan (周南), who
received the Secretariat’s orders. But it seems that he simply
disregarded them and continued to tax foreign ships.^([131])

In 1515, Liu Chun tried again to stop foreign trade in Guangdong. This
time, he recruited the help of the regional Investigating Censor
(巡按檢查御史), Gao Gongshao (高公韶). Investigating Censors were junior
scholar officials who served as the eyes and ears of the court, checking
on provincial officials and denouncing corruption.^([132]) Gongshao
first criticized Supreme Commander Zhou Nan^([133]) for his lack of
military success.^([134]) Then he criticized him for his trade policy:

  Powerful local personages became increasingly avaricious and the ships
  were allowed to anchor. This has led to the various [foreigners]
  colluding with evil persons and engaging in outrage and plunder. This
  has brought calamities to the region. The Assistant Administration
  Commissioner Chen Boxian previously memorialized that this should be
  stopped. However, those persons who were supposed to carry out the
  orders have let things continue as before and the activities have not
  been stopped.^([135])

Again, Liu Chun referred Gao’s memorial to Yang Tinghe. And again,
Tinghe approved it and banned foreign commerce.^([136]) They were
determined to abolish the Guangdong island trade system.

But suddenly, Tinghe was forced to stop. In mid-1515, his father passed
away.^([137]) He thus had no choice but to depart from the court and
begin his obligatory mourning period. When his rival, Liang Chu, took
control of the Secretariat, the political winds shifted, and
the traditionalist factions suddenly felt a chill. On August 22, 1515,
Gao Gongshao was exiled to the border.^([138]) Liu Chun himself was
demoted to Nanjing shortly after.^([139]) At that point, all efforts to
restore the Maritime Ban ceased.

Meanwhile, the pro-trade factions returned to power. On October 25,
1515, Chen Jin was reappointed as supreme commander.^([140]) The next
year, in 1516, Wu Tingju was promoted to be the Guangdong senior
administration commissioner—chief of the Three Offices.^([141])
Together, the two tried to restore their earlier policies. The island
trade system had been officially outlawed since Chen Boxian’s memorial
in 1514. And so, in 1517, Jin and Tinghe made a proposition to the
court. The Tianxia junguo libing shu records:

  In 1517, Supreme Commander Chen Jin, along with Commissioner Wu
  Tingju, memorialized: they wish to imitate either the 20% tax of the
  Song Dynasty, or the 30% tax of recent days. Precious goods will be
  sent to the capital, and crude goods will be sold and the money used
  to supply army salaries.^([142])

That is to say, they wanted to legalize foreign trade again. Grand
Secretary Liang Chu, who was much less hostile to such policies than
Yang Tinghe, examined the request and approved it.^([143])

In his ancestral manor in Sichuan, Tinghe doubtless heard the news, but
he was powerless. He would not return from mourning until late 1517.

4. The Foreign Trade Eunuch and False Embassies


(1)


 

 



Introducing the Eunuchs

The eunuchs acted as servants of the emperor and the nobility.^([144])
What gave them a special status within the Ming government was that,
unlike the scholars, they did not have a clear recruitment system—there
was no formal examination or nomination process. They were dependent
upon the Emperor, who could hire or dismiss them as he wished.
Accordingly, while the scholars were admired as independent voices who
could help guide and correct their lord, the eunuchs were looked down on
as minions who could only bow to his will. This reputation, whether
merited or unmerited, brought them scorn from the scholars, but it also
brought them greater trust from the emperor himself.^([145])

Over time, the eunuchs developed a governance structure of their
own.^([146]) This did not exist in the beginning of the dynasty. Ever
jealous for power, the Hongwu Emperor distrusted eunuchs.^([147]) He
only wanted them to play the role of palace assistants, forbidding them
from learning to read or write, and from interfering in politics.
However, his son, the Yongle Emperor, saw eunuchs differently. He
cherished their loyalty and used them to check the power of the scholars
and military officials. He created eunuch offices that matched with, and
balanced, the traditional offices.^([148]) For example, the scholars had
the Censorate (都察院) to handle surveillance and inspection. As a
parallel agency, the Yongle Emperor created a eunuch spy force, the
notorious Eastern Depot (東廠).^([149]) Similarly, the military
superintendents controlled provincial armies. As a parallel, the Yongle
Emperor created a eunuch general, the Grand Defender (鎮守太監). There
were many other similar such offices.

At the top of the eunuch pyramid was the Directorate of Ceremonial
(司禮監).^([150]) He and the Senior Grand Secretary together acted as
the voice of the Emperor.^([151]) On the one side, the Grand Secretary
had the authority to write decrees on behalf of the Emperor (票擬), but
did not have the authority to officially sign them. On the other side,
the Directorate of Ceremonial had the authority to sign decrees with the
imperial red ink (批紅), but generally did not make policy proposals. In
theory, neither side could function without the other. This situation
created a eunuch-scholar rivalry at the very highest echelon of the
court.

For this book, the Foreign Trade Eunuch (市舶太監) is particularly
important.^([152]) This eunuch was created by the Yongle Emperor as a
parallel to the scholar-run Foreign Trade Bureau (市舶司). By the late
fifteenth century, it seems that the eunuch not only assisted the
bureau, but largely took over it: he welcomed visiting embassies, hosted
them, and handled their trade goods.^([153]) This complicated matters,
since the bureau was still under the Three Offices, de iure. An
ambiguous combination of collaboration and rivalry thus developed in the
handling of commerce as well.

The foreign trade eunuchs were powerful figures who not only oversaw
trade, but also challenged the regular administration for control of the
province. They accumulated considerable wealth. The most famous example
is that of Wei Juan (韋眷), who served as the Guangdong foreign trade
eunuch intermittently from 1475–1489.^([154]) His tomb was discovered by
archaeologists in 1964. Although it had already been looted, there were
many hints of its past riches: a massive, monumental tomb, containing
scattered coins from distant places such as Bengala and Venice.^([155])

The Three-Way Competition for Foreign Trade

The last chapter described a two-way competition for foreign trade,
between the Secretariat and the supreme commander. This chapter adds a
third participant: the foreign trade eunuch.

From the beginning, the eunuchs knew about the supreme commander’s
island trade system and wanted to wrest it from him. For example, in
1487, the aforementioned Wei Juan memorialized:

  In the city of Guangzhou, the county of Dongguan, there are towns such
  as Xihu (西湖), each having a pier. The people buy and sell fish and
  salt there. However, their profits are unlawfully taken by the proud
  officials. I beg that this be stopped and that the taxes be reduced in
  order to provide for the use of tribute to the court.^([156])

This memorial is probably a veiled attempt to take over the island trade
system. The reference to Dongguan is probably a reference to the
fortress of Nantou, the coastal town that had a branch of the Foreign
Trade Bureau.^([157]) That bureau handled taxes on both salt and on
exotic goods coming in from the islands.^([158]) Thus, when the eunuch
declared that he wished to take the “fish and salt taxes” away from the
scholars, this may have been a euphemism. In fact, he wanted the foreign
goods. In any case, this would explain why he equated the salt taxes
with his own tribute finances. But the court may have understood his
intentions, and refused his request.^([159])

This context helps explain one of the mysteries from the previous
chapter: why the Secretariat approved Supreme Commander Chen Jin’s
request to legalize the island trade system in 1509.^([160]) A year
earlier, in 1508, the powerful Directorate of Ceremonial, the Eunuch Liu
Jin, had dispatched special envoys to “inspect” Guangdong’s salt tax
revenue.^([161]) They claimed that Guangdong was not sending enough of
its funds to Beijing.^([162]) Again, this may have been a veiled
reference to foreign trade revenue. It was probably for this reason that
Chen Jin, under pressure, decided to openly consult the court about his
previously secret activities. He perhaps preferred to put himself at the
mercy of the Secretariat rather than of Liu Jin.

When Chen Jin sent his 1509 memorial to the court, a three-way debate
ensued. As described in the previous chapter, he asked to keep private
foreign trade in his own hands, in order to finance the
Guangdong-Guangxi armies. However, the Foreign Trade Eunuch Xiong Xuan
(熊宣) immediately objected:

  Xiong Xuan figured that he could intervene in the matter and obtain
  great benefits, and he thus memorialized a request to the
  Emperor.^([163])

The Court Records do not say precisely what Xuan’s request was, but
presumably it was for himself to handle the commerce, rather than Jin.

The Minister of Rites, who at this time was Bai Yue (白鉞), acted as a
judge between the two litigants. He was doubtless discontent with both
of them, for he would certainly have preferred to simply maintain the
Maritime Ban—to have no private foreign trade at all. The Court Records
thus only give a brief summary of his response: “The Ministry of Rites
memorialized its opposition to the proposal.”^([164]) The Records go on
to explain the final decision:

  It was imperially commanded that as Xuan had improperly grasped at
  authority, he be instructed to return to Nanjing to manage matters,
  and that the Eunuch Director Bi Zhen, of the Directorate of Palace
  Eunuchs, replace him.^([165])

That is to say, the Secretariat did not positively decree that Chen Jin
had the right to conduct private foreign trade. Rather, it negatively
decreed that the foreign trade eunuch did not have the right, and
punished him for even asking. Chen Jin was deemed to be the lesser of
two evils. (It should be remembered that this happened in 1509—three
years before Yang Tinghe assumed leadership as Senior Grand Secretary.)

But the foreign trade eunuch continued to request control of the island
trade. The next year, in 1510, after Chen Jin had been dismissed and Wu
Tingju thrown in the cangue, the Foreign Trade Eunuch Bi Zhen (畢真)
again petitioned the court^([166]):

  Under the old regulations, all the ocean-going ships were subject to
  management solely by the [Foreign Trade Bureau]. Recently, the grand
  defender and grand coordinator and officials of the Three Offices have
  also been permitted to jointly manage these ships. It is requested
  that the old regulations be adhered to so as to facilitate
  matters.^([167])

Bi Zhen is effectively asking for private trade to be managed uniquely
by the Foreign Trade Bureau, which he controlled. But by the time his
memorial arrived in Beijing, the political context had radically
changed: Liu Jin had been executed. The request was thus rejected once
more. The Ministry of Rites scolded Zhen:

  The function of the [Foreign Trade Bureau] is to manage local products
  brought in tribute. The ocean-going merchant ships and the [foreign]
  ships forced to anchor by the winds were not included in the gamut of
  the Imperial orders. The regulations should not be meddled
  with.^([168])

Again, the language of the Ministry is noteworthy. It did not positively
say that the Supreme Commander had the right to tax foreign ships.
Rather, it said that the Foreign Trade Bureau (and thus the Foreign
Trade Eunuch) only had the right to tax official tribute trade—not
private ships. It thus explicitly rejected the rights of the foreign
trade eunuch, while remaining mute on the question of the rights of the
supreme commander.

The eunuch would request one last time in 1515, but he was rejected
again.^([169])

The “False Embassy” Trade System

In addition to the Secretariat’s preferred tribute trade, and the
supreme commander’s preferred island trade, there was a third type of
foreign commerce in Guangdong. The foreign trade eunuch practiced what
this book will call “false embassy trade.” He only got revenue when
official tribute ships came into Guangzhou and were taxed by the Foreign
Trade Bureau. This meant that the more tribute ships came into
Guangzhou, the more money he could collect. The problem was that, as
mentioned in the previous chapter, by the late fifteenth century, there
were very few tribute ships. The vast majority of Southeast Asian ships
preferred island trade. In response to this situation, the eunuch thus
sponsored “fake embassies,” allowing merchant ships to dock at the Trade
Bureau under the guise of tribute ships.

The foreign trade eunuch who was most notorious for doing this was,
again, Wei Juan.^([170]) For example, the Ming Court Records say that in
1486, a fleet of ships arrived, claiming to be tribute ambassadors from
Sumendala (蘇門答剌, Sumatra).^([171]) The Administration Commissioner
was suspicious, noting that the so-called ambassadors lacked
identification documents. He thus proposed to send them away. But Wei
Juan then intervened: “The Eunuch allowed three of the foreigners,
including Jin Dang, to take their tribute goods and send them [to the
court].”^([172]) One of the major cosmographies of the later Ming
period, the Shuyu Zhouzilu (殊域周咨錄) gives more precise details about
this incident:

  The foreigner Ma Lima (馬力麻) was a maritime merchant. He falsely
  claimed to be an ambassador from Sumendala in order to privately
  trade. The Foreign Trade Eunuch Wei Juan made profits from his goods
  without investigating him. The Guangdong Administration Commissioner
  revealed his wicked crime.^([173])

There were many similar incidents during these years. For example, there
was another suspicious embassy in 1486, from Samarkand,^([174]) and
another embassy from Turfan in 1497.^([175]) And doubtless, there were
many foreign ships entering Guangzhou which were not detected by the
scholar officials and thus not noted in the sources.

Furthermore, Wei Juan not only helped foreign smugglers, but also
domestic ones. This practice can be surmised from the aforementioned
Samarkand incident, in 1486. The Shuyu Zhouzilu says:

  Citizens of Fanyu (番禺) county named Huang Lu (黃肆) and Wang Kai
  (王凱), father and son, recruited foreign merchants from Samarkand,
  and colluded with the Eunuch Wei Juan to go to sea and trade with
  foreigners. They relied on force to kill people and they shocked the
  region. They were [alerted] to the mayor Gao Yao (高瑤), who sent
  strong soldiers to confiscate thousands of foreign goods, and reported
  it to the Administration Commissioner Chen Xuan (陳選).^([176])

It seems that the false Samarkand embassy was in fact organized by local
Guangzhou merchants. Indeed, the Ming Court Records, which record the
same event, even suggest that the merchants were none other than agents
of Wei Juan himself, saying that he had “sent people to go abroad and
trade.”^([177]) While such accusations must be read with some caution,
the incident does suggest that Wei Juan was colluding with Chinese trade
groups in order to circumvent the Maritime Ban.

In summary, this chapter has introduced the foreign trade eunuch. He was
a powerful figure who undermined the Secretariat’s official tribute
order, challenged the supreme commander’s island trade system, and
sponsored many false embassies. He would later become one of the major
protectors of the Portuguese embassy.
