5. The Minister of War and Tribute Policy


(1)


 

 



The Political Rivalry Between the Secretariat and the Minister of War

Before introducing the Minister of War specifically, it is important to
introduce the Six Ministries in general. The Six Ministries were the
Ministries of Personnel (吏部), Rites (禮部), War (兵部), Revenue
(戶部), Works (工部), and Justice (刑部).^([178]) As their titles
indicate, each ministry had jurisdiction over a different sector of the
central government. They have often been compared to a modern
ministerial cabinet, whence the English translation of “尚書” as
“Minister.”

The status of the Six Ministries fluctuated over time. At the dawn of
the dynasty, after the Hongwu Emperor abolished the office of Chancellor
in 1380, the ministers became the most powerful scholar officials in the
empire.^([179]) But after that, their authority was slowly nibbled away
by other groups. In Beijing, the Secretariat arose and supplanted them
as the leaders of the scholars. In the provinces, the supreme commanders
seized power and began handling many duties without consulting them. By
the end of the fifteenth century, they were largely reduced to
coordinating agencies which connected the different parts of
government.^([180])

The Secretariat in particular overshadowed the Six Ministries.^([181])
As mentioned in Chapter 2, the secretaries had a cursus honorum. They
were first to work in Hanlin Academy for several years, and then they
were to work in one of the Six Ministries as minister (尚書) or vice
Minister (侍郎). They generally entered either Personnel, Revenue, or
Rites.^([182]) When they were promoted into the Secretariat, they
usually gave up their ministerial powers, but retained the honorary
title.^([183]) This had a negative effect upon the ministries, for they
were reduced to being a step in the cursus. Ministers became like
“junior secretaries,” waiting for their turn to enter the
Secretariat.^([184])

However, there was one ministry which the Secretariat could not absorb:
the Ministry of War. The reason was simple: in order to become a
secretary, one had to spend one’s career in Beijing.^([185]) But the
Minister of War was expected to be a seasoned field general who could
personally command the armies, if necessary. Thus most war ministers had
made their careers out in the border provinces. Whereas the secretaries
had achieved the highest scores on the imperial exams, the war ministers
usually had gotten lower scores—for which reason they had been sent out
to the provinces.^([186]) They thus had a hybrid culture: they were both
scholars versed in Confucian learning, and also soldiers versed in
battle tactics. And whereas the Ministers of Rites, Personnel, and
Revenue became subordinated to the Secretariat, the Minister of War
remained a balancing voice within the government.

Perhaps surprisingly, the Minister of War often used his power to avoid
conflict. By the late fifteenth century, the imperial budget was no
longer equal to the ballooning military expenses.^([187]) There were
simply too many conflicts, on too many fronts: perpetual threats in the
north from the Oirat Mongols, in the northeast from the various Manchu
tribes, in the northwest from the Turfan Sultanate (subject of this
chapter), and in the south from the numerous ethnic groups which
periodically revolted.^([188]) The Ming armies were not prepared to meet
these threats. In many regions, facilities and equipment were aging and
the regiments were understaffed.^([189]) The ministers of war recognized
and understood these problems, for they had struggled with them
themselves when they had served on the frontiers. They knew that they
had to be cautious in choosing which wars to wage.

One example of the ministry’s cautious attitude can be seen in 1487.
Annam had invaded Champa and seized some of its territory. Champa thus
sent an embassy to the Ming to ask for help to recover its lands. The
Ministry of War deliberated on the question and gave the following
advice:

  We should send a despatch to Annam explaining the righteousness of
  ensuring the continued existence [of] a ruling line. If they do not
  accord with the Emperor’s will, an envoy should be sent to escort
  Gu-lai (古來) [king of Champa] back to his country so that they can
  resolve the matter themselves.^([190])

Clearly, the Minister of War did not want to get involved in a foreign
conflict. While he was willing to offer moral support to Champa, he
ultimately wanted the Champanese to “resolve the matter themselves.” He
did not want to use the armies. He preferred to use diplomacy.

The Turfan–Hami Conflict and Tribute Policy

One foreign policy crisis in which the Ministry of War took leadership
was the Turfan–Hami Conflict. It is important to consider this war in
detail, because it became the model for the Portugal–Bintan case.

Today, Turfan (吐魯番) is in Xinjiang province, in northwestern
China.^([191]) But in the late fourteenth century, it was part of the
Moghulistan Khanate—one of the successor states to the Mongol Empire.
Turfan steadily became autonomous from the rest of Moghulistan, and by
1487, it had become its own Sultanate. It was a Muslim nation, which
grew prosperous as an oasis along the Silk Road.

Hami (哈密) is also part of Xinjiang province today.^([192]) In the Ming
era, it was a small city wedged between two larger powers: Turfan, about
four hundred kilometers to the east, and the Ming Empire, about six
hundred kilometers to the west. In 1405, the Yongle Emperor officially
recognized Hami as a vassal state and gave its ruler the title of “Loyal
and Obedient King (忠順王).”^([193]) He wanted Hami to be a buffer state
with Moghulistan. The Ming sources say that the city was governed by
three local ethnicities: the Uyghurs (畏兀兒), the Muslims (回回), and
the mysterious Qaraqoi (哈剌灰).^([194]) Because of their shared faith,
the Muslims were often suspected of collusion with Turfan.

In the late fifteenth century, the founder of the Turfan Sultanate,
Sultan Ahmad Alaq (阿黑麻), began a concerted effort to exert control
over Hami. In 1488, he invaded the city, killed its king, and seized the
Ming seal of vassalage.^([195]) But he only wanted a limited conflict,
not a full war. In fact, the Ming Empire was his most important trade
partner. Thus, after conquering Hami, he asked the court to send an
envoy to negotiate peace.^([196]) In Beijing, the officials were unsure
what to do. They were furious at the rebellious behavior of Turfan,
which was supposed to be a loyal tribute vassal. But they lacked the
military strength to punish it.^([197])

The Ministry of War asked the court to deliberate on the matter. After
much back-and-forth, it was decided to order Turfan to return Hami, and
simultaneously to invite Turfan’s neighbors to attack it, with the
promise that the Ming Empire would reward them.^([198]) The neighboring
city of Handong (罕東) accepted the invitation and invaded Turfan. But
to the dismay of the court, the tentative backfired. Handong was
defeated, and its refugees fled to the Ming borders. The empire had no
choice but to shelter and feed them.^([199]) The fragility of the
empire’s position was only highlighted.

In 1489, a new Minister of War was appointed: Ma Wensheng.^([200]) He
was a peace-maker who sought to harmonize Confucianism and pragmatism, a
friend of both the grand secretaries and the supreme commanders. As seen
in Chapter 2, he had initiated the plan to tutor the young prince Zhu
Houzhao. After contemplating the matter, he set up a careful policy. He
argued that the court should refuse Turfan’s tribute until it restored
Hami as an independent state.^([201]) For example, in 1490, he
memorialized:

  This crafty barbarian [i.e. Turfan] is publicly giving tribute, but
  privately hides other plans. I ask to order the Gansu officials to
  strictly prepare for it. Also, order the Ministry of Rites to tell
  their envoys: we will wait for [Sultan] Ahmad to repent and submit,
  and then they [i.e. the Turfan envoys] can be rewarded in accordance
  with their memorial.^([202])

By refusing tribute, the court could cut off the trade supplies that the
Sultanate so eagerly desired. This was a method that punished Turfan in
a tangible way without sending out armies. And it seemed to work, for in
1491 the Sultan yielded to the Ministry’s demands. He restored the city
walls of Hami and returned the Ming seal.^([203])

But things soon went awry. In 1493, the Sultan invaded Hami again. Ma
Wensheng sent his assistant minister, Zhang Hai (張海), to Gansu
province to examine the situation.^([204]) Together, they decided to
tighten the policy further: they would again cut off tribute relations
with Turfan, and this time they would also imprison its tribute
envoys.^([205]) The envoys would be used as hostages to pressure the
Sultan. After a few years, the policy seemed to work again, for in 1498,
Turfan announced that it would stop occupying Hami and restore its
king.^([206])

Ma Wensheng’s approach pleased all parties. On the one side, it made the
Secretariat happy because it maintained the dignity of the tribute
system and took a firm moral stand. On the other side, it also benefited
his own ministry, the Ministry of War, because it spared the army budget
and avoided a bloody border war. Ma Wensheng’s strategy would thus
become the standard way of handling foreign intervention crises for the
next forty years. In this book, it will be called “the tribute denial
policy.”^([207])

Introducing Wang Qiong

Ma Wensheng worked harmoniously with the Secretariat, but he was not a
typical Minister of War. In the Zhengde era, a much more divisive figure
became minister: Wang Qiong (王瓊). He would compete against Yang Tinghe
for power.

Wang Qiong was the same age was Tinghe, both born in 1459.^([208])
However, his career had followed a different path. He had climbed up the
professional ladder more slowly, only passing the imperial exam in 1484,
and not achieving a top score. He had been sent out to the provinces,
where he distinguished himself as a smart problem-solver. Within the
Ministry of Revenue, he helped to raise money for regions that were
struggling financially.^([209]) The Mingshi gives a vivid portrait of
him:

  When border generals asked for rations and fodder, he would count on
  his fingers: such-and-such granary or pasture can stock so much grain;
  such-and-such a county could send to the border so many autumn crops
  for the year. And then he said: “There’s enough! No need to keep
  searching.” And people thought that Wang Qiong was even more of a
  genius.^([210])

But Qiong’s enemies whispered that there was another reason for his
rapid promotions: corruption. He had befriended the Emperor’s minions
and catered to their wishes.^([211])

In 1515, Wang Qiong became Minister of War. A dominating figure, his de
facto authority often surpassed that of the Secretariat. Certainly, in
routine affairs, Yang Tinghe controlled the government. But whenever he
wished, Wang Qiong could talk to his friends in the palace and pull
strings to push through his own policies. Accordingly, the Ming Court
Records contain numerous editorial comments, later added by Yang Tinghe
and his allies, which complain about him.^([212]) For example, when Wang
Qiong distributed rewards for the capture of the Prince of Ning (寧王),
he included himself in the list of honorees. The Court Records bemoan
this:

  In every affair, in whatever location, [Wang Qiong] inevitably gave
  himself credit. Whenever memorials came about military victories on
  the frontiers, there was never a case where credit was not given to
  the Minister of War himself.^([213])

Similarly, when Wang Qiong harshly punished the Grand Coordinator of
Yunnan, the Court Records add a word of criticism: “Wang Qiong’s cruelty
and authoritarianism certainly were frightful.”^([214])

In Chapter 3, we saw that Yang Tinghe and the Minister of Rites Liu Chun
sponsored a campaign to stop the Guangdong island trade policy. At the
same time, they also moved against Wang Qiong as well. In 1515, their
ally, the Investigating Censor Gao Gongshao, harshly criticized him:
“Wang Qiong is wicked and perverse, and daringly pushes his own
opinions,” he declared. Furthermore, he mocked Qiong as an inept
strategist:

  Since he has received the position, he has appointed army generals,
  but many of the people are not good; he has deliberated on border
  situations, but many of his policies are failures.^([215])

In protest, Wang Qiong angrily offered his resignation. But the Emperor
responded by upholding Qiong and demoting Gongshao.^([216]) Usually,
Yang Tinghe would not have given up at that. He would doubtless have
liked to continue pressuring his rival, but it was at this time that his
father died and he had no choice but to retire from court.

The struggle between Wang Qiong and Yang Tinghe, and more broadly,
between the Ministry of War and the Ministry of Rites, would shape Ming
foreign policy during the later Zhengde era. The vicissitudes of this
rivalry would make for both the early success of the Portuguese embassy
and for its ultimate failure.

6. The Emperor’s Personal Foreign Affairs


(1)


 

 



The Struggle Between the Secretariat and the Emperor

The Emperors sometimes strayed from their high calling. Although they
knew the laws and traditions, they often disregarded them. For example,
the Maritime Ban said that it was illegal to purchase foreign products.
But the Chenghua Emperor loved exotic goods, and sent his eunuchs to
procure them.^([217]) He did not repeal the Maritime Ban on a national
level, but he violated it in his own private life. Similarly, the
Hongzhi Emperor was enthusiastic about religion, and he invited Buddhist
priests and Muslim clerics to live in the Forbidden Palace as his
guests.^([218]) Technically, this also infringed on the Maritime Ban.
But the Emperor did not change the policy, he simply suspended it for
his own purposes. The Persian traveler, Ali Akbar, wrote:

  According to Chinese law, if a foreigner comes and already has proof
  that the Emperor has given permission, then he can stay here
  permanently.^([219])

The grand secretaries were concerned about this situation. They had a
moral duty to guide the emperor, and to correct him. Their
responsibilities were akin to those of children toward their father, as
described in the Classic of Filial Piety (孝經):

  The father who had a son that would remonstrate with him would not
  sink into the gulf of unrighteous deeds. Therefore when a case of
  unrighteous conduct is concerned, a son must by no means keep from
  remonstrating with his father, nor a minister from remonstrating with
  his ruler.^([220])

Presumably, nobody was more qualified to remonstrate than the
secretaries. As mentioned in Chapter 2, the other figures who surrounded
the emperor, such as the nobility and the eunuchs, were less trained in
moral philosophy. The secretaries had the most thorough knowledge of the
classics, the greatest repository of historical exemplars, the deepest
understanding of Confucianism.

When faced with reproach from his subjects, an emperor could react in
various ways. The ideal was that he agree with their objections, and
follow their suggestions. For example, the Hongzhi Emperor was
celebrated for his mild temper. On August 12, 1499, the Minister of
Rites warned him of a comet that had appeared in the sky.^([221]) He
took this to be a sign that the Emperor’s behavior needed to change:
namely, that the Son of Heaven needed to spend less money and be more
frugal. The Emperor responded in a way that pleased all of the court
officials:

  The heavens are giving a warning. I respect and fear it deeply. May
  all of you officials, both civil and military, may each of you
  earnestly increase frugality and fully execute your duties, in order
  to recover heaven’s favor!^([222])

However, while the Hongzhi Emperor generally listened patiently to his
officials, he did not always do what they asked.^([223]) Like his
father, the Chenghua Emperor, he often brushed aside their words. The
next year, in 1500, all Six Ministers presented a common memorial to
him. Again invoking signs in the heavens, they presented a list of
eighteen ways that the Emperor could improve himself. For example, they
asked him to wake up earlier in order to attend the morning court
session and to stop frequenting Buddhist priests.^([224]) The Emperor
again listened patiently, without anger. He even recognized the truth of
their accusations, saying, “my officials, what you are saying sharply
pinpoints the faults of the time.”^([225]) But his enthusiasm had
already cooled considerably.

Nonetheless, once emboldened, the officials could not stop offering
suggestions. Later that same year, still another official presented
another list of six ways that the Emperor should reform his behavior. He
again asked him to wake up earlier and also advised him stop ordering so
much incense for ceremonies. At that point, the Son of Heaven only made
a cryptic response: “Order his memorial to be handed to the appropriate
office.”^([226]) That is to say, he officially considered the advice not
to pertain to himself, but rather to his subordinates. He deflected the
remonstrance.

As the years went on, the Hongzhi Emperor seems to have become
increasingly taciturn and unresponsive. The words, “order his memorial
to be handed to the appropriate office” proliferate in the later court
records.

At the harshest level, sometimes the emperor not only slighted
protesting officials, but also punished them. Of course, he could always
fine, dismiss, cane, or imprison people who had committed infractions.
But in the case of remonstrances, it was more complicated. How could he
strike down servants who were just trying to fulfill their filial
duties? If the Son of Heaven were to throw them in the cangue, it would
provoke scandal.

Accordingly, emperors had special institutions to control problematic
officers. The most notorious was the Eastern Depot (東廠).^([227]) It
was a policing force which maintained internal security, spying on
anybody suspected of undermining the emperor’s power. Because it
operated secretly, it is difficult to track its activities in the
sources. But clearly it did sometimes eliminate remonstrators. For
example, Chapter 4 mentioned the powerful Foreign Trade Eunuch Wei Juan,
and his struggle against the Guangdong scholar officials. His most
intractable opponent was the Administration Commissioner Chen
Xuan (陳選).^([228]) Chen Xuan was eventually denounced for
corruption—an apparently false accusation. On the road to his trial in
Beijing, he suddenly died under unexplained circumstances.^([229]) It is
likely that the assassination was carried out by the Eastern Depot, for
no investigation was ever made into the causes of his death, nor was any
guilty party ever named. Two years later, after the Chenghua Emperor
passed away, one scholar asked for reforms:

  In recent years, the Eastern Depot was established to secretly
  investigate the faults of officials, and thus to punish and dismiss
  them. But in deciding between grace and vengeance, it has been the
  downfall of many innocent people!^([230])

The Zhengde Emperor and Yang Tinghe

The Zhengde Emperor had a complex relationship with the Secretariat.
When he first took the throne, he inherited the secretaries from his
father’s reign. He disliked them and distrusted them, giving power
instead to his old caretaker from the Spring Palace, the eunuch Liu Jin.
As mentioned in Chapter 2, the eunuch then proceeded to drive almost all
of the old secretaries into retirement. After Jin was put to death, a
new generation arrived. This group of secretaries, led by Yang Tinghe,
had a very different relationship with the Emperor. They had all
previously been his tutors in the Spring Palace. They knew him
personally, and it seems that he sincerely liked them. In any case, he
never physically punished any of them, and never forced them to
retire.^([231]) Indeed, unlike his grandfather, the Chenghua Emperor, or
his father, the Hongzhi Emperor, it seems that the gregarious Zhengde
saw his secretaries fairly often in person, albeit only at unofficial
events—banquets, theater performances, and other courtly pastimes.

Even so, while the Zhengde Emperor did like his secretaries, he did not
necessarily listen to them. In the Court Records, his typical response
to their memorials is: “no response (不報).”^([232]) Furthermore, early
on in his reign, he explicitly ordered his officials not to remonstrate
with him. Accordingly, the scholars frequently requested that he “open
communication channels”—that is to say, to allow them to speak
freely.^([233]) For example, in 1505, one official pleaded:

  Recently, in cases of officials who speak up (言官), they are given
  stern warnings and forced to retract their words. Among such
  officials, most of them cherish their lives and few of them wish to be
  martyrs. They timidly see the trend and keep silent, abandoning their
  duty. How can this benefit the fatherland?^([234])

The Zhengde Emperor received such requests, but, as usual, did not
respond. In the post-Liu Jin era, while the secretaries were not
punished or imprisoned, they nonetheless were silenced and ignored.

The Private World of the Zhengde Emperor

The Zhengde Emperor had “favorites (佞臣).” These were courtiers who
catered to him and entertained him. In particular, there was a man named
Jiang Bin (江彬).^([235]) He was a relatively educated man, who had
passed the military exam and become a mid-level army officer along the
northern border.^([236]) The Mingshi says that Jiang Bin was “crafty and
cruel. In his appearance, he was commanding and forceful.”^([237]) Over
and over, he convinced the Emperor to give him promotions. By 1518, he
had become the director of both the Imperial Guard (錦衣衛) and of the
Eastern Depot.^([238]) The Ming Court Records complain: “all authority,
both central and provincial, went to Jiang Bin.”^([239])

The Zhengde Emperor always longed to create his own private world. When
he was 16, in 1508, he began to build his own personal palace within the
Forbidden City: this was the Leopard House (豹房), a former imperial
menagerie in one corner of the gardens.^([240]) There, he gathered many
friends: dancers, musicians, acrobats, horsemen, and foreign priests.
When he was 20, in 1512, he began venturing out of the Forbidden City,
into the bustling streets of Beijing. He sometimes did not return for
several days.^([241]) After the Emperor met Jiang Bin in 1513, his
dreams grew larger.^([242])  Bin was from Xuanfu (宣府), near the
northern border of the empire. The Zhengde Emperor had always wanted to
go to the frontier and fight the Mongols like his ancestor, the Yongle
Emperor. And so, in 1516, when he was 24 years old, he began to build
another palace at Xuanfu.^([243]) In 1517, he escaped from the Forbidden
City and fled north to his new residence. During his long stay on the
frontier, he had Jiang Bin gather pleasures for him. The Ming Court
Records say:

  At night, [Bin] went out to visit the homes of high officials and big
  notables. As soon as he entered, he searched for their wives and
  daughters.^([244])

A Qing-era chronicle detailing the Emperor’s scandalous behavior, the
Wuzong Waiji (武宗外紀), claimed that Jiang Bin also ran about gathering
personal items: “he looked for hunting dogs and hawks, rare treasures,
and ancient vases.”^([245]) In Beijing, in 1518, the Joseon embassy was
surprised to find that the Emperor was absent. The ambassador reported
back to the king in Seoul about the rumors from Xuanfu:

  The emperor goes hunting in the wild, or he goes out in the streets
  incognito. In his behavior, he does not separate himself from
  commoners.^([246])

For this book, it is particularly important to note that the Zhengde
Emperor enjoyed the company of foreigners. Like his father, the Hongzhi
Emperor, the Zhengde Emperor was fascinated with Tibetan
Buddhism.^([247]) When priests arrived from Tibet or Sichuan, he invited
them to stay in Beijing and serve in the Leopard House.^([248]) Indeed,
he built a temple on the grounds there.^([249]) A commentary in the
Court Records offers a portrait of his religious life:

  The Emperor chanted and studied foreign scriptures. He worshipfully
  esteemed their teachings. He often wore clothing like a foreign priest
  and recited the sutras.^([250])

Rumors said that the Emperor even took on a new title, akin to that of a
Lama: “The Buddhist King of Great Blessings (大慶法王).”^([251])

The Zhengde Emperor was also devoted to Islam.^([252]) The most
important witness to this is the Persian traveler Ali Akbar, who came to
the Hongzhi court around 1500.^([253]) He wrote that the “Prince”
(presumably the young Zhengde Emperor) once had a vision:

  One day, in a dream, he saw the Prophet Muhammad. He accepted the
  Muslim faith. After awakening, he saw a confirmation engraved in green
  letters on the wall.^([254])

The envoys from Joseon observed the Emperor’s behavior firsthand. They
stayed at the Beijing guesthouse in 1519, alongside the Turfan and Hami
envoys. When they returned to Seoul, they reported on the striking
things that they had seen:

  The emperor goes out to play in the “action building” (行院, i.e.
  brothel), and participates in playboy (養漢) games without moderation.
  An “action building” is a place where playboys gather. The emperor
  also often visits the foreign guesthouse (會同館), and plays with the
  Tartar and Muslim chieftains. The emperor takes the Muslim utensils
  and dishes and tries them himself. He also wears their foreign clothes
  in order to study their customs.^([255])

The Zhengde Emperor commissioned ceramics engraved in Arabic, which seem
to reveal his Muslim name: Suleiman.^([256])

To summarize, in his treatment of both Tibetan priests and Central Asian
imams, the Zhengde Emperor displayed certain salient tendencies. He
wished to learn their language and customs. He tried on their clothing
and ate their food. It is important to note this, because in Chapter 9,
we will see him treating the Portuguese in much the same way.

It goes without saying that Yang Tinghe did not approve of the Emperor’s
behavior. For example, the Grand Secretary’s views of Buddhism can be
perceived in a memorial from 1522:

  If by chance it did bring benefit to the nation, and did not bring
  harm to the people, then keeping [the Buddhist temple] would be fine.
  Obeying your wishes would be fine. However in this case, it is but a
  form of heresy and perverse talk: it defiles the classics and confuses
  the rules. […] From the times of the ancient kings, it was punishable
  by death and not permitted.^([257])

As a loyal subject, Yang Tinghe tried to warn the Emperor many times.
For example, in 1514, when one of the imperial palaces burned down, the
Emperor asked his officials if it was an omen.^([258]) This, at last,
opened up the communication channels, and gave them a chance to
remonstrate with him. The next day, an investigating censor immediately
spoke up:

  You distance yourself from the Confucians and approach yourself to the
  foreign priests. You abandon the scholars and favor the border guards
  [i.e. Jiang Bin]. You neglect the court and open liquor bars!^([259])

Not entirely sure how to react, the Zhengde Emperor gave a response in
the style of his father: he referred the memorial to lower offices to be
acknowledged.^([260])

The next day, another censor spoke up: “captive Mongols should not live
in the Forbidden City. Foreign priests should not come and go from the
palace.”^([261]) The Emperor tried to display patience, again referring
the matter to lower offices. But when still another official tried to
remonstrate with him that same day, the Emperor clearly had enough. He
retreated back to his habitual “no response.”^([262]) All in all, about
a dozen officials offered their wisdom to the Emperor, though for the
most part, he probably did not read their memorials.

This context is important to note, for the Portuguese embassy would
later join the Emperor’s entourage, having been recruited by Jiang Bin.

7. Portuguese Trade in Guangdong 1512–1521


(1)


 

 



This chapter marks a transition point. The first five chapters of the
book focused on the Ming government, and the competition between the
Secretariat and various rival groups. Now the book turns toward the
Portuguese and examines how they fit into this situation. This chapter
builds specifically on Chapter 3, which discussed the Guangdong-Guangxi
supreme commanders and the island trade system.

The Portuguese as Southern Sea Merchants

The Portuguese were able to smoothly integrate into the island trade
system because their ships were nearly indistinguishable from those of
Southeast Asian merchants.^([263]) It must be remembered that the
departure port for all Portuguese ships bound for China was the port of
Melaka. In the fifteenth century, Melaka had been a great merchant
empire, with a population of over a hundred thousand.^([264]) After
Afonso de Albuquerque conquered the city, he only left a small
contingent of European troops: around two to three hundred people at any
given time.^([265]) That is to say, the Portuguese occupiers made up
less than 1% of the population. Furthermore, they lacked the knowledge
to control the Melakan commercial network: they neither spoke the local
languages, nor knew the local goods, nor understood the local trade
systems.

For those reasons, the Portuguese tended to follow the Melakan
merchants, rather than lead them. In his letter of 1518, the Captain of
Melaka, Afonso Lopes da Costa, described how sailors were placed aboard
local ships:

  Those junks, which are necessary to equip each year, are about fifteen
  or twenty for the regions that Melaka cannot do without. And each one
  of these junks has need of ten men, and this of necessity, for matters
  including navigation and trade for Melaka, and for trading your
  goods…^([266])

In other words, most of the ships that sailed out of Melaka were Malay,
or Siamese, or Javanese, or Luzonese, but they also carried about ten
Portuguese sailors. The great historian Luis FR Thomaz noted that “these
voyages were totally organized in the Malay manner […]. Everything was
regulated by the maritime customs of Melaka and by the Malay system of
taxation.”^([267])

This practice applied to the China trade as well. For example, in
1512, João Viegas tried to sail to the Middle Kingdom aboard the ship of
a “moor”—probably a Muslim Malay trader.^([268]) However, the tentative
never materialized. The first known successful trip to China was in
1513, when the famous Jorge Alvares went with a Chinese merchant called
“the Cheilata”.^([269]) In 1515, Rafael Perestrello also sailed there
within the fleet of the Cheilata.^([270]) Even Fernão Peres de Andrade’s
embassy of 1517 had a strong local contingent. In his letter reporting
on the voyage, he described the fleet's composition^([271]):

  Also going with him was Jorge Botelho in the junk of a merchant from
  Melaka called Curiaraja, and Manuel de Araújo in another [junk] of the
  Cheilata, and in another of his [i.e. the Cheilata’s] junks, Antonio
  Lobo Falcão.^([272])

In addition, there was the junk of Captain Duarte Coelho, which
rendez-vous’ed with Fernão Peres in China. Although Coelho was the
nominal captain, it seems that the ship actually belonged to locals.
Simão de Andrade saw it in Melaka in 1518, and described it as “a junk
that came from China […] in which there went some Portuguese
people.”^([273])

Because of this, while the Guangdong officials did view the incoming
Portuguese as a new group, they did not view them as completely
unfamiliar. For example, the Ming official Gu Yingxiang (顧應祥), who
came aboard Fernão Peres de Andrade’s ship in 1517, shared his
impressions of the Europeans^([274]):

  They all have high noses and deep eyes. Their bodies are shaped like
  those of Muslims (回回). They wear decorative robes that drape down
  and spread out. Their pants are made of hides. […] Their leader often
  looked at a book. I took it and examined it. It was a Buddhist
  Sutra.^([275])

Yingxiang thinks that the Portuguese are quite similar to other southern
groups: in his eyes, their clothes are like those of the Arab merchants
and their religion is like that of the Siamese Buddhists.^([276]) In
other words, while he does think that they are strange, he does not
think that they are that much stranger than other foreigners.

Similarly, the Portuguese trading practices were roughly similar to
those of other ships from the south. Gu Yingxiang listed the goods that
they were selling:

  Objects like coral, precious wood oils, colorful robes, golden
  helmets, and glasswork. In addition, there was a kind of reddish-brown
  [cloth], called “scarlet (撒哈剌)” There was one three-edged sword,
  and a sword whose blade is bent back.^([277])

The list mainly mentions objects from the Indian Sea region: precious
woods (perhaps from the Malay peninsula), coral (perhaps from the Red
Sea), colorful robes (perhaps the famous Gujarati textiles), glasswork
(perhaps from the Ottoman Empire), a curved sword (perhaps a shamshir).
There do seem to be a few European items mixed in—most notably, Venetian
scarlet—but they are in the minority. Andrade’s cargo was probably not
much different from that of other ships trading in Guangdong.

In regard to the products that the Portuguese were selling in bulk, out
on the islands, it seems that they followed local practice as
well—focusing on pepper. Even before coming to China, Tomé Pires had
heard that this was the product that the Chinese most wanted from
Southeast Asia:

  The chief merchandise is pepper—of which they will buy ten junk-loads
  a year if as many [junks] go there—cloves, a little nutmeg […]. Pepper
  apart, they make little account of all the rest.^([278])

Simão de Andrade confirmed this information. After returning from China
in 1520, he advised his successor as captain, Martim Afonso de Melo, to
focus on spices, reporting that “the Siamese who go there every year
bring some pepper.”^([279])

The Portuguese were also acting much like local sailors in that they
were often trading for basic, practical goods. Indeed, some sources seem
more excited about Chinese construction materials than about luxury
items. For example, in 1527, Captain Diogo Calvo wrote:

  In China, you can get them very cheap: namely, sticks, masts, pitch,
  planks, iron nails, lead, iron, copper, mercury, and other merchandise
  which is all very rich.^([280])

In 1521, the director of Melaka’s warehouse (almoxarife do almazem),
Tristão Vieira, wrote up an inventory. He listed various Chinese
products that lay in stock: work tools such as saws and files, ship
parts such as pinewood masts, oak planks, and pitch to cover the ship.
In addition, he had six large iron cooking pots from China—perhaps the
earliest mention of the wok in Western literature.^([281])

Finally, it is also worth noting that the Portuguese not only blended
with the Southeast Asian merchant communities, but also with the local
Guangdong coastal communities. In the Pearl River delta, out on their
designated trade island of Tamão, there was in fact a small settlement.
One later text mentions that there were Chinese “merchants and
craftsmen” at Tamão.^([282]) These were probably local boatmen who
temporarily came over to live with the foreign merchants during each
monsoon season.^([283]) When the Portuguese were driven away in 1521,
the court not only ordered the Guangdong navy to destroy their fortress,
but also to get rid of the “population” there.^([284]) So the trade
island was probably a small port town, hosting very diverse peoples.

To summarize, the Portuguese did not come to China as completely exotic
foreigners. Rather, they flowed into the Guangdong trade, looking much
like typical southern sea merchants.

The Portuguese Crimes in Guangdong

The Portuguese not only integrated into the Guangdong trade networks,
but also into the crime networks. In 1397, over a century before the
embassy, the Ming Law Code (大明律) already mentioned the two major
items on the coastal black market: “Those who leave the border carrying
people or military weapons, and cross the sea: they are to be strangled
to death.”^([285]) In this section, let us examine these two
problems—human trafficking and arms trafficking—and consider how the
Portuguese participated in them.

The slave trade had long been a problem in Guangdong. Anthony Reed has
noted that Southeast Asia had a labor shortage during this
period.^([286]) In contrast, Ming China was suffering from
overpopulation, and hunger was causing mass emigration from the rural
villages.^([287]) These two trends combined resulted in endemic human
trafficking. For example, the Ming Court Records mention a scandal in
1481:

  When the envoys from the two countries of Siam and Sumendala were
  returning home after coming to the court to offer tribute, along the
  way, their boat-men showed them how to purchase the sons and daughters
  of impoverished people.^([288])

The Portuguese quickly linked into these networks and took advantage of
them.^([289]) For example, João de Barros, following the Captain of
Melaka’s 1521 report,^([290]) criticized Simão de Andrade, for his
relentless slave purchasing:

  There were found many boys and girls from Guangdong, children of
  honorable foks, whom Simão de Andrade and his armada had purchased
  without realizing that they were offending the city by doing so. For
  they knew that generally in all those Oriental regions, the fathers
  and others were accustomed to sell their children […] and it seemed to
  them that those [children] who had been sold to them were of that
  sort, and not kidnapped by bandits.^([291])

Andrade and his crews had the role of buyers. Out on the trade islands,
they waited for kidnappers to come, and they purchased their victims
without asking questions.

Another source gives a somewhat different portrait of the people whom
Simão de Andrade brought back from China. In a 1522 letter written to
King Dom Manuel, João de Caminha complained about Andrade, describing an
intense interpersonal conflict. He wrote that Andrade “had fifteen
Chinese musketeers.”^([292]) And he claimed that he sent those
musketeers against him:

  [Andrade] ordered his servants and the Chinese to attack my own
  people. And in the middle of the day, all of his people attacked one
  of mine, and they struck him eighteen times with knives.^([293])

Caminha’s testimony thus suggests that Andrade was not only purchasing
innocent children from the Guangdong bandits—he was also hiring the
bandits themselves!

Weapons trafficking was another chronic problem in Guangdong. Long
before the Portuguese brought cannons to China, the Chinese themselves
had already developed their own firearms. Nakajima Gakushō has noted
that as early as the thirteenth century, the Yuan Empire had already
begun diffusing gunpowder weapons in Southeast Asia.^([294]) Laichen Sun
has examined shipwreck evidence showing that such trends accelerated
during the Ming dynasty.^([295]) Subsequently, Southeast Asian countries
began to develop their own guns, which they, in turn, began to
export.^([296])

The Portuguese thus entered into a preexisting market. It seems that
they competed against Javanese arms smugglers for clients. Lin Wenfeng
(李文鳳), who served as an officer in Guangdong in the 1530 s, hinted at
this situation:

  The Portuguese cannon are big. The Javanese cannon are small. The
  [Portuguese] countrymen can use it with great accuracy. With a small
  one they can hit a sparrow.^([297])

In the passage, one can hear the voice of Chinese consumers weighing the
strengths and weaknesses of the various products on the market.

How could such a dangerous commerce be handled in secret? A story by the
Judicial Commissioner Wang Hong (王鋐), who would later defeat the
Portuguese ships at the Battle of Tamão, gives hints. He proudly
described how he procured Portuguese cannon technology for the Emperor:

  [Among the Portuguese], there were some Chinese men: Yang San (楊三),
  Dai Ming (﻿戴明), and Shen Zhiyi (﻿審知伊). For many years they had gone
  to stay in the Portuguese country [i.e. Melaka]. They had learned the
  method for making ships, iron cannons, and gunpowder. I immediately
  ordered [Officer] He Ru (何儒) to secretly send someone over to them,
  under the pretense of selling food, in order to secretly hold talks
  with Yang San. I notified him that if he returned his loyalty [to the
  Ming], that he would be richly rewarded. He then joyfully consented to
  an agreement. That night, He Ru secretly sailed a ship to take him
  back.^([298])

At first glance, this would seem to be a patriotic piece of writing.
Wang Hong is describing his valiant efforts to procure cannonry for his
country. But between the lines, one can perceive techniques for
circumventing the Maritime Ban: bandits could sail to the islands
disguised as food merchants, conduct secret negotiations with Chinese
expatriates working among the Portuguese, and then quietly procure their
illegal goods on a secluded shore at night.

To summarize, in both human trafficking and arms trafficking, the
Portuguese joined preexisting crime networks. They did not act alone. On
the contrary, they were highly dependent upon both their Southeast Asian
crews and upon the local Chinese smugglers.

Admittedly, some of the Portuguese crimes were uniquely European in
nature. But even these cases indirectly confirm their dependence on
local agents. For example, they tried to build a fortress on the island
of Tamão.^([299]) In his book, The Gunpowder Age, Tonio Andrade has
shown that this was a distinctly European strategy to seize and hold
territory.^([300]) However, it is significant that the Portuguese were
never able to complete the fortress. The problem was that the idea of
“holding territory” did not fit with local practices. Their ship crews
were generally Malay, and the Malay polities of this time prioritized
mobility over fixed occupation, preferring light, disposable buildings,
and yielding territory whenever necessary.^([301]) In the same way, the
Chinese fishermen tended to only make temporary housing, shifting from
island to island as needed.^([302])

Consequently, when Simão de Andrade met with Martim Afonso de Melo in
1522, Andrade complained that he had been forced to abandon the
fortress. He said that the location was not good: “It is very bad for
one’s health, because even for the moors themselves the land is
harmful!”^([303]) In other words, the Malay crew members (the “moors”)
apparently refused to stay at the unhealthy fortress. They pushed
Andrade to search for a new location. He wrote:

  On the same island, closer to Guangzhou by three or four leagues,
  beyond another point, there is another port called “the Siamese port.”
  It’s as good as the other, and as good a safe haven. And since the
  inland area that he found was filled with pines and good water, and
  given that it’s closer to Guangzhou, there are more advantages. All
  the better that it’s healthier!^([304])

That is to say, Andrade ultimately adopted a much more mobile approach
toward choosing a settlement location, abandoning an unhealthy place
rather than adamantly defending it. This fit better with his own
workers.

The lesson of the failed fortress would seem to be that Portuguese were
dependent upon their crews and upon the local artisans. When these
laborers decided to abandon a defensive position, the European officers
had no choice but to follow them.

The Portuguese in the Island Trade System

Well before Fernão Peres de Andrade arrived in China, the Portuguese had
already integrated into the Guangdong island trade system. The Suma
Oriental of Tomé Pires contains the following description:

  Thirty leagues on this side of Canton, towards Melaka, there are some
  islands near the mainland of Nantou, where are the ports already
  allotted to each nation, viz. Pulo Tumon and others. And as soon as
  the said junks anchor there, the lord of Nantou sends word to Canton
  and merchants immediately come to value the merchandise and to take
  their dues, as will be told later. Then they bring them the
  merchandise made up from one part and another. Each one returns to his
  home.^([305])

This passage can be compared to a description of the island trade system
found in the Guangdong Tongzhi (廣東通志):

  The Foreign Trade Bureau was established to manage [trade]. […] As to
  those foreign merchants who privately offer merchandise and come in
  order to trade it, when their ships arrive, then their documents
  should be thoroughly examined. 20% of their goods are levied, and then
  they are allowed to trade.^([306])

Assuming that “the lord of Nantou” is the Foreign Trade Bureau, the two
descriptions basically match: first the bureau sends agents to tax the
merchants, and then the merchants are allowed to trade freely. What is
particularly striking is the fact that Tomé Pires wrote his description
of this system around 1515. At that point, he had not yet gone to China.
In fact, only one Portuguese captain had gone and come back: Jorge
Alvares, who made a voyage in 1513–1514. That suggests that Alvares
himself had already integrated into the Guangdong system. But if he was
the first Portuguese man to ever go to China, then how was that
possible?

There is a probable explanation. The foreign merchant community of
Melaka must have already negotiated this arrangement in 1512—a year
before Alvares arrived. After Afonso de Albuquerque conquered Melaka in
1511, the community was divided.^([307]) On the one side, the Muslim
merchants of Gujarat and the local Malay elites largely rejected the
Portuguese invaders, and followed the Sultan of Melaka when he fled to
Bintan (where he founded the short-lived Bintan Sultanate). On the other
side, the merchants of Choromandel, China, Luzon, and Java accepted the
Europeans and continued to stay in Melaka. This division created a
problem. As mentioned above, the Foreign Trade Bureau allotted one port
to each nation. Presumably, the next year, all of the aforementioned
merchant groups, both pro-Portuguese and anti-Portuguese, must have
arrived at the same single “Melakan” port. In order to prevent conflict
among them, the Ming officials must have re-assigned locations, sending
the Bintan-allied merchants to one island, and the Portuguese-allied
merchants to another. Thus, the following year, in 1513, Jorge Alvares
already was able to sail into the designated “Portuguese Melakan”
port—the aforementioned Tamão.

This hypothesis has an important implication, one whose consequences
will echo throughout this book: namely, that the Portuguese must have
been accepted into the island trade system as “Melakan ships.” That is
to say, they were not considered to be an unknown foreign country, but
rather a well-established vassal state of the Ming. In the following
chapters, this confusion between Portuguese Melaka and the Melakan
Sultanate will become problematic for Tomé Pires’ embassy.

In any case, during the four years of normal Sino-Portuguese relations
(1517–1521), island trade was the main type of commerce. Although the
sources only give vague indications, it is clear that private shipping
predominated. The best estimate comes from 1521. At that moment, there
were roughly thirteen Portuguese vessels at Tamão:

1.  o

    One was a royal merchant ship from Lisbon,^([308])

     

2.  o

    Three were private merchant ships from Melaka,^([309])

     

3.  o

    Four were government-sponsored merchant ships from the fortress of
    Melaka,^([310])

     

4.  o

    Roughly five were private ships from Pattani and Siam.^([311])

     

These numbers, which are incomplete, suggest that about two-thirds of
the ships were private merchants. Nearly half were probably solteiros,
completely free merchants, based in Siam and other Southeast Asian
ports. Only one of the ships could be considered an official “tribute
ship” that could participate in the tribute trade system: the royal ship
from Lisbon, under the command of Diogo Calvo.^([312]) All of the rest
were under island trade.

The Perspective of Yang Tinghe

When Yang Tinghe read the reports on Portuguese trade and criminality in
Guangdong, he was disgusted, but not surprised. Indeed, the lost letter
of Cristóvão Vieira says that the Secretariat wished to pass over their
actions along the coastline:

  In regards to the other things that were said about us: it sufficed to
  say that we are a foreign people who do not know the customs of the
  land; that people of that sort, since they do things out of ignorance,
  should not be punished, but only warned about what they should
  do.^([313])

The expression “people of that sort” should be particularly noted. Yang
Tinghe did not have high expectations of the Europeans. They were not
mortal foes to be feared, but rather brutish foreigners to be pitied.

More than the Portuguese, Yang Tinghe blamed the Supreme Commander and
the Admistration Commissioner, for they should have known better than to
let such people trade on the islands. His attitude toward Chen Jin and
Wu Tingju is expressed best in a commentary that was added to the Ming
Court Records around 1522–1523.^([314]) This commentary first criticizes
Wu Tingju’s legalization of foreign trade in 1517:

  The Executive Officer Wu Tingju cleverly argued in favor of raising
  profits, and requested to establish a general rule [about foreign
  trade]. The high regional officials and the Ministry of Revenue were
  all deceived, and approved it.

The passage then continues on to discuss the Portuguese:

  Not many years had passed when the Portuguese troubles began. […] The
  source of profits itself began a problem without end. This was the
  fault of Wu Tingju.^([315])

In short, for Yang Tinghe, the core issue was the island trade system.
This illegitimate practice had become the gateway for foreigners to
haunt the Chinese coastline and abuse local citizens. The clear solution
was to stop it, and restore the Maritime Ban.

8. A Pawn Between Two Factions: The Embassy in Guangdong, 1517


(1)


 

 



Chapter 4 introduced the foreign trade eunuch and his influence over
Guangdong trade and politics. This chapter now explores his relationship
with the Portuguese. More specifically, it focuses on how he made both
Captain Fernão Peres de Andrade and the ambassador Tomé Pires into his
own clients.

The Portuguese as a False Embassy

On the coastline to the southeast of Guangzhou was the fortress of
Nantou (南頭). It served many purposes. The famous Ming military
maritime defense manual, the Chouhai tubian (籌海圖編), says that it
hosted one of Guangdong’s naval fleets.^([316]) In addition, it must
also have had a branch of the Foreign Trade Bureau, presumably run by
one of the Bureau’s assistant directors (副提舉).^([317]) Fernão Peres
de Andrade called this person the “Pio”—a rough transliteration of the
word “Foreign Trade Bureau”: shibosi (市舶司). Nantou was the nearest
fortress to the trade islands, which were scattered around what is today
Hong Kong.

When Andrade arrived in August 1517, the Assistant Director of the
Bureau tried to determine his status,^([318]) sending a letter asking
“who they were, from where they had come, and what they were
seeking.”^([319]) He wanted to know if Andrade was a private merchant
who wished to engage in island trade or an official ambassador who
wished to give tribute.

But Fernão Peres de Andrade unintentionally gave an ambiguous answer.
The Renaissance historian Fernão Lopes de Castanheda, who gives the most
literal rendition of his letter,^([320]) writes that the young captain
responded as follows:

  Fernão Peres told the messenger that one could know the desire that
  the King of Portugal had for friendship with the King of China just by
  looking at the good deeds that the governor Afonso de Albuquerque did
  to the Chinese who had arrived in the port of Melaka when he
  had seized it. And this [desire] obligated him to send his ambassador
  with a present, which he was bringing.^([321])

In other words, Fernão Peres declared himself to be a tribute
ambassador. The problem was that he had mentioned two different
countries: he claimed to come from an unknown place called “Portugal”
(which his interpreters translated as “Folangji 佛朗機”), and he also
claimed to come from Melaka, a vassal state of the Ming. While the
Bureau had doubtless heard rumors about the conquest of Melaka, those
rumors were conflicting. Some said that Portugal was indeed a real
country, located somewhere near Java.^([322]) Others said that it was
not a real country, but just a group of pirates who had sacked the
Sultanate.^([323]) It was also said that those pirates had already left
Melaka, and that the Sultan had regained control of the city.^([324])

Understandably, the Assistant Director asked Andrade to present proof
that he really was a tribute ambassador. More precisely, he wanted
Fernão Peres to show him the imperial seal that the Ming had given to
Melaka, as well as the tally slips that the Ministry of Rites had
issued for identification. This was a basic duty of the bureau, as
described in the Guangdong Tongzhi:

  [The bureau] meets with tribute-giving foreign countries. First, they
  give their tally slips to be registered. Whenever an envoy arrives,
  the Three Offices match their tallies, and check their documents and
  tribute goods to make sure that there is no fraud.^([325])

But Andrade did not possess any of these documents, for the former
Sultan of Melaka had taken them with him when he had fled the
city.^([326])

Accordingly, it seems that the Assistant Director did not trust the
newcomers. He did not treat them like honored guests, but rather like
crafty merchants. First, he wanted a bribe.^([327]) He ordered the
captain major to wait while he consulted the “Council of Guangdong.”
Procedurally speaking, this meant notifying the Three Offices. But the
Ming sources make it clear that he did not actually do so.^([328])
Instead, he kept Andrade’s letter with himself, and he waited. About six
weeks passed— from mid-August to the end of September.^([329]) Andrade
grew impatient, and suspected foul play. His letter said that it seemed
as if “everything proceeded from some personal interest of the Pio, or
trickery of the officers through whom this affair was passing.”^([330])

The turning point came when a Portuguese envoy, Giovanni da Empoli,
sailed to Nantou in order to personally plead with the Assistant
Director. At that point, Castanheda records a change in attitude:

  Hearing the letter of Fernão Peres, and his protestations, [the Bureau
  officer] was surprised that our people possessed such rationality and
  that they conducted their affairs in such good order, because he had
  regarded them as barbarians as the Chinese look at all the other
  nations.”^([331])

The Assistant Director immediately gave permission to Andrade to advance
to Guangzhou, only requesting that he wait one day before
departing.^([332]) Strikingly, he had completely reversed his position.
Such an unexplained change of attitude certainly smells of bribery.

The entry into Guangzhou was conducted in secrecy. The Assistant
Director had his own navigator guide Fernão Peres’ ship directly into
the city, where they arrived during the night.^([333]) They came to a
building “where there was a dock made of stonework.”^([334]) This must
have been the foreign guesthouse, Huaiyuan Station.^([335]) Most likely,
the bureau’s original plan was to quietly trade there, and then send the
foreigners away. In 1521, the Foreign Trade Eunuch Niu Rong (牛榮) was
accused of doing this^([336]):

  Siam and Champa, and other such foreigners, each with their ships and
  goods, came to Guangdong. But they did not report taxes. The Foreign
  Trade Eunuch Niu Rong, with his personal agents Jiang Yishan (蔣義山)
  and Huang Lin (黃麟), privately purchased their sandalwood, pepper,
  and frankincense and ashwood, and other goods. They shipped them to
  Nanjing. They also evaded taxes, selling [goods] off and storing them
  in the official warehouses.^([337])

Similarly, in 1529, the Supreme Commander of Guangdong-Guangxi Lin Fu
(林富) wrote about the practices of the Foreign Trade Eunuch:

  When foreign ships arrive, many different people come get their
  profit. The Director of the Foreign Trade Bureau does not give
  notification of this.^([338])

These passages seem to be describing the same process that Andrade
experienced. It would seem that he had become a “false embassy,” as
described in Chapter 4.

The Intervention of the Scholar Officials

Chapter 3 had introduced two leading officials in Guangdong: the Supreme
Commander Chen Jin and the Administration Commissioner Wu Tingju. That
chapter ended in summer 1517, when they successfully re-legalized the
Guangdong island trade system. Now, in October 1517, they had a new
controversy to deal with.

The Three Offices—which were led by Wu Tingju—soon discovered the
Portuguese ships and tried to stop the false embassy trade. In fact,
Andrade himself inadvertently sounded the alarm. In order to greet his
guests—or perhaps to intimidate them—he fired off his cannons.^([339])
He reported that “all the people of the city who were outside on boats
came to the pier.”^([340]) The Chinese sources say that “the sound of
their cannon was like thunder.”^([341]) All at once, the Three
Offices discovered that they had guests. The Administration
Commissioner, perhaps Tingju, then sent an angry letter, scolding the
Portuguese for entering the city without permission. But Andrade quickly
apologized.^([342])

At that point, the Three Offices put an end to the eunuch's plans.
They took control of the Portuguese dossier and began to treat it using
the official embassy procedure. This meant that a series of officials
began to visit Andrade's ships: one met with the Portuguese
interpreter;^([343]) another inspected the tribute goods, etc.^([344])
Andrade was clearly confused, writing simply that “the mandarins of the
city ordered him to be visited often with many gifts.”^([345]) Normally,
all of these steps should have been taken place out on the
islands.^([346]) But in this case, the ships had already been permitted
to enter Guangzhou, so the procedure had to be executed retroactively.
In the meantime, the Administration Commissioner (again, perhaps Tingju)
ordered the Portuguese to stay in their ships.^([347])

The last step was that the Supreme Commander, Chen Jin, officially
reported the embassy to the Court and made his recommendation as to
whether to accept it or reject it. Gu Yingxiang described Chen Jin’s
pronouncement as follows:

  The Supreme Commander ordered that, given that the Da Ming
  Huidian大明會典 (Ming Law Code) fundamentally has no record of this
  country, [the ambassador] could rest peacefully in Huaiyuan Station as
  he waited for the Court to be consulted (奏准). During which time, he
  could submit his foreign goods.^([348])

Andrade also reported something similar:

  [The Supreme Commander said that] he would write [to the king] later,
  and until the king’s response was received, the ambassador could not
  leave Guangdong. And during that time, they would send to him [i.e.
  the ambassador] all that he needed, […] and he ordered him afterwards
  to come on land with the present that he was to bring to the king of
  China.^([349])

In short, Chen Jin decided to invite the Portuguese to reside at
Huaiyuan Station until an official response came back from the
Court.^([350]) This usually took several months.

As noted in Chapter 4, Chen Jin did not have the power to make this
decision by himself. It seems that he had to negotiate with the Foreign
Trade Eunuch Ning Cheng (寗誠).^([351]) Traces of these negotiations can
be detected in the passages quoted above. On the one hand, Chen Jin
clearly suspected that this was not a true embassy, for he declared that
the law code had no record of Portugal. On the other hand, he
nonetheless allowed the embassy to enter the official guesthouse and
begin trading. This would seem to reflect the contrasting positions of
two rival powers within the Guangdong administration: the traditionalist
scholar officials, who wished to reject suspicious embassies, and the
Foreign Trade Eunuch, who protected them. The final decision was a
compromise: for the moment, Ning Cheng would be allowed to trade with
the Portuguese. But Chen Jin was recommending that the court reject
them, so after a few months, they would be sent away and not allowed to
return.

The Supreme Commander thus wrote a scathing report. A summary of it is
recorded in the Ming Court Records:

  The envoy Jiabidan Mo (加必丹末) [i.e. Tomé Pires]^([352]) and others,
  who had been sent by the country of [Portugal], offered tribute of
  local products and requested enfeoffment and the provision of tally
  slips. The Guangdong grand defender and grand coordinator stated: "Of
  the various [foreign] countries of the Southern Ocean (海南), there is
  none called [Portugal] and, further, the envoys do not have any
  documents from their country. They cannot be trusted.^([353])

The memorial was signed by the “Guangdong Grand Defender and Grand
Coordinator”—that is to say Ning Cheng (the Grand Defender) and Chen Jin
(the Grand Coordinator). This shows that they had reached a consensus.

So what attitude did Chen Jin and Wu Tingju have toward the Portuguese?
While later sources would accuse them of favoring them, such claims seem
to be exaggerated.^([354]) The general impression is that they were
pragmatic, even indifferent. In the 1518 Court Records, Chen Jin's
report on the Portuguese is preceded by another report that he wrote on
his victorious military campaign against the Yao peoples.^([355]) It
seems that he was primarily focused on the campaign, only stopping by
Guangzhou before heading back out to the front. While it is certainly
possible that he casually accepted bribes from Fernão Peres de Andrade,
he was not particularly committed to protecting the ambassadors as
clients.

The Foreign Trade Eunuch Protects the Portuguese

Not long after those events, the Portuguese ambassador Tomé Pires came
ashore and settled down in Huaiyuan Station, the official
guesthouse.^([356]) He was only supposed to stay for a few months, until
the court’s rejection letter arrived. However, even after that letter
arrived (around March 1518), he still did not leave.^([357]) On the
contrary, in early Autumn 1518, Fernão Peres de Andrade, preparing to
depart from China, reported the following:

  He took leave of the governors of Guangdong, from whom he received
  word that a letter had come to them from their king, saying that he
  could send the ambassador Tomé Pires.^([358])

That is to say, the unnamed officials did not say that the embassy had
been rejected, but rather than it had been accepted. This, of course,
was not true. Presumably, as historian Huang Qinghua has suggested, what
the “governors” really told Andrade was not that the embassy had been
approved, but rather that it would soon be approved.^([359])

These mysterious officials were most likely the new Foreign Trade Eunuch
Niu Rong and the Grand Defender Fu Lun (傅倫), who took office
in 1518.^([360]) As mentioned earlier, they were later denounced for
illegally trading with foreign merchants through the false embassy trade
system. Tomé Pires might have been bribing them to send gifts to the
Emperor. The Mingshi directly says that the Portuguese “pulled strings
with the Grand Defender and they were permitted to go to court.”^([361])
Another key source, the biography of Liang Zhuo (梁焯), ambiguously
reported that^([362]):

  The foreigners bribed people with authority and dignity, having them
  present exotic treasures to the court.^([363])

As mentioned earlier, Niu Rong was sending goods specifically to
Nanjing. This was perhaps not coincidental, for about this time, the
Zhengde Emperor himself arrived in Nanjing.^([364]) In any case, there
was doubtless a mixture of corruption and mutual interest in the
relationship between Tomé Pires and the Foreign Trade Eunuch.

Thus a standoff dragged on between the Ministry of Rites, who insisted
on rejecting the embassy, and the Guangdong eunuchs, who defended it.
The two sides wrote back-and-forth, unable to come to agreement. João de
Barros, following the envoy Cristóvão Vieira’s lost letter,^([365])
writes:

  And after three letters in all had already been sent from Guangdong to
  the King, and after the King had sent an equal amount back to the
  governors of the city, asking in great detail about our affairs, he
  ordered that the ambassador come.^([366])

In actuality, it seems that the two sides never saw eye to eye. After
two years of fruitless debate, unforeseen circumstances broke the
deadlock. In the following chapter, we will see why Pires was eventually
summoned to court.

The Perspective of Yang Tinghe

From the beginning, Yang Tinghe and his circle suspected that the
Portuguese were a false embassy. They knew about the Foreign Trade
Eunuch’s machinations. In 1518, the Minister of Rites, Mao Ji, read Chen
Jin’s report. In response, he wrote:

  Suddenly [these Portuguese ambassadors] now wish to give tribute and
  ask for vassalage! If this is deceit, [if this is] a crime of
  impostorship, or if wicked folks are taking advantage of the situation
  to imitate [an embassy], then [the Portuguese] could go around
  seducing people, provoking trouble, to the detriment of the
  region.^([367])

Particularly interesting is the mention of “wicked folks” who are taking
advantage of the situation and helping the Portuguese to “imitate” real
ambassadors. This is perhaps a reference to the eunuchs and their false
embassy system.

Yang Tinghe agreed with Mao Ji. He approved the Minister of Rites’
rejection decision, calling for the expulsion of these strange phony
ambassadors. He would continue to accuse the Portuguese of being
impostors up to the very end.
