9. Rites and War: The Debate Over Tribute Policy at the Court, 1517–1519


(1)


 

 



Chapter 5 introduced the Minister of War and the Turfan–Hami crisis.
This chapter returns to those themes. The Ming Court associated the
Portugal–Bintan problem with Turfan and Hami. Just as Minister of War
Wang Qiong viewed Turfan rather positively, in the same way he also
viewed Portugal more favorably. And just as the Minister of Rites Mao
Cheng distrusted Turfan, so also did he strongly oppose Portugal. In the
following pages, we will examine their confrontation—the confrontation
between the Ministries of Rites and War.

The Turfan–Hami Crisis and Portugal, 1517–1518

In 1513, the Turfan armies unexpectedly occupied Hami again.^([368]) The
Sultan sent a messenger to the Ming, demanding that the empire dispatch
an envoy to him, to pay a large sum silver in exchange for the city to
be returned.^([369]) The court immediately enacted Ma Wensheng’s
“tribute denial policy,” arresting Turfan’s ambassadors and demanding
that Hami be restored.^([370]) But it was confused and unsure what to
do.

The Secretariat dispatched Supreme Commander Peng Ze (彭澤) to Gansu
province in order to negotiate with Turfan.^([371]) The choice was not
coincidental. Peng Ze was in fact a student of Yang Tinghe, having been
his disciple many years before, when he had been assigned to Tinghe’s
home province of Sichuan.^([372]) The Grand Secretary was grooming him
to be a future minister, giving him a high profile assignment. But Ze
might not have been the best candidate for the position. The Mingshi
offers the following assessment of him: “Ze had military talent and army
knowledge. But he was scatter-brained and impulsive.”^([373])

Ze held secret talks with Turfan. Even today it is not clear what
precisely they concluded, for the sources give contradictory
information.^([374]) It seems that he agreed to pay a ransom and to
release the Turfan diplomatic hostages, and in exchange the Sultan would
withdraw his armies from Hami and return the city’s seal. But the Sultan
refused to release Hami’s king.^([375]) It seems that he was willing to
give the Ming nominal jurisdiction over the city, but he wanted to keep
de facto control. For Peng Ze, this was good enough. He triumphantly
announced to the Secretariat that he had succeeded in pacifying the
border and requested permission to return to Beijing. He did not mention
that he had paid a bribe to Turfan.^([376])

After Ze departed, the deal collapsed. The chieftain of the Muslim
community of Hami, a man named Xieyi Huxian (寫亦虎先), had been sent to
deliver part of the ransom to the Sultan.^([377]) After difficult
negotiations, Huxian received the seal, and the two parties formed a
joint Hami–Turfan embassy to officially return it to the Ming.^([378])
This embassy arrived in Ganzhou, and there they presented the treasured
item.^([379]) It was now the turn of the Gansu officials to uphold their
end of the agreement. They were supposed to release the Turfan tribute
ambassadors whom they had taken hostage. But they, holding strictly to
the tribute denial policy, refused to do so.^([380]) They took the seal
but did not release the prisoners.

The Sultan felt that he had been tricked. In 1517, he sent his armies
out again, and they not only re-occupied Hami, but also surged forward
and attacked the Ming border town of Suzhou (肅州). They were unable to
break through the city walls, so they pillaged the surrounding area and
then departed.^([381]) When news arrived in Beijing, the court was
shocked. Had not Peng Ze declared that the border had been pacified?

Still, the Sultan of Turfan did not want a war with the Ming Empire. He
thus sent another embassy to Ganzhou to ask for peace.^([382]) The
provincial officials demanded that the Sultan return the King of Hami,
return the pillaged goods, and return all the hostages whom the Sultan’s
armies had seized during their attack. Turfan thus sent another peace
embassy, which returned some of the hostages, but not the King. The
Gansu officials reported this to the court and asked for guidance.

In Beijing, an intense debate began: should the court accept Turfan’s
peace offer, or should it demand that the Sultan fully restore Hami
first? In the debate, the Minister of War Wang Qiong and the Minister of
Rites Mao Ji began to cautiously take sides against each other. As a
firm traditionalist, Mao Ji wanted to deal harshly with Turfan. In
contrast, Wang Qiong wanted conciliation. In his memorial of November
11, 1517, he asked Gansu to send the Turfan embassy to Beijing:

  [The Turfan ambassadors] should be joined by a local escort who would
  accompany them to the capital. They will temporarily stay at the
  Foreign Guest House where they will be held in custody while they wait
  for their matter to be investigated.^([383])

Already in this decision, he gave a hint of his opinion: he wanted to
allow the suspects to stay in the official guesthouse—an unusually
generous privilege.

It was in this context that, on February 11, 1518, the Minister of
Rites, Mao Ji, received another memorial. This one was from Guangdong.
The Supreme Commander Chen Jin reported the arrival of a strange embassy
from a country called “Portugal.” As Mao Ji wrote his response, one can
sense that he was thinking about Turfan:

  This country [i.e. Portugal] relied on its own force to pass through
  Melaka and Sumendala. Everywhere, it did pillaging. If in the future
  they were to receive a document from the court giving orders to issue
  them tallies, that might support their plans for mischief. And if
  various overseas countries were to hear of this, they would not be so
  willing to completely offer respectful loyalty.^([384])

In other words, Mao Ji was worried about setting a precedent. He worried
that approving Portugal would give a bad example to “various overseas
countries.” Portugal, like Turfan, had occupied a Ming vassal state, and
then asked to give tribute. If Portugal were approved, then this would
only encourage Turfan—and Wang Qiong.

Wishing to maintain a consistent tribute denial policy, Mao Ji
instructed Chen Jin to reject Portugal.

Rites and War: The Philosophical Debate over Tribute Policy

Over the next two years, the debate intensified. On the one side, the
Minister of War Wang Qiong increasingly asked to abandon the tribute
denial policy and restore relations with Turfan.^([385]) In a 1519
memorial, he simply gave one brief word of explanation for his
suggestion:

  If they are not allowed to give tribute, and the regional officials
  continue to not obey the court’s orders, […] then I fear that [Turfan]
  will grow suspicious and provoke border trouble.^([386])

Later, when the Turfan–Hami crisis flared up again in 1527, he would
explain these ideas more explicitly, saying^([387]):

  [Refusing tribute] cannot achieve peace. […] If by chance Turfan were
  to sweep across the land towards us, our border defenses are in
  shambles. What’s more, the price of grain [i.e. army supplies] is high
  due to drought.^([388])

Wang Qiong recognized that, realistically, the Ming could not reconquer
Hami: the border defenses were too weak; the cost of an expeditionary
force would be too high. Indeed, if the empire were to annoy its
neighbor, it risked losing even more territory. The most practical
option was simply to make peace. Furthermore, Qiong considered the
Mongols to be the greatest threat.^([389]) He wanted Turfan to be an
ally in resisting the northern invaders.

But Qiong’s proposal was highly controversial, since it meant tacitly
permitting the Sultan to keep Hami. Yang Tinghe’s faction could not
accept this. They insisted on the primacy of justice, the importance of
maintaining proper lord–vassal relations. Turfan had to be held
accountable for its crimes. In 1520, the Investigating Censor of Shaanxi
criticized the Ministry of War’s approach:

  If they rebel and are not firmly condemned, then they will benefit
  from pillaging. If they come give tribute and are not firmly rejected,
  then they will benefit from the court’s rewards.^([390])

To allow tribute would not only teach the wrong lesson to Turfan, but
also set a precedent, one that would surely be noticed by other
rebellious vassals—such as Portugal.

But Wang Qiong spurned such objections. In the later 1520s, he would
say:

  We are the ones who mistreat [Turfan], because we fear their proud
  spirits. Thus we only know how to stick to the past: namely, that we
  want to cut off their tribute route. But we do not know how to
  evaluate the present: namely, that we cannot cut off their invasion
  route!^([391])

In short, by late 1519, the Ministry of War and the Ministry of Rites
were openly opposed. They had fundamentally different views of tribute
policy.

Rites and War: Personal and Factional Strife

At the same time, the personal rivalry between Wang Qiong and Yang
Tinghe intensified. The controversy centered on the figure of Peng Ze,
former Supreme Commander of Gansu. Yang Tinghe had wanted Peng Ze to be
the Minister of War.^([392]) That was why he had called him back to
Beijing in 1513. But Wang Qiong had appealed to the Emperor’s favorites,
and had seized the Ministry for himself.^([393]) Qiong thus feared Ze as
an adversary, and sought to eliminate him.^([394]) He wished to blame Ze
for the Turfan crisis, and launched an investigation in 1518. This
became a proxy battle against Yang Tinghe himself.

One of the chief witnesses against Ze was the aforementioned chief of
the Hami Muslim community, Xieyi Huxian. Huxian doubtless knew many dark
secrets, since he had been the negotiator with Turfan. At the time of
the investigation, the Ming court held him as a diplomatic hostage. But
Wang Qiong made plea deal with him, freeing him of guilt in exchange for
his help in the trial. Meanwhile, the Ministry of Rites searched for
ways to impede him.

At this point, it is important to introduce the Beijing Foreign
Guesthouse (會同舘).^([395]) This was the housing facility for all
visiting tribute embassies. It not only played a key role in the Peng Ze
trial, but also in the investigation on the Portuguese, as will be seen
in Chapter 11. Indeed, it was not simply a hotel. It was managed by the
Ministry of Rites, and it helped maintain the smooth functioning of the
tribute system: making sure that foreign guests obeyed the law, showed
up on time for court functions, and were given the proper ceremonial
gifts and banquets. With few other tools at his disposal, Mao Cheng used
the guesthouse to thwart Wang Qiong’s plans. The Ming Court Records say:

  Wang Qiong then sent his servant Chu Xunlu (儲詢路) to directly order
  the Guesthouse director Zhang Can (張燦) to incite the foreigners who
  were detained in the facility, and order them to denounce Peng Ze. But
  [Qiong] was unable.^([396])

The director of the guesthouse, Zhang Can, held the keys to the
facility, and had the power to detain its guests. He thus refused to
release Xieyi Huxian from his room. The Mingshi says:

  [Wang Qiong] invited the foreigners in the Guesthouse to denounce Peng
  Ze for committing crimes, and he enticed Zhang Can to sign the record,
  saying, “Peng Ze’s behavior followed the path of destruction of the
  Southern Song. If you do this, you will clearly be promoted.” Zhang
  Can firmly refused, saying, “Sir Wang is mistaken. Peng Ze did present
  a war declaration to Turfan. How can this be compared to the border
  appeasement (和戎) of the Song? ［…]” And he did not agree to sign the
  document.^([397])

Xieyi Huxian was thus unable to testify against Peng Ze. The Rites
official Liang Zhuo, who was probably Zhang Can’s assistant, described
this in more detail in his biography:

  The foreigners Xieyi Huxian and his nephew Mihei’er Maheima
  (米黑兒馬黑麻), while giving tribute, slandered the great military and
  civil officials of Gansu. […] The two foreigners rode horses in the
  marketplace, and enjoyed the meals reserved for great officials in the
  Ministry of Justice, and they rode palanquins to go eat the remaining
  fine meals at the foreign guesthouse, and they sat and rose at the
  same time as public servants. […] In doing each of these things, they
  were belittling court officials. In each of these things, Liang Zhuo
  restricted them using the law.^([398])

That is to say, as Rites officials, the guesthouse directors could
punish Xieyi Huxian for protocol violations. Whenever they saw the Hami
chieftain taking privileges that were not properly his own—privileges
such as riding horses or palanquins, or having special banquets, or
immediately sitting down without respectfully deferring to court
officials—they immediately punished him. The punishment was apparently
to lock him into his room. A frustrating legal game thus played out:
over and over, whenever Huxian would try to go to the Ministry of
Justice to testify against Peng Ze (“the great military and civil
officials of Gansu”), the guesthouse directors arrested him on protocol
violations and sent him back to the facility. This curious strategy
should be pointed out, for we will see it again in Chapter 11, when
Liang Zhuo will use it against the Portuguese.

Despite losing his witness, Wang Qiong pushed forward with his plans and
condemned Peng Ze. The Court Records record an intense debate at the
court. When Mao Cheng tried to defend the former supreme commander,
Qiong grew angry.

“Throw [Peng Ze] in the cangue before the palace!” he said. “Torture him
to make him cough up the truth!”^([399])

“What are you saying?” retorted Mao Cheng. “Traditionally, you cannot
torture a high official!”^([400])

As always, the Emperor supported Qiong, and condemned Peng Ze to death.
But Yang Tinghe quietly cleared his old disciple of criminal charges,
simply letting him resign.^([401])

The Court Grants Permission to Portugal

In 1519, in the midst of this struggle between Rites and War, the Court
received another memorial from Guangdong. A new tribute embassy had
arrived, this time from the Sultanate of Bintan. In contrast to the
Portuguese embassy, these envoys could prove their legitimacy: they had
brought the Ming seal and tally slips.^([402]) “With great fear [the
Sultan] had taken the seal of the King of China and fled,” their letter
said.^([403]) As the “true” tribute embassy of Melaka, they were
permitted to come to court.

Why had the Sultan sent an embassy? The Portuguese envoy Cristóvão
Vieira wrote^([404]):

  The Malays said that the ambassador from the King of Portugal who was
  in China did not come in truth, that he had come falsely in order to
  deceive, and that we would usually come see locations and that
  afterwards we would come against them. And since we had placed a stone
  [a padrão] and had a trade factory, afterwards we would have this land
  as our own. [They said] that we were bandits.^([405])

The Sultan’s ambassadors had a specific request: they wanted military
support in order to take Melaka back from the Portuguese. Barros,
following Vieira’s lost letter, writes that the embassy^([406]):

  …complained to the King of China, saying that we had taken their
  kingdom, asking him to send help, since [the Sultan] was his vassal
  and had received his seal as a sign of obedience.^([407])

The Ming Court Records also summarize the same letter as follows:
“Melaka also memorialized, asking the Court for aid.”^([408])

At this point, the court’s perception of Portugal changed. This can be
ascertained from the fact that at about the same time that the Bintan
embassy was summoned to court, Tomé Pires was also summoned. Both
embassies left Guangdong around January 1520.^([409])

Why did the Ministry of Rites change its position on Portugal? Clearly,
Mao Cheng must have been surprised by the Sultan’s letter. We have seen
that his predecessor, Mao Ji, had assumed that Portugal was not a real
country, just a band of ocean-roving pirates who were pretending to give
tribute. But the Bintan embassy now confirmed that Tomé Pires’ story was
true: Portugal really did exist, and it really did occupy Melaka. This
called for a more official diplomatic response. The case of Annam and
Champa is particularly instructive. During the Chenghua era, Annam
invaded its neighbor, Champa—inappropriate behavior for a vassal state.
However, in 1472, the court decided not to immediately cut out off
relations with Annam, but rather to wait for its envoys to come to
Beijing. At that point, it formally ordered the Annam ambassador to
return the stolen territory.^([410]) This was perhaps the precedent that
Mao Cheng had in mind in allowing Portugal to come to court.

For Wang Qiong, the arrival of the Bintan embassy must have had a
different meaning. The envoys wanted military aid. At this time, when
Qiong was struggling to defend the border against Turfan in the west and
the Mongols in the north—not to mention the rebellion of the Prince of
Ning—this was an absurd request. Just as it was better to accept tribute
from Turfan rather than engage in an unwinnable war, in the same way, it
was better to accept tribute from Portugal rather than be dragged into a
distant overseas dispute.

The Ming Court Records affirm that Portugal was officially approved to
come to court, but they do not say who approved that request. A later
court memorial simply notes: “it was commanded that they be permitted to
come to the capital.”^([411]) Gu Yingxiang says that “they obeyed
imperial orders which permitted them to give tribute, and arrived in
Beijing.”^([412]) Given that the Portugal–Bintan dossier was a case of
military occupation, it seems reasonable to assume that it was under the
jurisdiction of the Ministry of War. Wang Qiong was most likely the one
who made the approval.

10. In the Company of the Emperor in Nanjing, 1519–1520


(1)


 

 



Chapter 6 discussed the various ways that the Ming emperors in general,
and the Zhengde Emperor in particular, violated the Maritime Ban in
their private lives. This chapter returns to that theme, exploring the
relationship between the Son of Heaven and Tomé Pires, the
apothecary-turned-ambassador.

The Emperor Decides to Depart

In the summer of 1519, urgent news reached the court: far to the south,
in the province of Jiangxi, Zhu Chenhao (朱宸濠), the Prince of Ning,
had revolted.^([413]) He was a nobleman, a distant relative of the
Zhengde Emperor. The initial reports were frightening: he had seized
several small cities and now was moving toward the southern capital of
the empire, Nanjing.^([414])

At that time, the Emperor was idle in Beijing, dabbling in his usual
pleasures. He was also spending time at the Beijing foreign guesthouse,
where he befriended the envoy from Hami, Xieyi Huxian.^([415]) The news
of the revolt excited him, for it gave him an opportunity for adventure.
Overruling Yang Tinghe’s objections, he forced the Minister of Personnel
to issue a decree:

  Immediately order the overall military commander […] the great lord
  protector of the country (鎮國公), Zhu Shou (朱壽) to personally
  command the border armies on a military campaign.^([416])

“Zhu Shou” was in fact the Zhengde Emperor’s own alter-ego. It was a
name that he had created for himself, to embody his military
persona.^([417]) This was thus the Emperor’s declaration that he would
personally go to Nanjing to defeat the rebel Prince.

But a few days later, news arrived in the capital: the Supreme Commander
of Jiangxi, the famous philosopher Wang Yangming (王陽明), had captured
Zhu Chenhao. The crisis was over. But that did not stop the Emperor. He
continued with his plans to lead his armies southward. What was
originally a military expedition would now become a touristic
excursion.^([418])

Yang Tinghe had no institutional power to resist the Emperor.
Accordingly, he tried to use non-institutional methods. Two
investigating censors broke the official “non-remonstrance” orders and
submitted a long memorial, a strongly worded litany of complaints
against the Emperor’s recent behavior. They called for the dismissal of
the Emperor’s favorite, Jiang Bin: “If Jiang Bin is not executed, then
he will certainly be the beginning of the empire’s ruin.”^([419])
Furthermore, they bemoaned the Emperor’s favoritism for foreign priests
and his neglect of scholar officials: “Reject heretics, push away petty
people! Summon veteran officials, consult faithful people!”^([420]) They
also asked him to stop calling himself Zhu Shou: “This game has no
benefit!”^([421]) And finally, they asked him to renounce his voyage
south. Before the Emperor could punish these two daring individuals, a
large crowd of junior court officials gathered around them, voicing
their support.

Jiang Bin felt threatened, and used his power to punish this crowd. He
forced some of them to bow under the hot sun before the Noon Gate for
five days, and he threw others into jail.^([422]) The director of the
guesthouse, Liang Zhuo, was among those punished. His biography says:

  Jiang Bin hated Liang Zhuo. [Bin] directed [Zhuo’s] punishment to be
  harsh. He was caned. While that happened, [Zhuo] cursed Jiang Bin, and
  his voice would not cease.^([423])

Even after this, Investigating Censors continued to warn the Emperor
against such a reckless project, but the Son of Heaven gave a stern
response:

  Henceforth, if anyone who dares to openly memorialize and cause
  trouble, I will certainly punish him according to the Grand Law,
  without mercy.^([424])

The Emperor thus departed for Nanjing.

Imperial Exoticism in Nanjing

The Emperor arrived in the southern capital in January 1520.^([425]) A
great train of courtiers and servants followed him: “On the water route,
[…] the waiting crowds number in the hundreds of thousands!” exclaimed
Grand Secretary Jiang Mian.^([426]) The Emperor had brought his hunting
horses and racing boats.^([427]) Mian, exasperated, asked to move the
herds out of the city.^([428]) Furthermore, there are reports that
eunuchs were sent to Yangzhou to abduct young women as
concubines.^([429]) One of the investigating censors bemoaned their fate
in Nanjing: “Young girls and older wives fill up the summer palace,
exposed to the harsh rain and cold wind.”^([430]) It is difficult to
assess the reliability of these reports, but there can be no doubt that
the city was cramped and chaotic.

The Emperor also continued to take interest in foreigners, and
particular, in his Muslim guests. Shortly before arriving in Nanjing, he
issued a decree banning the killing of pigs.^([431]) The Wuzong Waiji
gives the most detailed version of the anecdote:

  In regards to raising swine and killing pigs: […] [the emperor’s]
  regular interpreter had already been given a noble title and the
  emperor’s own name (“Zhu 朱”). The word “pig” (“zhu 豬”) had a
  different character but the same pronunciation. Moreover, when [the
  emperor] ate [pork], he then felt deeply sick, and had constipation.
  Because of this, he reflected deeply and gave the orders: in the
  region, pigs were not allowed to be raised, sold, or killed; only cows
  and sheep and other permissible animals,.^([432])

This ban shocked many scholars of the time.^([433]) It was decidedly not
traditional, and it troubled Yang Tinghe. He wrote a memorial to the
Emperor:

  Mengzi said: “In keeping fowls, pigs, dogs, and swine, let not their
  times of breeding be neglected, and persons of seventy years may eat
  flesh.”^([434]) That was the way of the wise kings of old.^([435])

The Grand Secretary appealed to Confucian authority, but the Emperor
just answered with his usual “no response.”^([436]) Yang Tinghe was
worried about the rumors that he heard, and memorialized:

  Muslim foreigners are close by [the emperor’s] side. Foreign priests
  are invited to accompany him. Such things as this have never been
  heard of before!^([437])

Untraditional Foreign Policy in Nanjing

As mentioned in the previous chapter, the Portuguese embassy was
dispatched toward the court around January 1520. The normal procedure
was to proceed to Beijing, in order to bow at the Noon Gate. This was
what most other embassies did during the 1519–1520 years: for example,
the numerous envoys from Joseon and the Jurchen tribes.^([438]) A Ryukyu
embassy which arrived in 1519 also proceeded to Beijing in the regular
manner.^([439])

However, it seems that the Emperor specifically diverted certain foreign
embassies—the ones that he liked—away from Beijing, having them come
directly to Nanjing instead. The envoys from Joseon were surprised by
the situation. Upon returning to their country, they reported the rumors
about the southern capital:

  Usually, when the Emperor goes out, he chooses a few people from
  various countries such as Tartary, HuiHui, Portugal, Champa, and the
  Lamas, and puts them in his entourage.^([440])

In looking at this list of foreign countries that were surrounding the
Emperor, one can note two trends. First of all, the countries which the
Emperor had always loved were present: the Hui Muslims (including Xieyi
Huxian, whom the Emperor had brought with him),^([441]) the Mongols, and
the Buddhist monks. Second of all, the countries specifically from the
Guangzhou Foreign Trade Bureau were present: Champa, Portugal, and
Bintan. This might be explained by the fact that the Guangzhou Foreign
Trade Eunuch, Niu Rong, had connections in Nanjing, as discussed in
Chapter 8.

The foreign embassies that went to Nanjing did not go there for official
business, but rather to entertain the Emperor. The Son of Heaven told
all of them that he would not deal with their diplomatic matters until
after he returned to Beijing. For example, Cristóvão Vieira said:

  In May, we were with the king in Nanjing. From there, he ordered us to
  go ahead to the city of Beijing, in order to give us his judgement
  there.^([442])

In his lost letter, he also mentioned that the Bintan embassy was told
something similar:

  When the King left for Beijing, he ordered to tell [the Bintan
  embassy] that they should go ahead, that he would hear them
  there.^([443])

As for the Turfan embassy, the last memorial that Wang Qiong made
concerning it was from August 1519—just before the Emperor departed for
Nanjing.^([444]) It seems that once the Emperor left, he took the Turfan
envoys with him, and all official diplomacy was put on hold.

In fact, the Emperor’s circle might have intentionally disabled the
normal government functions in order to block foreign affairs in
Nanjing. There was a Nanjing branch of the Ministry of Rites, which
could certainly have handled tribute procedure. But it seems that it had
no minister to lead it. In June 1519, an old, respected scholar, Shao
Bao (邵寶), asked to leave court in order to care for his sick mother.
Out of compassion for him, the court granted him a sinecure in Nanjing
(which was near his hometown of Wuxi 無錫), so that he could have an
income during the convalescence period.^([445]) In August 1519, as the
Emperor prepared to set forth on his journey, he promoted Shao Bao to be
the Nanjing Minister of Rites.^([446]) But it seems that Shao Bao
continued to stay in Wuxi, for sixteen months later, in January 1521, he
was still there, still caring for his mother.^([447]) In other words,
during the entire time of the Emperor’s visit, there was no active
Ministry of Rites in Nanjing. It had been effectively adjourned.

Accordingly, when Tomé Pires arrived in the southern capital, what
ensued was not a controlled diplomatic process, but rather a chaotic
competition for imperial favor. The rival Bintan ambassador tried to
secure an audience before Pires could arrive. Vieira, in his lost
letter, wrote:

  An ambassador named Tuam Mahamed, whom the King of Bintan—which had
  [formerly] been Melaka—had sent before Tomé Pires... […] That
  ambassador, when Tomé Pires arrived in the city of Nanjing, was going
  around waiting to hear from the King [i.e. the Emperor].^([448])

Vieira also mentions that the ambassador later bribed the governor of
the city to send information to the court.^([449])

But Tomé Pires was quite a wiley strategist himself. Furthermore, he had
a skillful interpreter named Huozhe Yasan (火者亞三), who knew how to
pull strings. This mysterious figure has been the object of many
debates.^([450]) “Yasan” was likely a transliteration of “Hassan.” He
was probably a middle-aged man, daring and proud, of Chinese Malay
heritage. Pires sent him to meet with Jiang Bin to try to win favor.
Liang Zhuo’s biography says:

  The powerful official Jiang Bin led the imperial armies, following the
  Emperor on his leisure tour. He took a bribe from the Portuguese, and
  led Huozhe Yasan to have an audience. The Emperor was delighted, and
  kept him.^([451])

The diplomatic gambit had succeeded. The eyes of the Son of Heaven
shifted to focus upon the apothecary from Portugal. 

The Emperor Makes Pires into a Personal Client

Tomé Pires thus became a personal client of the Emperor.^([452]) He
wrote a letter on his situation, which was later summarized as follows:

  [Pires] was very well received by the king and by all his people, and
  much unspeakable honor was given to him. And the king of the Chinese
  was so delighted with him, and with the news that he gave about your
  highness [Dom Manuel], that he [the emperor] did not go riding without
  him.^([453])

From the above, it is obvious that Pires received a privilege that most
tribute embassies did not: he spoke with the Emperor face-to-face. In
his extant letter, Vieira particularly insists on this point:

  In Nanjing, we saw the king in person, who was going about having fun,
  against the style and custom of the land, which has the custom that
  the king never leaves his residence. And, since the country of China
  is a country, rarely has a king dared to abandon the custom, nor has a
  foreigner seen the king of China, like I say that we saw him.^([454])

Later, when the Joseon envoys came to visit the Portuguese in Beijing,
Pires boasted to them about this personal contact. The envoys described
the his room in the foreign guesthouse:

  In the middle of the room was a single chair. They had covered it with
  a red cover, saying: “this here is the place where the Emperor had
  deigned to sit.” For when they gave tribute, the Emperor met with them
  while traveling. He had come and seen their residence.^([455])

Pires enjoyed numerous amusements with the Emperor. In mid-1520, when
the torrential summer rains finally stopped, the Emperor departed on a
massive hunting trip in the hills.^([456]) Pires rode in the party. He
wrote to Simão de Andrade saying that “[the king] brought him hunting
many times.”^([457]) Vieira says that the envoys were also invited to
the Emperor’s parties: “He had us attend banquets with all the magnates.
As of now, we have seen this three times.”^([458]) (These were probably
private banquets rather than formal ceremonies, since there is no record
of them in the Chinese sources.^([459])) Pires also reported more
broadly that the Emperor “showed him all his entertainments.”^([460])
This perhaps meant that he joined the Emperor on his sightseeing
outings,^([461]) or that he got to watch Chinese opera on the stage that
had specially been set up for the court.^([462]) On a sidenote, Vieira
also mentions that Pires played chess with the Emperor.^([463])

The Son of Heaven was fascinated with Portuguese culture. As noted
above, Pires spoke with him about Portuguese arts, reporting that he
was, “constantly asking [Pires] about the customs and ways of your
highness [i.e. Dom Manuel].”^([464]) Just as the Emperor enjoyed wearing
the clothes of Buddhist priests and Muslim clerics, so also did he wish
to try on European pants and shirts. Vieira wrote:

  [The emperor] entered in the ships in which we were voyaging. He
  ordered all the trunks to be taken outside. He took the clothes that
  seemed good to him.^([465])

Similarly, he might have learned some Portuguese words. The Joseon
ambassadors heard a report that whenever the Emperor met with foreign
ambassadors in Nanjing, he enjoyed learning about their culture:

  Sometimes he studied their language, sometimes he watched their
  arts.^([466])

The Mingshancang (名山藏), following an unknown source, says:

  The Emperor saw [Huozhe Yasan]. Often he studied his language for
  fun.^([467])

Finally, the Emperor gave many gifts to Pires. Vieira says this
obliquely: “He ordered us to be given the best ships that there were in
the land of China, along with […] trade goods.”^([468]) He also says
that when they were imprisoned in Guangzhou in 1521, the ambassadors
possessed about 1500 rolls of fine silk and about 4000 sheets of Nanjing
silk.^([469]) Such large quantities must have presumably been gifts.

More darkly, it seems that the Son of Heaven also gave them concubines.
Vieira later reported that at the time of their arrest, Pires and the
interpreters all had “women,” who were confiscated and sold off as
slaves.^([470]) It is possible that these were people who had been
abducted from Yangzhou, as mentioned above.

The Emperor’s Personal Invitation to Beijing

At the end of his stay in Nanjing, the Emperor made an important
decision: he chose to invite the Portuguese envoys to come to Beijing as
his guests. “He gave the grace to Tomé Pires that we go to Beijing,”
wrote Vieira.^([471]) Gu Yingxiang also heard about this: “[The Emperor]
kept them at the Guesthouse for more than half a year.”^([472])

On this topic, the most precise source is Liang Zhuo’s biography. Zhuo
received a letter from the security guard of the Portuguese embassy,
reporting that: “the Emperor kept seven people, including Huozhe
Yasan.”^([473]) That is to say, he did not retain all thirty members of
the ambassadorial team, only the chief ambassadors and their
interpreters. This point was also hinted at in the aforementioned
quotation from the Joseon envoys, which said that the Emperor selected
“a few of their envoys.”

Similarly, the Emperor also made another important decision: he chose
not to invite the Bintan embassy to Beijing. The evidence here is more
circumstantial. Whereas the Portuguese embassy arrived in Beijing around
October or November 1520, and the Emperor himself arrived on December
5,^([474]) it seems that the Bintan embassy never arrived. In January
13, 1521, when the Minister of Rites Mao Cheng presented his memorial on
the Portuguese, he mentioned that the Bintan embassy still was not in
Beijing.^([475]) The Court Records never mention the embassy formally
giving tribute, which implies that they never came to the capital. No
explanation is ever given for why, but it is likely that they simply had
not been invited.^([476]) All the descriptions of the Bintan ambassadors
suggest that they were worried, that they urgently tried to press the
Emperor to hear their cries for help. Perhaps the Son of Heaven viewed
them as bothersome diplomats rather than as “friends.”

The Perspective of Yang Tinghe

Yang Tinghe never specifically commented on the activities of the
Portuguese in Nanjing. Perhaps he had been ordered not to remonstrate on
this topic. Nonetheless, his attitude can be inferred from the actions
of Liu Chun. Chun was the former Minister of Rites, who, in Chapter 3,
had fought so valiantly to restore the Maritime Ban. He had lost this
struggle, and had been demoted, by coincidence, to Nanjing.^([477]) Yang
Tinghe had not forgotten his old friend. In 1519, as the Emperor
prepared to depart for that city, the Grand Secretary made a request:

  The Secretariat lacks an official to manage the imperial decrees. The
  Secretary Yang Tinghe recommends promoting the Nanjing Minister of
  Personnel Liu Chun for the position.^([478])

Tinghe wanted his friend to keep an eye on the Emperor in the southern
capital. But the Emperor saw through the ploy. Instead of his usual “no
response,” he explicitly rejected Tinghe’s request, asking him to
nominate somebody else. Tinghe insisted, leaving the post vacant,
refusing to propose any other candidate. He even convinced his rival,
Grand Secretary Liang Chu, to support Chun for the position.^([479]) But
the Emperor adamantly refused.

Nonetheless, when the imperial camp arrived in Nanjing, Liu Chun did his
best to control the situation. But with his limited powers, he could do
little. He later complained to the Court:

  The Nanjing Minister of Personnel Liu Chun denounced his own guilt,
  and also accused the officials You Yue (尤樾) and Huang Qi (黃祺) of
  being lax in surveillance on that day that the Emperor had arrived in
  Nanjing, and hundreds of officials had all come out to meet him. Liu
  Chun accused himself and asked to retire.^([480])

That is to say, Chun had tried to maintain proper protocol and procedure
in Nanjing, but he was simply overwhelmed. The crowds (which perhaps
included the foreign envoys) had approached the Emperor in a disorderly
manner. For this failure, Chun asked to be dismissed. But again, in an
unusual gesture, the Emperor directly responded to the petition:

  The Emperor, however, said that this was not a crime. He only imposed
  a fine of three-month’s salary on You Yue and Huang Qi.^([481])

In summary, for Yang Tinghe, the ultimate problem was doubtless not the
foreigners per se. Rather, the problem was the Emperor himself. He and
his favorites, such as Jiang Bin, were disregarding protocol. They were
permitting forms of sociality—and forms of diplomacy—which should have
been illegal.

11. At the Heavenly Court in Beijing, 1520


(1)


 

 



Up to this point, the book has examined the interactions between the
Portuguese and Yang Tinghe’s rivals: people like the Supreme Commander
of Guangdong-Guangxi, the Foreign Trade Eunuch, and the Minister of
War—all of whom supported the embassy in various ways. In this chapter,
however, the Portuguese encounter Yang Tinghe’s own circle, and their
interaction is much less cooperative.

Entering the Guesthouse in Beijing

In September 1520, the Emperor at last departed from Nanjing.^([482])
When Yang Tinghe heard the news, he wrote a celebratory memorial:

  The word has come that the Emperor is now coming back home. He has
  already left the southern capital and soon his triumphal return to the
  court will be memorialized. All officials, big and small, central and
  provincial, hear it, and their joy is incomparable!^([483])

However, Yang Tinghe bemoaned the many affairs that urgently needed to
be dealt with: there were rituals that needed to be performed, and
memorials that needed to be addressed. Among those memorials were
several foreign affairs dossiers that had been neglected for nearly two
years. Yang Tinghe now wished to process them:

  Clearly investigate the foreign envoys who had been sent in past years
  from the region of Gansu to give tribute. Also, order them to stay at
  the gates.

The foreign envoys from Gansu were the tribute embassies of Turfan and
Hami, which included Xieyi Huxian. The memorial also mentions Portugal:

  Also, recently, [Portugal] and the countries of Melaka [i.e. Bintan]
  and Champa have submitted [foreign]-script documents in respect of
  their regions. None of these matters has been handled. The
  [foreigners]'s sentiments are changeable and these matters must be
  given consideration.^([484])

Tinghe clearly viewed these groups with suspicion. One sign of this is
that he did not want the Turfan and Hami ambassadors to be allowed into
the official foreign guesthouse. Rather, he wanted them to “stay at the
gates”—i.e. to wait outside the city. The Grand Secretary wanted careful
investigations to be conducted on them before welcoming them as official
embassies.

Moving from Nanjing to Beijing, Tomé Pires doubtless felt the political
climate change. While Jiang Bin ran the southern capital like a circus,
Yang Tinghe made sure the northern capital strictly adhered to tradition
and high ceremony. And while in Guangzhou, the Foreign Trade Eunuch
readily accepted bribes in exchange for favors, in Beijing, the Director
of the Guesthouse, Liang Zhuo, was incorruptible and uncompromising.
Pires and company would be unpleasantly surprised .

As mentioned in the previous chapter, only seven members of the
Portuguese embassy had been invited to Beijing. But the sources agree
that the entire thirty-person party came northward.^([485]) The guard
who was escorting them sent advance-warning to Liang Zhuo: “They are not
obeying the limitation. They should not be allowed to enter the
capital!”^([486]) Zhuo, in turn, notified the imperial office of this
violation, but the Emperor’s circle approved of it, rather than
condemning it.^([487])

To make matters worse, when the Portuguese did arrive, they were just as
insolent as the rumors said. The Director of the Guesthouse was
horrified to hear Pires and his men belittle his beloved facility: “When
they arrived at the guesthouse,” his biography records, “they hated how
small the rooms were. They did not want to stay there.”^([488]) This
rude entry was also reported by the Joseon envoy Sin Sang (申鏛), who
was also in Beijing at the time:

  When [the Portuguese] first came to give tribute, they considered the
  Guesthouse [玉河館] to be ugly, and there were many disrespectful
  words. The Ministry of Rites hated their lack of manners.^([489])

In defense of the Portuguese, it should be noted that when the Japanese
envoy Sakugen (策彦) visited the guesthouse in 1537, he had a similarly
unpleasant impression:

  The facility was dilapidated. Although we leisurely arrived at the
  guesthouse [玉河館], there was no place to rest. There was just one
  building and the woodworkers were repairing it. We counted on our
  fingers and waited.^([490])

Sakugen had the discretion to keep his thoughts to himself and did not
complain. The Europeans, in contrast, were not so soft-spoken.

In addition, Zhuo and Pires also clashed over the strict rules of the
guesthouse. The Director wished to follow Yang Tinghe’s instructions to
carefully watch and restrain these suspicious guests. He treated Pires
much like he had treated Xieyi Huxian in Chapter 9—i.e. he locked him
into his room and did not allow him to set foot outside. His biography
records that:

  He ran the facility with strict prohibitions; he impeded their open
  movements [風路]; he strictly forbid the foreigners from leaving and
  entering.^([491])

This would expain Cristóvão Vieira’s quite negative assessment of the
lodgings. He simply wrote that:

  The custom for ambassadors in Beijing is to put them in some houses
  like big barnyards, and to keep them shut in there.^([492])

The Portuguese did try to win over Zhuo with bribes, but his biography
proudly says that he refused them all:

  Zhuo upheld the law. At first they tried to make him submit, giving
  him much gold and silver and precious goods. But Zhuo did not give
  them even a single glance.^([493])

Despite the tensions, during these initial few weeks at the court, the
Portuguese were generally treated like other tribute ambassadors. For
example, Cristóvão Vieira wrote that they joined with the other envoys
in the ritual of bowing at the Noon Gate.^([494]) And although they
could not leave the Guesthouse, they could at least visit with other
foreigners who were staying there. For example, the Joseon envoy Sin
Sang (申鏛) stopped by, and he sat down with Pires and had a long
conversation. Perhaps surprisingly, it seems that the two got along
quite well. Sang reported:

  They had one ambassador, and below him, over twenty people. I spoke
  with him. His heart is quite enlightened (開明)! I looked at his
  books: their form is similar to Buddhist proverbs in a Hangul-style
  script (諺文), and their artistry is incomparable.^([495])

Guests were delivered diverse meal packages, including jujubes,
chestnuts, rice, goose, mutton, and various greens.^([496]) But Sin Sang
noticed that Pires only ate two items: chicken and noodles. “Because
those are the only things that their land produces.”^([497])

The Letter Scene

Vieira wrote that the turning point of their time in Beijing was a
mysterious scene involving the embassy’s diplomatic letters.^([498]) He
is clearly confused by this incident. He says that the officials accused
the letters of being mistranslated, but he does not explain why this
made them so angry. Historians have thus interpreted this incident in
various ways.^([499]) The following section is an effort to reconstruct
this event and clarify the key issues.

As mentioned, Yang Tinghe had ordered investigations on the incoming
embassies. He needed to know why Portugal and Bintan had gone to war
against each other. The Portuguese envoys would be easy to interrogate,
since they were in Beijing. But the Bintan ambassadors were still in
Nanjing. Because of this, Tinghe ordered the Nanjing district
Investigating Censor, Qiu Daolong (丘道隆), to go speak with
them.^([500]) Daolong met with the Bintan envoys and wrote a report,
which must have arrived in Beijing around December 1520. Cristóvão
Vieira later heard rumors about this:

  This Tuam Mahamed stayed for a few days in Nanjing, and he colluded
  with the governor of the city, and with bribes, he got him to write
  all the information to the King that he, Tuam Mahamed, gave him about
  us.^([501])

The “governor of the city” is doubtless Daolong.

Meanwhile, another official was assigned to examine the Portuguese side
of the affair: the Investigating Censor He Ao (何鰲).^([502]) It seems
that the Minister of Rites Mao Cheng selected him for this case because
he was from the Guangdong coastal area (from Shunde County 順德縣), and
thus could speak the local dialect.^([503]) This was important, because
the interpreters of the Portuguese may have spoken a form of
Cantonese.^([504]) He Ao thoroughly examined the matter, reading through
all the memorials that the court had received concerning the
Portuguese.^([505])

The investigating team then met with the Portuguese embassy itself.
Vieira says that multiple “mandarins” conducted an
interrogation.^([506]) Two of them can be identified with some
probability. One was the director of the guesthouse, Liang Zhuo. This
was dictated by Ming law, which said that if a tribute embassy was
suspected of a major crime, the director should conduct the
investigation.^([507]) Moreover, some information from the interrogation
appears in Liang Zhuo’s biography.^([508]) Another interrogator was, of
course, He Ao. His 1521 report suggests that he met directly with the
Portuguese in Beijing, and had difficulties with them: “those who came
to the capital were fierce and reckless and vied for supremacy.”^([509])
However, in the following paragraphs, I will simply call them “the
interrogators,” since Vieira’s letter does not identify them.

Around late December 1520, the interrogators summoned the whole embassy
to the Imperial Palace.^([510]) Pires and Vieira, as well as Huozhe
Yasan and the Chinese interpreters, all appeared as ordered. The
interrogators then asked them various questions. They wanted to know
whether the interpreters had changed the wording of the Portuguese
documents. They also asked about the letter from the “governors” of
Guangdong.^([511]) But in the end, they made one basic accusation, an
accusation that is repeated across all the major sources: they alleged
that the Portuguese had falsely claimed to be tribute
ambassadors.^([512]) He Ao’s biography states the accusation in this
way:

  The Portuguese used giving tribute as a pretext (為名). […] They
  secretly came to the capital.^([513])

Liang Zhuo’s biography says:

  The Portuguese foreigners: nobody knows what sort of hometown they
  have. They pretended to come give tribute as a pretext (為名).^([514])

On the Portuguese side, Vieira’s lost letter says:

  It was decided among them in the council of the king that the embassy
  was false, and that Tomé Pires was coming to spy on the land.^([515])

Vieira’s extant letter says something similar:

  It seemed to all of them that we had deceitfully entered the land of
  China to see the land.^([516])

This was not a new accusation. We have seen that from the very
beginning, the Ming officials had suspected that the Portuguese were a
false embassy: the Foreign Trade Bureau at Nantou had suspected
it^([517]); Supreme Commander Chen Jin had directly accused them of
it^([518]); the Minister of Rites Mao Ji had ordered to reject them for
it.^([519])

This first accusation (of being a false embassy) naturally entailed a
second accusation. Vieira goes on to say:

  It seemed to all of them that we had deceitfully entered the land of
  China to see the land, that the difference between the letters [in
  which] was written the letter to the king [i.e. the emperor]: it was a
  case of deceit.^([520])

In other words, the second accusation was that the embassy carried
falsified documents. The letters were supposedly from the “King of
Melaka,” but in fact they were not. In order to understand this
accusation, it is helpful to examine a Ming memorial from 1564. In that
year, Portugal again tried to send a tribute embassy to the
Emperor.^([521]) The Guangdong Administration Commissioner Chen Xian
(陳暹) received their dossier, which included a letter from the
Portuguese King, Dom Sebastião. Xian had similar suspicions about
counterfeiting:

  Originally, the Melakan [Sultanate] was a tribute-giving country. […]
  If [the Portuguese] have been sent by that country’s king, then it is
  not appropriate to reject them. An examination of the Ming Law Code
  [says that] the previous emperor had originally granted them a seal
  and tally slips. But looking at the seal which [the Portuguese] are
  now submitting, it is only two and a half inches long, and uses wax to
  make an impression. It does not look like the seal that the previous
  emperor gave. And they have not given any tally slips. I have visited
  the archives to compare it. They are not from the Sultan of Melaka.
  There is no doubt about it!^([522])

In other words, Chen Xian thought that the piece was merely a poor
effort to imitate a letter from the Sultan of Melaka. He did not realize
that it was a legitimate diplomatic document in its own right, from a
completely different sovereign.

In 1521, He Ao probably felt the same confusion. The translated letter
that he held in his hands probably claimed to be from the “King of
Melaka.” After all, in Chapter 8, we saw that Fernão Peres de Andrade
had declared that Dom Manuel ruled over that city. However, when He Ao
looked at this letter, he knew that it could not have come from the true
king of Melaka—i.e. the Sultan. The paper material was different; the
seal was different, and it was covered with an indecipherable barbarian
script. Accordingly, Vieira says that the interrogators accused the
interpreters of falsification:

  The interpreters were asked why they had made a false letter, which
  did not conform to that of the king our lord.^([523])

At the end of the session, the interrogators asked the interpreters to
say their hometown—a standard piece of information that was asked of
criminals.^([524]) The ambassadors were bewildered and frightened by
this experience. Vieira later looked at it as the moment when the
hardships began.

The Embassy Is Restored to Normal Status

Perhaps surprisingly, the letter incident did not have serious
consequences. Neither the Portuguese ambassadors nor the Chinese
interpreters were seriously punished. Vieira mentions only a couple
small effects:

  The king ordered that we no longer come to his palace to give
  reverence and that we have guards over us.^([525])

Actually, these measures were the only punishments that the Ministry of
Rites had the power to impose. It had authority to organize the court
rituals (such as the bowing ceremony) and it maintained security at the
guesthouse. But it did not have the right to independently make
diplomatic decisions, especially when dealing with “friends” of the
Emperor, like the Portuguese.

In addition, the Ministry of Rites did not have the power to physically
harm the guests. This became clear on December 5, 1520, when the Emperor
finally arrived on the outskirts of Beijing, at the city of Tongzhou
(通州).^([526]) He did not immediately enter the capital, but stayed
there for several weeks. The officials in Beijing rushed out to pay
their respects to the returning Son of Heaven. It seems that the
interpreter Huozhe Yasan also went out to meet with Jiang Bin, who was
returning as well. But this violated the guesthouse’s “no exit” rules.

Displeased, Liang Zhuo found a minor protocol violation as a pretext to
punish the interpreter. When Huozhe Yasan returned to the guesthouse, he
did not bow to Zhuo. This was impolite, for foreigners were supposed to
honor the director whenever they entered the facility.^([527]) Zhuo’s
biography thus records the following story^([528]):

  One day, Huozhe Yasan rode a horse, following ten people back from the
  palace of the emperor.^([529]) He entered straight into the facility
  and did not do the bowing ritual. Liang Zhuo arrested him for
  questioning, and caned him. He said: "You are just a nameless lackey
  of the Emperor!" He got angrier, caned him again, and put him in the
  cangue. The foreigner [i.e. Huozhe Yasan] privately communicated with
  him, saying: "I can approach the face of the Emperor, but I cannot be
  impolite to the face of a guesthouse director?" [Liang Zhuo] sent him
  to the jailhouse.^([530])

This confrontation is relatively well documented. He Ao’s biography also
briefly mentions it: “They saw the Ministry but did not bow.”^([531])
The Judicial Commissioner, Gu Yingxiang, who perhaps knew Liang Zhuo
personally, also referred to the incident.^([532])

The fight between Liang Zhuo and Huozhe Yasan was a fight between the
institutional power of the Ministry of Rites and the
“super-institutional” power of the Emperor. Liang Zhuo had the legal
right to cane the interpreter, but Yasan possessed extra-legal rights
that exempted him from such treatment. In fact, at the end of this
incident, it was not Yasan who was punished, but rather Zhuo. His
biography continues:

  [Liang Zhuo] sent him to the jailhouse. But the Army bureau of the
  Forbidden City did not accept to take [Yasan] into custody. They
  brought him before the hallway of the Guesthouse and released him.
  They reported it to Jiang Bin. Jiang Bin was furious. He sent four
  lieutenants and over twenty foot soldiers to seize Liang Zhuo.^([533])

Zhuo was disgraced and lost his position as guesthouse director.

After that, it seems that the Portuguese were restored to normal status.
They were no longer banned from court rituals. This can be inferred from
the fact that they were present at the Emperor’s official entry ceremony
into Beijing, which happened on January 18, 1521.^([534]) Cristóvão
Vieira was apparently an eye-witness. For example, in his lost
letter, he offers the following detailed description:

  To one of the Tartars, with whom he was at war, and also to other
  neighboring kings whom he needed for his affairs—they were received
  with honor, with some of the principle lords going themselves where
  the king was, on the road, on the day of the entry, in the same way
  that it is done among us. And other embassies of kings and princes who
  had given him their obedience, or who were from distant parts, and of
  whom the king took little notice: he did not give them any
  recogition.^([535])

Interestingly, the “Tartar” in this passage is most likely Xieyi Huxian,
who had been officially adopted by the Emperor and thus could ride in
the Emperor’s train.^([536]) On the other hand, one can sense that when
Vieira complained that “other ambassadors from distant places” were
neglected by the Emperor, he is referring to himself and Pires, as well
as their friends, the Joseon ambassadors, who probably stood in the
designated location for foreigners, watching from afar.^([537])

All in all, the Portuguese had a strange status at court. In the eyes of
the Ministry of Rites, they were impostors. But due to the Emperor’s
favor, they were given full diplomatic honors. And officially, their
case had not even been processed yet! When the Joseon ambassadors
returned to their country, they complained about the chaos that they saw
at the Zhengde court. They commented on the status of the Portuguese:

  The Ministry of Rites argued that [the Portuguese] had dared to
  destroy a country which the court had enfeoffed, and should not be
  approved. The Ministry did not allow them to participate at court.
  Nonetheless, in their treatment at the guesthouse, there is no
  difference from other countries.^([538])

Yang Tinghe and Mao Cheng were frustrated with how the Portuguese–Bintan
dossier was progressing. They would soon try to make a direct appeal to
the Emperor.

12. The 1521 Report on the Portuguese


(1)


 

 



This chapter examines the most important document for Sino-European
relations of the entire Ming era: the memorial on the Portuguese embassy
of January 1521. This memorial became the foundational guideline for
Ming policy toward Portugal. For example, in 1554, during negotiations
to open the port of Macau, the Judicial Commissioner of Guangdong cited
it as a warning to Captain Leonel de Sousa.^([539]) In 1568, Father
Belchior Nunes Barreto also mentioned it as the precedent that still
impeded the Portuguese from entering China, fifty years later.^([540])
Furthermore, its principles would eventually be applied to the Spanish
and Dutch as well.

This chapter enters the debate over how to interpret this document. It
argues that the memorial was not primarily targeting the Portuguese.
Rather, it was an attack on Yang Tinghe’s factional rivals.

The Context

As mentioned in the previous chapter, in October 1520, Yang Tinghe
called for an investigation on the Portuguese embassy. By late December,
the Minister of Rites, Mao Cheng, had received initial reports from both
Qui Daolong and He Ao. On January 13, 1521, he thus went to Tongzhou to
present his findings to the court.

The Zhengde Emperor himself was probably absent. A month earlier, he was
enjoying himself, fishing on a lake, when he accidentally tumbled into
the cold waters. He never fully recovered from the shock.^([541]) He was
now weak and sickly, with a bloody cough. Furthermore, he was reportedly
worsening his condition by trying to drink his way back to
health.^([542]) Consequently, Mao Cheng probably only presented his
report to imperial representatives: the Grand Secretary Yang Tinghe, the
Directorate of Ceremonial Wei Bin (魏彬), and the Emperor’s favorite
Jiang Bin, etc.

He Ao Against the Supreme Commander

Mao Cheng’s “report” was in fact a compilation of smaller documents.
There were two general sections: (1) a dossier of letters from
Guangdong, with a commentary on them from the Investigating Censor He Ao
(2) the letter from the Sultan of Bintan, with a commentary on it from
the Investigating Censor Qiu Daolong.^([543])

In regard to the dossier of letters from Guangdong, Vieira says that it
contained about three pieces, spanning from Chen Jin’s original memorial
of 1517 up to a very recent report, written perhaps in December
1520.^([544]) He Ao had selected each letter carefully. He wanted to
transmit one unified message: that the Portuguese had caused the
Guangdong island trade system to become unprofitable. For example,
letter #2 complained about the Portuguese request to build a trade
factory—a clear disruption of the official trade system. And letter #3
reported that the Portuguese were impeding ships from other countries
from paying taxes. The same letter also described an incident in which
the Portuguese themselves refused to pay taxes:

  Another mandarin said that in the year of 1520, on the Island of
  Trade, the Portuguese struck his hat and gave him blows and seized
  him, when he went to collect the taxes under the orders of the
  mandarins of Guangdong.^([545])

This story is repeated multiple times across the letters, creating an
“echo chamber effect.”^([546]) Clearly, He Ao wanted to highlight the
financial chaos that the Portuguese had caused.

Ao’s compilation was not a representative sample of all the letters that
had been written about the Portuguese. Vieira’s lost letter mentions
that Tomé Pires had also brought a positive letter from
Guangdong—perhaps from the Foreign Trade Eunuch.^([547]) Similarly,
Fernão Peres de Andrade had said that every official in Guangzhou who
had met the Portuguese had written a letter in 1517, and that “some said
good about us, others bad, and others neither good nor bad.”^([548]) But
only the bad letters appear in Ao’s compilation.

The Censor concluded with policy suggestions. First, he directly
denounced the Guangdong officials themselves, for they had set up the
island trade system:

  Recently, because of Executive Officer Wu Tingju’s arguments that he
  lacked spices to send to Beijing and that the army command lacked
  revenue for army salary, foreign goods have been immediately taxed
  without regard for tribute year. The result is that foreign ships
  constantly are in our harbors, and foreigners pile up in our cities.
  Law and order have become more neglected, while the roads are becoming
  more frequented. The Portuguese took advantage of this to push their
  way in.^([549])

For Ao, the true guilty party is Wu Tingju. As for the Portuguese, they
simply “took advantage” of the opportunity that Tingju had offered to
them.

Accordingly, He Ao’s policy recommendations focused less on the
Portuguese specifically, and more on the legal framework of Guangdong
commerce:

  Please examine and restore the old system. Completely drive away the
  foreign ships in the harbors and the foreigners who secretly live
  there. Ban private trade, strictly keep defense.^([550])

Ao was returning to the old struggle that the Minister of Rites Liu Chun
had started in 1515. He wanted to abolish the Guangdong island trade
system, reinstate the Maritime Ban, and restore “the old system,” i.e.
the tribute system that the Hongwu Emperor had established at the
founding of the dynasty.

Qiu Daolong Against the Ministry of War

In his report, Qiu Daolong first presented the letter from the Sultan of
Bintan, and then gave his analysis of it. His own political objectives
can be perceived in comparing the two sections. In the Sultan's letter,
the culprit was clearly the Portuguese themselves. Vieira summarizes the
letter as follows:

  The Malays said that the ambassador from the king of Portugal who was
  in the country of China, that he was not coming truthfully, that he
  had falsely come to the country of China in order to deceive, and that
  we were going around scouting the land, and since we had placed a
  stone and had a house, we would have the land as our own, that we had
  done so in Melaka and in other places, that we were bandits.^([551])

In other words, the Bintan envoys were giving a warning to the Zhengde
Emperor. The Portuguese had initially appeared harmless when they first
came to their city, but afterward they invaded it. In the same way, the
Emperor should beware: the Portuguese might try to conquer China as
well.

In contrast, in his commentary, Qiu Daolong takes a slightly different
perspective:

  Melaka [i.e. Bintan] is a country which offers tribute and which has
  been Imperially enfeoffed. The Portuguese have annexed it and,
  enticing us with gain, are seeking enfeoffment and rewards.
  Righteousness will certainly not allow this.^([552])

Daolong agrees that the Portuguese should be rejected, but his reasons
are different. He does not consider them to be a military threat. On the
contrary, he says that they are submitting themselves to the Ming
(asking for enfeoffment) and even offering certain unnamed “gains.” He
more portrays them as corrupt minions than as powerful invaders.

Qiu Daolong is not concerned with national security, but rather with
tribute policy. He argues that, because the Portuguese have unjustly
occupied another vassal, they cannot be given a Ming seal. That is to
say, he wants the court to enforce the tribute denial policy:

  It is requested that their tribute be refused, that the difference
  between according and disobedience be clearly made known and that they
  be advised that only after they have returned the territory of Melaka
  will they be allowed to come to Court to offer tribute.^([553])

It would seem that Qiu Daolong is implicitly criticizing the Minister of
War Wang Qiong. Just as the Ministry of Rites was trying to stop Qiong
from allowing Turfan to give tribute, so also was it trying to stop him
from allowing Portugal to do so.

The Outcome of the Report

Perhaps for a couple hours, the imperial council sat and listened to the
Minister of Rites as he explained each of these documents. At the end of
his long exposition, Mao Cheng arrived at his conclusion. He urged the
court to follow the recommendations of He Ao and Qiu Daolong. “Daolong
was previously a magistrate in Shunde [in Guangdong province], and Ao is
from Shunde. Thus, they have a good knowledge of the
situation!”.^([554])

But the Minister’s hopes were frustrated. Cristóvão Vieira recorded the
immediate response of the court:

  The king responded that “these people do not know our customs, but
  they will continue to learn them.” He said that his judgement would be
  put off till after entering the city of Beijing. With this response
  that the king gave, the big officials were not very happy.^([555])

Although Vieira writes that the king (i.e. the Emperor) excused the
Portuguese on the basis of their ignorance, this should not be taken
literally. As mentioned in Chapter 8, this statement probably came from
Yang Tinghe himself, acting as the imperial representative. The fact of
the matter was simply that the Emperor was not present. Consequently, no
decision was made: “his judgement would be put off till after entering
the city of Beijing.”

In this situation, Mao Cheng could only make limited policy proposals.
First, he recommended waiting till the Bintan embassy arrived, and then
conducting further investigations into why the Portuguese “invaded
neighbouring countries and brought great disturbance to the
area.”^([556]) Second, he recommended having the Ministry of Revenue
“examine the precedents and remove [Wu Tingju].”^([557]) Finally, he
asked that the island trade system be terminated:

  In the future, the prohibition must be tightly enforced. […] [foreign]
  ships which come at a time other than the appropriate tribute year are
  to be chased far away, and are not to be subject to the proportional
  levy.^([558])

While at first glance, these proposals might seem weighty, a deeper
examination shows their limits. As mentioned, the Mao Cheng did not have
the authority to singlehandedly reject the Portuguese embassy.
Accordingly, he could only call for “further investigations.” Similarly,
he did not have the authority to dismiss Wu Tingju, who belonged to the
Ministry of Revenue. Accordingly, he could only ask his colleague in
Revenue to look into the matter. (In fact, Tingju was not dismissed on
this occasion.) Finally, he did not have the de facto authority to
reinstate the Maritime Ban. This could only be done by the Supreme
Commander of Guangdong-Guangxi, who usually disregarded such
orders.^([559]) In short, these proposals seem to be little more than
wishful aspirations.

To conclude, this chapter has tried to give a different understanding of
the 1521 memorial. It should be interpreted within the context of Ming
court factionalism. Yang Tinghe and Mao Cheng were seeking to use the
Portuguese dossier to strike at their political rivals. However, they
did not have the power to do so, at least not for the moment.
