13. The Rise of Yang Tinghe and the Fall of the Embassy, 1521


(1)


 

 



Yang Tinghe, Interim Ruler of China

On January 18, 1521, the Zhengde Emperor entered Beijing.^([560]) The
next day, he performed his most important duty: officiating the
sacred annual ritual, the Jiaosi (郊祀). But as he prostrated before the
great altar, he suddenly convulsed and coughed up blood. The ceremony
was interrupted and he was rushed back to the Leopard House. For three
months, he lay sick. Finally, on the night of April 19, 2021, he
summoned two eunuchs and gave them his final words. In regard to the
government, he simply said:

  Whatever happens, report to her ladyship [empress dowager Zhang] that
  she should work out pressing matters of state with the grand
  secretaries.^([561])

He died soon after, bringing an end to one of the most unusual reigns of
the Ming dynasty.

Yang Tinghe had been waiting for this moment.^([562]) He had already
gained the trust of the empress dowager Zhang.^([563]) He had also
negotiated with the palace eunuchs and the high nobles who commanded the
armies in Beijing. Furthermore, he had consolidated his hold over the
Secretariat, promoting his two allies, Mao Ji and Jiang Mian.^([564])
Thus, the next morning, he immediately issued an announcement—a
testament (遺旨)—in the name of the deceased Emperor.^([565]) Not
surprisingly, it declared that Yang Tinghe would lead the government
till a new emperor arrived.^([566])

Thus, from April 21, when the Zhengde Emperor died, till May 27, when
the Jiajing Emperor ascended the throne, Tinghe ruled the Middle
Kingdom. A tide of change now rolled across the empire. Previously, the
grand secretary had been powerless against his rivals, subjected to the
whims of the supreme commanders, the eunuchs, the Minister of War, and
the Emperor’s favorites, like Jiang Bin. Now, the roles were reversed.
After decades of patient waiting, Tinghe could at last restore the
Secretariat to its former glory.

Punishing the Emperor’s Favorites

The Grand Secretary first dealt with Jiang Bin. Even in his initial
announcement, he had already begun setting the conditions to arrest him.
It ordered all of Bin’s personal troops to go home. It also excluded
him from the plan for defending Beijing’s city gates, re-assigning his
troops to other commanders.^([567])

On April 24, three days after the Emperor died, Yang Tinghe and Empress
Dowager Zhang issued orders to arrest Jiang Bin.^([568]) That very day,
he was caught in an ambush when he came to perform a ceremony in the
palace, and was immediately executed.^([569]) Officially, he was accused
of trying to overthrow the government. Still, scholars have rightly
raised doubts about this. His power had come from the Zhengde Emperor,
and after the Emperor died, he was largely isolated. Historian Tian Shu
has suggested that the Grand Secretary simply needed a pretext to
tighten security during this sensitive moment.^([570])

Next, Yang Tinghe sent away the various friends and guests whom the
Emperor had gathered in the Leopard House. The imperial testament
already said:

  The foreign priests of the Leopard House and the priests of Shaolin
  Temple, and the craftsmen, sailors, and temple managers; the horses
  and racing boats from Nanjing, all such non-regulation items should be
  sent away.^([571])

Three months later, when the Jiajing Emperor pronounced his enthronement
speech (also written by Yang Tinghe), he called for further
punishments.^([572]) Any soldiers, Buddhist monks, or entertainers who
had been adopted by the Zhengde Emperor were to be stripped of the
imperial name.^([573]) Such illegitimate priests were to be dismissed
from their positions and thrown into prison, or into forced army
service.^([574]) Muslim clerics who had been living in the Leopard House
were to be arrested as well.^([575])

In addition, the Emperor’s testament also expelled the embassies of
Turfan, Hami, and Portugal:

  Orders for the foreigners who had originally been sent to give tribute
  by Hami and Turfan: […] The government ministries are each to follow
  the original precedent for reward-giving: [the foreigners] are to be
  given a reward, and then an escort should accompany them to return
  [home]. […] As to the Portuguese foreigners, send an escort to
  accompany them back to Guangdong.^([576])

Vieira’s extant letter also mentions these orders:

  The king [i.e. emperor] entered into Beijing and was sick for three
  months. He died. The next day, [they said] that we would come back to
  Guangdong with the present [i.e. the tribute].^([577])

Doubtless confused and worried, Tomé Pires and Vieira departed from the
guesthouse, and slowly made their way back toward Guangzhou.^([578])

At this point, their interpreter Huozhe Yasan was arrested and executed.
He was no longer treated as a foreign guest, but rather as a rebel who
had collluded with barbarians. Vieira wrote:

  In Beijing, the interpreters were arrested and killed, and their
  servants were given as slaves to the official, for being slaves of
  traitors. […] The four [interpreters] were decapitated in Beijing for
  having gone outside of the land [i.e. violating the Maritime Ban] and
  bringing Portuguese into the land of China.^([579])

Another person who was arrested at this time was Xieyi Huxian, the Hami
envoy.^([580]) He was thrown in jail, and about a year later, he and his
sons were executed.^([581]) Furthermore, his nephew and son-in-law were
beheaded, and their families enslaved.^([582]) Liang Zhuo’s biography
describes the executions of Huozhe Yasan and Xieyi Huxian together as a
pair:

  Huozhe Yasan had already been put in jail. […] At that point, along
  with Xieyi Huxian, both were executed.^([583])

Restoring Friends

Yang Tinghe did not forget his friends. First of all, he restored Liu
Chun, the former Minister of Rites. Even before the Emperor died, on
February 13, 1521, he called his old colleague back to Beijing to
support the Secretariat.^([584]) Similarly, Liang Zhuo was
rehabilitated. He had been demoted after his conflict with Yasan, but
his biography says that a month after the Emperor died, on May 25, he
was rewarded: “The [Jiajing] emperor ordered to kindly grant twenty
taels of silver to Liang Zhuo […] Zhuo received a promotion of one
rank.”^([585])

In June 1521, an investigating censor bemoaned the many officials who
had been demoted by Wang Qiong, people such as Supreme Commander Peng Ze
and the Investigating Censor Gao Gongshao.^([586]) Accordingly, these
officials were also given back their dignities and presented to the
Ministry of Personnel for re-appointment. Gongshao was soon returned to
the position of censor.^([587]) Ze was also restored, as will be seen
below.

Punishing the Ministry of War

Yang Tinghe took revenge against his rival Wang Qiong, who was now
serving as Minister of Personnel.^([588]) Even before the Emperor died,
Tinghe had begun to exclude him. He called meetings but did not invite
Qiong to them. The Minister reportedly appeared at one such event and
cried out: “How is it that I, one of the nine great officials, have not
been informed beforehand of this little gathering?” But nobody
responded.^([589])

After the Jiajing Emperor was enthroned, the investigating censors began
to accuse Qiong.^([590]) For example, one denounced him for corruption,
saying: “he wantonly sold positions through his flattery of the
[Zhengde] Emperor.”^([591]) The court ordered the Censorate to arrest
and investigate him.^([592]) Qiong protested, accusing Yang Tinghe of
abusing his position: “he holds the strings of power, singlehandedly
taking over many affairs.”^([593]) But his complaints were in vain. He
was sentenced to death, though this was later lightened to exile on the
frontier.^([594])

In June 1521, Yang Tinghe appointed a new Minister of War: the former
Supreme Commander of Gansu, Peng Ze.^([595]) This was an unusually high
promotion, and Ze recognized it, immediately offering his resignation.
But Tinghe (speaking in the name of the Emperor) urged him to accept it:

  Because Peng Ze is a veteran official who has a distinguished record
  both at court and in the provinces, the Emperor grants this important
  position in the Ministry of War. Resignation refused.^([596])

With Peng Ze as its leader, the Ministry of War now abandoned its
hard-nosed pragmatism, and adopted Tinghe’s favored tribute denial
policy. Ze first applied the policy to Turfan, cutting off the
negotiations that Wang Qiong had started, saying:

  If we allow them to give tribute, I fear that their repentance will
  not be deep and that good relations will struggle to last
  long.^([597])

He thus sent away the various Turfan diplomats.^([598])

Ze also applied the policy to Portugal. In the summer of 1521, the
Ministry of Rites calmly declared: “Melaka [i.e. Bintan] asks for aid.
The best is to transfer this request to the Ministry of War for
deliberation.”^([599]) Ze examined the case, and agreed with his friend
Mao Cheng:

  I ask that the emperor order Portugal to be condemned, and order them
  to return the lands of Melaka.^([600])

The court thus issued instructions to Pires’ embassy. This letter is not
found in the Chinese sources. It is only recorded in the lost letter of
Cristóvão Vieira:

  [The Emperor] considered it good that […] our ambassador return to
  Guangdong with the present that he brought, and that the governors [of
  Guangdong] keep him in custody while letters go to our captain who was
  in Melaka, and to [the captain] who was in India, and also to the
  king, [commanding] that they give up Melaka to the king [i.e. Sultan]
  whom they had driven out of there.^([601])

In other words, Ze employed the classic tribute denial policy: he
ordered the ambassadors—Pires and Vieira—to be imprisoned and held as
hostages until Portugal returned the land that it had stolen.^([602])
These orders were given to the ambassadors while they were still
traveling toward Guangdong. Vieira was thus allowed to write a long
letter to the Portuguese fleet, explaining the situation and
transmitting the court’s orders. He sent off this letter around early
July, 1521.^([603])

Thus, after several years of permissive tribute policy under Wang Qiong,
the court returned to the firmer tradition forged by Ma Wensheng.

Punishing the Guangdong Administration

Yang Tinghe also punished the Guangdong regional administration. First
of all, on May 20, 1521, the court inspectors invited former Supreme
Commander Chen Jin to voluntarily offer his resignation. He did so, and
the Secretariat allowed him to honorably withdraw from politics.^([604])
For his part, former Administration Commissioner Wu Tingju wanted to
fight for his position. He preemptively appealed to the Jiajing Emperor,
denouncing himself for his previous mistakes and offering to
retire.^([605]) He doubtless hoped that the Son of Heaven would deny the
request and reaffirm support for him. But the Emperor did not show
favor, and Tingju was demoted to Nanjing.^([606])

The mid-level and lower officials of the Guangdong coastline were also
punished for tolerating criminality. In August 1521, Minister of War
Peng Ze declared:

  If the officials who are engaged in maritime patrols and those engaged
  in defence against the Japanese pirates do not swiftly memorialize
  when they hear of changes in the situations of the [barbarians], they
  should be arrested and prosecuted.^([607])

A couple weeks later, the Guangdong Antipiracy Admiral (備倭都指揮同知),
Lu Ying (盧英), as well as another local military officer, were
denounced for corruption and arrested.^([608]) It seems that even the
Foreign Trade Bureau in the fortress of Nantou was shut down, and moved
further down the coastline to Dianbai (電白).^([609]) The only coastal
official who escaped unscathed was the Coastal Judicial Administration
Admiral (海道按察司副使) Wang Hong, whom we will see again in the next
chapter.

After thirteen years of frustration, Yang Tinghe at last restored the
Maritime Ban in Guangdong. He issued an imperial decree on June 13,
1521:

  From now on, foreigners who come to give tribute must have confirmed
  tally slips and come within their tribute period. If all is according
  to regulations, then they can be taxed. If there are wicked private
  ships who are not coming to give tribute, or if they come to give
  tribute outside of their tribute period, […] then they all should be
  rejected and sent back.^([610])

Furthermore, on July 18, the court issued a further amendment in order
to close potential loopholes^([611]):

  All tribute-giving foreign countries have their proper [tribute]
  period. Recently, they have increasingly violated [the period], or
  when they arrive here, they immediately stop at a town or city, or
  relay station, and make much trouble. It is recommended that the
  restrictions and laws be made clear: that when they come, they cannot
  surpass the time limit.^([612])

That is to say, the Court tried to stop foreign ships from tarrying on
the islands—they should be driven away immediately. This would prevent
them from illegally trading.

Finally, Yang Tinghe pursued the Guangdong eunuchs. In 1522, the Grand
Defender Fu Lun and the Foreign Trade Eunuch Niu Rong were both
denounced for corruption.^([613]) The investigating censors demanded
that the two eunuchs be disciplined. But the Jiajing Emperor spurned
such calls, confiscating the smuggled goods, but not imposing any
punishment.^([614]) In early 1523, the censors tried again to denounce
the two eunuchs^([615]):

  Fu Lun and Niu Rong have been in the town [i.e. Guangzhou] for many
  years. Their wishes have already been fulfilled. Furthermore, Niu Rong
  has previously been denounced and has received a warning.^([616])

The Court Records say that Yang Tinghe supported these
propositions.^([617]) However, the Emperor again intervened, pardoning
the eunuchs, and even praising them. “Be diligent in handling matters,
as in the past,” he told them.^([618]) One can infer that he also was
using their services, and wished to protect them.^([619])

In conclusion, we have seen that after the Zhengde Emperor’s death, Yang
Tinghe radically changed the government. He struck down his rivals and
appointed his friends; he tore down irregular policies and reinstated a
traditionalist regime. “The sage rulership of Yao and Shun is seen once
more today!” he proudly declared.^([620]) In this tidal shift, the
Portuguese embassy was swept away.

14. The Battle of Tamão


(1)


 

 



The Guangdong Administration’s Five-Year Ban on Trade

When the Emperor died in April 1521, the Guangdong administration was
dominated by people who were inimical to Yang Tinghe, people such as the
Supreme Commander Xiao Chong (蕭翀), an ally of Wang Qiong,^([621]) as
well as the Foreign Trade Eunuch Niu Rong and the Grand Defender Fu Lun,
who have been discussed in previous chapters. Although the Ministry of
Rites had officially ordered Xiao Chong to restore the Maritime Ban, it
is safe to assume that he had disregarded those orders. During this
time, there was a rebellion in Guangxi, and the island trade system
helped pay for his army expenses.^([622])

By May, news had arrived that the Zhengde Emperor had died, and that
Yang Tinghe had temporarily taken control of the empire. This placed the
Guangdong officials in an awkward situation. They did not know what the
new regime expected of them. Would they be pardoned? Would they be
punished? This situation perhaps explains the strange decision that they
made. They sent a messenger to the current captain major of the
Portuguese ships, Diogo Calvo, and told him the following:

  Due to a rule of China [which says] that when the king dies, no
  foreigner can stay in the country, not even in a port, under penalty
  of death, when the news came [of the king’s death], Diogo Calvo and
  the others were asked to leave from there.^([623])

The mourning period was reportedly for five years. In a letter from
1527, Diogo Calvo wrote that “the king [i.e. emperor] ordered them to
tell me that no merchandise could come until five years had
passed.”^([624])

It seems that the officials told this not only to the Portuguese, but
also to other foreign merchants along the coast. For example, in the
aforementioned letter, Calvo seems to suggest that the Siamese were
being invited back after five years also.^([625]) Similarly, in
1528—that is to say, a little over five years after the events—Jorge
Cabral, captain of Melaka, reported that the officials also wished to
receive the ships from the Bintan Sultanate again.^([626]) In other
words, it seems likely that the entire Pearl River delta was being
cleared of foreigners for five years to mourn the Zhengde Emperor’s
passing.

But it was a lie. The story of a five-year mourning period was
fabricated by the Guangdong officials. It certainly did not come from
the Ministry of Rites: Mao Cheng wanted to abolish the island trade
system permanently, not just for five years. It is true that the court
did ask for a pause on tribute visits to Beijing during the mourning
period. On May 8, 1521, it was decreed:

  The ancient rule is that when foreigners come to court, they all
  receive a banquet. Recently, due to national mourning, that rule is
  temporarily suspended. Just order border officials to host them in
  their halls. Until the twelfth month of this year, tribute givers must
  be treated in this way.^([627])

In examining this decree, it is clear that the Guangdong officials were
going far beyond the court’s orders. The decree does not say that
foreigners cannot visit China, only that they cannot visit Beijing: the
court was just ordering provincial officials to host foreign embassies
and give them a banquet locally. In addition, the decree was only valid
till the end of the year, not for five years.

No source says why the Guangdong administration spread this false
information. Perhaps the most likely explanation is that it wished to
temporarily suspend foreign trade during this sensitive transition
period. It wanted to give the appearance of making foreigners depart, as
if it were restoring the Maritime Ban. That way, the Supreme Commander
could reassure Yang Tinghe of his obedience, and meanwhile, he could
observe the new Emperor. If all was peaceful, then after five years,
foreign merchants could be invited to return.

The Battle Begins

Diogo Calvo’s discussions with the Guangdong officials must have been
amicable. In 1527, he was confident that the Chinese would welcome him
back to the trade islands:

  A relative of mine who was in the kingdom of Siam told me that the
  Chinese were desirous of receiving from us pepper and black wood and
  putchuck and frankincense and ivory and saffron, and that they would
  now give everything by weight and for money.^([628])

Calvo did not think that he was considered a persona non grata in China.
This suggests that when they asked him to leave, he must have consented
and voluntarily left. Indeed, Vieira’s extant letter says that prior to
the battle, Calvo “departed” before the other ships.^([629])

However, while Calvo himself was compliant, the other merchants were
not. Even as captain major, he could not convince them to follow his
example. The truth was that, although he was officially the leader of
the fleet, he had little real control over the others. This was a
typical situation in the eastern reaches of the Portuguese Empire, where
the private merchants did their business quite independently.^([630])
Furthermore, Calvo lacked legitimacy. He was not a fidalgo, and not an
officer. He was just a trade agent of a high nobleman, Dom Nuno Manuel,
serving as the temporary captain major until the official leaders,
Rafael Catanho and Rafael Perestrello, arrived.^([631]) Thus, when Calvo
told the other Portuguese at Tamão to leave for five years, they scoffed
at him. “They did not want to do it because they had not finished
trading off their merchandise,” wrote Barros.^([632]) Consequently,
Calvo’s lone ship departed earlier than the others.

The Guangdong officials observed the Portuguese ship movements and made
a strategic decision. First, they would let the Calvo’s official ship
depart. Then afterward, they would arrest the remaining
merchants.^([633]) In theory, this would not be a battle against a
tribute fleet, but rather against criminals who were tarrying on the
trade islands in violation of the Maritime Ban. Vieira wrote that after
the battle, the captured sailors “were strangled, carrying plaques that
said that they would die as bandits of the sea.”^([634]) The Ming Court
Records also confirm that interpretation. The first report on this
event, recorded in 1522, does not describe it is a battle against the
Portuguese, but rather against a local Chinese smuggler:

  The Guangdong bandit Fang Gantong and others had travelled across the
  oceans and had been in communication with the [foreigners]. They had
  also plundered and looted the people and had become a very powerful
  force. The Provincial Surveillance Commissioner Wang Hong had
  previously served as coastal defence circuit vice commissioner and had
  led troops in capturing them.^([635])

The event recorded here is certainly the 1521 Battle of Tamão, for the
date is the same as that of the battle, and the commanding Ming officer,
Wang Hong, is the same as well.^([636]) The pirate who was captured,
Fang Gantong, is probably the close Chinese ally of the Portuguese
called the “Cheilata” (mentioned in Chapter 7).^([637])

Wang Hong doubtless intended the battle to be quick and decisive. No
source says exactly how he initiated it, but one can infer that he made
a surprise attack. As mentioned in Chapter 7, he sometimes sent agents
aboard the Portuguese ships disguised as merchants—presumably, he could
plant undercover soldiers there as well. And indeed, he immediately
captured four out of the five ships that were docked at Tamão.^([638])
By most standards, his ruse was a success.

However, one ship did escape the admiral’s grasp: that of Jorge Alvares,
who managed to pull away to sea.^([639]) Furthermore, when Diogo Calvo
realized what was happening, he changed course, and returned back to
Tamão, rescuing the fleeing sailors who had jumped into the
waters.^([640]) Wang Hong now had to directly confront two Portuguese
ships and their formidable cannonry. He gathered his own fleet and
prepared for a blockade.

The Shift to a Hard-Line Strategy

At about this same time, the trade policy of the Guangdong
administration radically shifted. They not only stopped the island trade
system, but also the official tribute trade system. The editorial
comments on the Ming Court Records, which were made around 1523, mention
this:

  Because of the Portuguese, all of the foreigners and barbarians who
  were supposed to come to bring tribute were blocked and the ships and
  goods did not arrive.^([641])

In his 1529 memorial, Supreme Commander Lin Fu also complained of this:

  Previously, the Portuguese person Huozhe Yasan and others were
  executed and the Guangdong authorities barred the ships coming from
  Annam, Melaka, as well as the various other foreigners.^([642])

The mention of Annam being cut off is particularly noteworthy, since
Annam had no relation with the Portuguese and was not involved in the
1521 battle. The Guangdong administration apparently refused to trade
with any foreigner, without exception.

It is possible that the regional officials were reacting to emergency
court orders. Vieira’s extant letter describes this:

  Afterwards, the King ordered Guangdong to tear down the fortress that
  the Portuguese had made, and also the population, [saying that] he did
  not want trade with any nation, and that if anyone should he come,
  that he should be ordered to return.^([643])

The statement that the Emperor “did not want trade with any nation”
would seem to explain Guangdong’s strict policy. Vieira does not say
when these orders were issued, though presumably it must have been
immediately after the Zhengde Emperor passed away and Tomé Pires was
sent back to Guangdong—i.e. April 1521.

In April 1521, such extreme measures might have been comprehensible.
However, by June 1521, the Jiajing Emperor sat securely on the throne
and the court had reauthorized tribute trade.^([644]) At that point, the
hard-line stance of the Guangdong administration began to appear
inappropriate. Surprisingly, the officials persisted in the complete ban
on all foreign exchange until 1529.^([645])

On a military level, the Guangdong administration became more aggressive
as well. Again, they adopted a strategy that was harsher than that of
the court. The Ministry of Rites had simply asked the navy to drive away
foreigners. In contrast, the Guangdong navy tried to capture the
Portuguese ships and kill the sailors. Captain Duarte Coelho, who
participated in the battle, described this:

  [The Ming] formed an armada with many sails which almost kept [the
  Portuguese ships] encircled, after having attacked them a few times in
  the port where they were, not daring to engage with them
  directly…^([646])

Clearly, the Ming navy did not want to drive the Portuguese away. On the
contrary, it tried to trap them in the harbor and prevent them from
escaping.

Furthermore, the Foreign Trade Bureau itself began to quietly intervene
in the conflict. When﻿ other merchant ships arrived and asked to trade,
the bureau apparently arrested them. Vieira writes:

  The Portuguese, as I said—some of them were taken deceitfully by
  force, and came to Nantou. And also, due to false letters, the people
  came on land and were taken, because they came scattered: one day one
  and tomorrow another.^([647])

Vieira claims that over fifteen hundred people lost their lives.^([648])
While these numbers are doubtless exaggerated, the general brutality of
the campaign is not. Wang Hong’s own biography boasted that “he
completely defeated them, without a single survivor.”^([649])

There were multiple reasons for the shift toward a more aggressive
strategy. First of all, during this sensitive time, the regional
officials did not want to cause scandals. They knew that the battle
risked drawing the ire of the court. Accordingly, Vieira writes that
they wished to keep it secret:

  The mandarins said that these might escape who were well-known
  persons, that these Siamese would write letters against the mandarins
  regarding the goods that they had taken from them, and that it would
  fare ill with the mandarins; that it would be better to put an end to
  the whole matter, so that nothing should be known of such a
  thing.^([650])

This cautious attitude was not unusual. It also appears, for example, in
the chronicle of the Joseon official Choe Bu, written about thirty years
earlier. He drifted to the Zhejiang coastline in 1488, shortly after the
Chenghua Emperor had passed away, and he wrote of the tension that this
situation caused. One navy officer asked him not to complain to the
court about the pirates who had attacked him:

  The emperor is now newly enthroned. Laws are strictly observed. If the
  emperor were to see the testimony that you submitted earlier, he would
  necessarily say that banditry is rampant, and blame would fall on us
  border commanders.^([651])

A second reason for this more aggressive strategy was that the Guangdong
administration wished to appease Yang Tinghe. The battle lasted for
nearly four months: from May till September 1521. Over the course of
that time, the officials steadily received news about the situation in
Beijing. They doubtless heard that Tinghe had gained control of the new
government and was purging his rivals. They heard that both the former
Supreme Commander Chen Jin and the current Supreme Commander Xiao Chong
had both been denounced.^([652]) They doubtless feared for their own
careers. Accordingly, they tried to show their utmost loyalty, enacting
the strictest possible policies.^([653])

A last reason for the aggressive policy was corruption. This was
certainly Cristóvão Vieira’s preferred explanation. Commenting on the
decision to enforce the Maritime Ban, he said: “the mandarins of
Guangdong only did this in order to steal.”^([654]) Similarly, in regard
to the decision to execute the Portuguese sailors, he wrote:

  [Due to the] rich goods that these junks had, the mandarins ordered
  that no Portuguese should escape, such that in any way they would have
  to give account for these goods that they had stolen.^([655])

Vieira’s suspicions were shared by some Chinese as well. In Wang Hong’s
gazetteer biography, there is a curious effort to emphasize his honest
handling of captured goods:

  [Wang Hong] fought and conquered [the Portuguese]. He completely
  submitted all the riches that he confiscated. He did not keep even the
  tiniest morsel for himself.^([656])

In most Ming battle commemorations, it is enough to simply celebrate the
victory. The strong insistence on Hong’s integrity, the explicit
declaration that he did not engage in corruption, would seem to imply
that there were rumors circulating.

The End of the Battle

The Portuguese sources contain two accounts of how the Battle of Tamão
ended. The more famous story comes from the chronicle of João de Barros.
He follows the testimony of Captain Duarte Coelho, writing:

  Because this attack of the enemy was on the 8th of September of 1521,
  which was the feast of the birth of our Lady, [Coelho] recommended to
  all that they take her name, because with her name, he hoped that she
  would save them. And just as she is accustomed to help those who call
  on her in such necessities, she helped with a storm, which was for us
  at the stern. And for the enemies, it caused some of them to be
  scattered and lost: with which Duarte Coelho and his companions came
  to Melaka at the end of October of 1521.^([657])

Most historians have accepted this explanation for the end of the
battle. They dismiss Coelho’s religious overtones, but accept the
general idea that the Portuguese fleet was saved by a sudden
typhoon.^([658])

I propose an alternative explanation. As noted in the previous chapter,
around July 1521, the Ministry of War issued orders to the Guangdong
officials, commanding them to tell the King of Portugal that the
Portuguese ambassadors would be held hostage until the stolen lands were
returned to the Sultan of Bintan. Cristóvão Vieira thus wrote a letter
to the Portuguese fleet, informing them of the orders, and sent it off
around July 1521.^([659]) These documents—i.e. the Ministry of War’s
orders along with Vieira’s letter—must have arrived in Guangzhou by
August. Upon receiving them, the administration changed its strategy. It
no longer tried to capture Diogo Calvo’s ship. Instead, it sent a
messenger out to Tamão, who delivered Vieira’s letter to Calvo, and
asked him to transmit it to the King of Portugal. With that done, the
Ming fleet allowed Calvo to depart, on September 8, 1521.

This explanation is indirectly supported by the second Portuguese
account of how the battle ended, which is less famous. It is found in
the other two major Renaissance chronicles: those of Fernão Lopes de
Castanheda and Gaspar Correa. Correa’s version is quoted here:

  [Diogo Calvo] was so weak in defending the ship that if it were not
  for the Portuguese who went to it, the ship would have been taken by
  the Chinese. But the men who gathered to it fought in such a way that
  they defended it, and even wanted to fight with the Chinese, but the
  captain did not wish to put himself at such risk, so he came back to
  Melaka.^([660])

In this telling of the story, a cautious Diogo Calvo gave the order to
depart, against the wishes of the sailors. (Duarte Coelho was perhaps
one of these unwilling ones.) There is no mention of the Chinese fleet
blocking their passage, nor of any miraculous storm. This would also
explain why, as mentioned earlier, Calvo had felt that his negotiations
with the Guangdong officials had been amicable and successful. Whereas
the sailors had wished to fight, he had chosen to be diplomatic. And
perhaps the Ming messenger reciprocated the good will. Just maybe, after
officially handing the letter to Calvo, he also unofficially whispered
the invitation to return after five years.

Arriving in Melaka, Diogo Calvo must have declared that he had a
message for the King. The Renaissance historian Fernão Lopes de
Castanheda describes how the Captain of Melaka, Garcia de Sá,
reacted^([661]):

  Our people escaped from this danger and gathered together on the way
  to Melaka, where they arrived at the end of October 1521. And they
  gave news of the uprising of China. And an investigation was conducted
  on this, which was sealed and carried to the King of Portugal, in
  which were clarified some reasons for this uprising, which, like I
  said, I could not find out, and for this I do not tell about
  them.^([662])

In other words, Sá conducted an investigation into why the Chinese had
become angry at the Portuguese. He then took his report and Vieira’s
letter, sealed them, and sent them directly to Lisbon for the King to
read. These two documents were truly kept secret, for they are no longer
extant today, and neither Castanheda nor Correa was able to read them.
However, we know that they arrived safely at the court, for João de
Barros had both of them in his hands, and they form two chapters of his
famous Decadas.^([663])

In Portugal, a new king was sitting on the throne: João III, son of
Manuel I. He did not actively reach out to the Emperor of China. It
seems that he preferred to wait. In 1526, when he did finally give
orders about the Middle Kingdom, he took a pragmatic approach. He chose
not to send another embassy, but simply entrusted the matter to the
Captain of Melaka, asking him to find ways to restore relations.^([664])
One can speculate that this caution must have been a reaction to the
secret documents that he had received. After all, Vieira’s letter had
made it clear that if the king wanted official relations with China, it
would come at a price that he was unwilling to pay: he had to return
Melaka to the Sultan. He thus backed away from direct diplomacy.

15. Conclusion


(1)


 

 



This book has sought to clarify why the 1517 Portuguese embassy was
rejected. In the introduction chapter, three common explanations were
presented. Here in the conclusion, let us now return to those three and
reexamine them.

First of all, one dominant explanation says that the embassy failed due
to unbridgeable cultural differences: the Portuguese and the Chinese had
never encountered each other before, and consequently, they constantly
misunderstood each other’s intentions. In this book, that idea has found
some support, but only to a limited extent. Certainly, misunderstandings
did occur, but they were by no means insurmountable obstacles. There
were many middlemen who facilitated communication, people such as the
Melakan trade partners who brought the Portuguese to Tamão, or the
Chinese interpreters, led by Huozhe Yasan, who handled negotiations.
Furthermore, such misunderstandings were by no means unique to the
Portuguese. We have seen that even age-old neighbors such as Annam,
Turfan, and Siam had similar problems in dealing with the Ming Empire.

Fundamentally, many Ming officials were fairly pragmatic, and willing to
overlook such intercultural squabbles. The Supreme Commander Chen Jin
could accept foreign criminality on the coastline as long as he received
his tax revenue, and the Minister of War Wang Qiong could even tolerate
small-scale invasions if it would spare his armies from unnecessary
expeditures. Some officials championed benevolence and mercy. Yang
Tinghe himself ultimately pardoned the Portuguese for most of their
crimes on the basis that “they do not know Chinese customs, but will
learn them.” There was perhaps more flexibility in the system than
historians have realized.

This book thus participates in on-going efforts to better understand
intercultural contact in the Early Modern Era, efforts championed by
historians such as Sanjay Subrahmanyam.^([665]) Cross-cultural
interactions were complex and unpredictable, and cannot be reduced to a
binary of friendship or enmity. Sometimes, miscommunication meant
conflict, but other times it did not. In this book, the person who
perhaps most profoundly misunderstood other cultures was also the person
who most passionately enjoyed them—the Zhengde Emperor himself.

A second explanation says that the core factor behind the embassy’s
failure was Western imperialism and colonialism: the Portuguese occupied
Chinese land and disregarded the law, forcing the Ming government to
counter-attack. While this book does not at all condone the Portuguese
behavior, it does seek to contextualize it in a different manner. We
have seen that they heavily depended on local collaborators: Malay
sailors, Guangdong bandits, lower-level government officials. Their
crimes often blended with those of their partners. In other words, the
Portuguese actions were partially colonial in nature, but not fully. For
example, when they purchased children and sold illegal firearms, in some
ways, they were practicing European forms of violence, but in other
ways, they were also adopting Asian practices—illegal activities that
had begun haunting the Chinese coastline long before they arrived.

Even in cases when the Portuguese behaved in frankly imperialistic
fashion, we have seen that the Ming officials generally did not see it
as such. For example, the Portuguese certainly did conquer the Sultanate
of Melaka and then boasted of it to the Ming Court. But the Ministry of
Rites did not view this as colonialism, but rather as another sign of
the decay of the tribute system—it was a quarrel among vassal states,
not unlike that betweeen Turfan and Hami, or Annam and Champa.
Similarly, the Portuguese efforts to get trade access were not viewed as
free-market capitalism or globalization, but rather as symptoms of the
ailing Maritime Ban. The Ming court interpreted the world using their
own set of mental concepts.

This book thus seeks a more complex definition of “colonialism.” Early
Modern Asia was a multi-polar world. As scholars such as Serge Gruzinski
have noted, the sixteenth-century South China Sea was culturally
hybrid.^([666]) At this point in history, European influence was
relatively weak in the region, and colonialism was not an overriding
historical force—at least not yet. At this point, it was but one factor
among others, and those “other factors” must also be given appropriate
consideration.

Finally, a third explanation says that the embassy was rejected due to
Chinese isolationism: the Ming Empire was too inward-turned, unable to
accept the outside world. This book has argued against that view. While
the Maritime Ban and the tribute system did officially remain in the law
books, they were not strictly enforced. At nearly every level of
government, exceptions have been noted—from the small coastal officials
in Nantou, to the regional officials in Guangdong, to court officials
such as Wang Qiong, up to the Zhengde Emperor himself. It is true that
Yang Tinghe was to some extent an isolationist. But he only represented
one faction of the court, and he only held power for a short time. Three
years after the events of this book, in 1524, he was disgraced.^([667])
By 1529, the island trade system had been reinstated, and the Portuguese
merchants were returning to Guangdong. Eventually, one of those islands
would even become a permanent settlement—the port of Macau.

This book thus joins in the on-going reconsideration of Ming foreign
policy. It adds further support for the arguments of scholars such as Li
Qingxin, Cheng Wing-sheung, Nakajima Gakushō, and Oka Mihoko.^([668])
The empire had a strange relationship with the outside world, one which
mixed an intense xenophobia with a touch of xenophilia. This paradoxical
situation has not yet been explored fully, and certainly merits more
research.

In addition to the three explanations above, this book has also proposed
another reason for the failure of the embassy: it argues that the
embassy was rejected as part of Yang Tinghe’s efforts to reform the Ming
political system. The founder of the dynasty, the Hongwu Emperor, had
created an unstable governmental structure. This caused a perpetual
struggle among the different branches of government, with no clear
procedure for decision-making. In 1521, Yang Tinghe tried to reassert
the priority of the Secretariat. In the process, he struck down his
rivals and their clients, who included the Portuguese ambassadors.

This explanation has been built in opposition to the “traditional
narrative,” outlined in the Introduction. While the traditional account
adopts the perspective of the Portuguese captains, this book consciously
has taken that of the Ming officials, and especiallythat of the Grand
Secretary. This is not to say that he understood the situation better
than the Portuguese. But when we see the world through his eyes, we can
better understand both why Sino-Portuguese relations had to fail in
1521, and also why they were so easily revived a few decades later. In
that sense, this approach might be compared to studies such as James
Polachek’s Inner Opium War, books which also try to reconsider the
Chinese view, to see history from the other side of the
equation.^([669])

The overarching metanarratives of Sino-European relations—such as
colonialism, isolationalism, and culture clash—are not sufficient to
explain the events of this book. Such big concepts must be balanced with
microhistory. A proper perspective must also take into account the
smaller details, such as the frustrations of the grand secretaries, the
extravagance of the Zhengde Emperor, the cunning of Huozhe Yasan, etc.
As we fit these various pieces into the puzzle, perhaps we can break out
of overly rigid interpretive models, and begin to develop new
stories—stories that perhaps can give a deeper, subtler, and ultimately
more human picture of the Early Modern global community.^([670])
