16. Appendix: Philological Notes


(1)


 

 



Part 1: The Lost Letter of Fernão Peres de Andrade

Chapter 7 often cites a letter of Fernão Peres de Andrade. However, this
letter is no longer extant. The following section thus explains how this
book has reconstructed its contents.

João de Barros mentions the letter in his Da Ásia. In January 1518,
Andrade decided not to immediately return to Melaka, but rather to stay
in China for one more year. He thus sent out Duarte Coelho to notify the
captain of Melaka of his situation. Barros writes:

  Fernão Peres dispatched Duarte Coelho, since he was already fully
  prepared, to take news to Melaka about how the ambassador whom he had
  brought had been received, and how he had secured peace with the
  governors of Guangdong, and how our affairs had been very well
  received in those regions. That Duarte Coelho […] arrived in Melaka at
  the end of March, 1518.^([671])

This passage in fact gives a short summary of the letter. It described
(1) how Tomé Pires had successfully been accepted in Guangzhou (2) how
Andrade had successfully negotiated with the Guangdong administration
(3) how Sino-Portuguese trade had begun smoothly.

Based upon that summary, it can be inferred that the three great
Renaissance historians—João de Barros, Gaspar de Correia, and Fernão
Lopes de Castanheda—all had access to this letter, for all three tell
that same story, and all three tell it from the same perspective—that of
Andrade himself. It is also worth noting that all three historians had
direct access to the letter. That is to say, the similarities between
them cannot be explained by saying that they were copying each other.
Each author occasionally gives unique details that cannot be found in
the other two. For example, Andrade wrote that after arriving in
Guangzhou, the Administration Commissioner (buzhengshi) ordered him not
to leave his ship. Here is how each author treats that topic:

- Castanheda: “The captain major did not allow any Chinese person to
  enter our ships, nor any of our people to go on land, and he ordered
  any refreshments that he wanted to be bought from boats that were on
  the river. Nor did he allow any junk of those who entered afterwards
  to dock together with our ships. And he ordered this to be told to the
  Puchanci.”^([672])

- Correa: “Fernão Peres did not allow any of our people to go out onto
  land, and he ordered whatever he needed to be bought by Chinese slaves
  from boats that were on the river, which had everything for sale. Nor
  did he allow our ships to let enter anyone from the land.”^([673])

- Barros: “The Puchancii […] ordered it to be told to him that […] if he
  had need of anything in the meantime, that he [i.e. the Puchancii]
  would very happily provide it.”^([674])

Each of the three quotations contains unique details which seem too
precise to simply be fabricated. They must be copying different parts of
Andrade’s letter.

In regard to the ending of the letter, it must have stopped at January
1518, when Duarte Coelho departed from Tamão. The last section must have
described the Ryukyu islands (Lequios). All three historians wrote that
just before Andrade sent Coelho to Melaka, he sent another of his
captains, Jorge Mascarenhas, northward to search for those
islands.^([675]) This “Ryukyu-focused” ending can be confirmed from a
letter of Simão de Andrade, written in August 1518. Simão wrote that he
had arrived in Melaka, and that he had found a ship that had returned
from China (doubtless Coelho’s). The ship brought news that his brother
Fernão Peres had stayed in Guangdong for another monsoon, and also
brought news of a rich place called “Lequios.”^([676])

In summary, although Fernão Peres de Andrade’s original letter is no
longer extant, there are numerous sources that give a clear sense of its
contents.

Part 2: Cristóvão Vieira’s Inaccurate Calendar

Cristóvão Vieira was a member of Tomé Pires’ embassy. After suffering
the Ming court’s rejection, he was imprisoned, and he lived in captivity
for many years.^([677]) His letters give what appear to be
precise dates. However, when checked against the Ming Court Records, it
is clear that the dates are incorrect.

- Vieira says that “the king arrived at a village that is two leagues
  from the city of Beijing in January of the era of 1521.”^([678]) But
  the Ming Court Records say that the Zhengde Emperor arrived in
  Tongzhou on December 5, 1520.^([679])

- Vieira says that “in February, the King entered Beijing.”^([680]) But
  the Ming Court Records say that the Zhengde Emperor entered Beijing on
  January 18, 1521.^([681])

- Vieira says that “[the emperor] died. The next day, [they said] that
  we would go to Guangdong with the present […]. We left Beijing on the
  22 of May.”^([682]) The Ming Court Records agree that orders were
  given to send away the Portuguese the day after the Emperor died. The
  date of those orders was April 20, 1521.^([683])

From the above, it can be seen that Vieira’s calendar is roughly one
month ahead of the correct date.

It is not clear why his calendar became so inaccurate. After leaving
Guangzhou, the embassy no longer had contact with other Europeans. One
would thus expect his dates to increasingly lag behind the real date, as
he occasionally forgot to mark days on the calendar, and the missed days
slowly added up. But that is not what happened. His calendar did not
fall behind, but rather jumped forward. By December 1520 (i.e. less than
a year after he had left Guangzhou), his calendar was already one month
ahead of the true date. And then after that, his calendar seems to have
stabilized, continuing to maintain the one-month inaccuracy for
subsequent events.

How can this be explained? It is possible that Vieira was haphazardly
guessing at the date, and that he always over-guessed. But that seems
unlikely, since he invariably missed the real date by the same interval:
roughly one month. In my opinion, the most likely explanation is that he
was trying to estimate the Julian calendar date based upon the Chinese
lunar calendar date. He knew that the Chinese date was ahead of the
Western one, but he was not sure by how much. It should be noted that
when Tomé Pires and his team first arrived in Guangzhou in October 1517,
the lunar calendar was just two weeks ahead of the Julian one. But at
the end of that year, the Chinese calendar added a supplementary “leap
month 閏月,” which created a much wider gap. It seems that Vieira never
recovered from the ensuing confusion.

In any case, caution is needed in using the dates that Vieira gives in
his letter. In this book, I assume that his dates are one month ahead.

Part 3: The Lost Letter of Cristóvão Vieira

Vieira is particularly important for this book because he wrote two
letters from prison in which he recounted his experiences in the
embassy. However, there are textual problems with them. Today, only the
second letter is extant; the first letter has been lost.^([684]) The
goal of this section is thus to reconstruct that lost letter. More
specifically, I argue that João de Barros had it in his hands, and that
one of his book chapters (Decada 3, Livro 5, Capitulo 1)
follows it closely.

We only know that the first letter existed because the second letter
mentions it:

  In the year 1520, on the 23^(rd) day of January, we left to [see] the
  king of China. In May, we were with the king in Nanjing. From there,
  he ordered us to go to the city of Beijing ahead of him, to give us
  the judgement there. On August 7, it was written to Guangdong about
  what had happened with the king up till then. The letter came to Jorge
  Botelho [and] Diogo Calvo, who was at the island where commerce is
  done. For that reason, I do not return to write about it, because the
  times require brevity and little reading.^([685])

This passage, although brief, gives considerable information about the
lost first letter. The key points are as follows**:

1.  (1)

    The letter began in January 1520 with Tomé Pires departing from
    Guangzhou to see the Zhengde Emperor.

     

2.  (2)

    The letter ended on August 7, 1521, when Vieira sent out the letter.

     

3.  (3)

    The letter extensively discussed “what had happened with the king”
    in Nanjing and Beijing prior to August 7. This is in contrast to the
    second letter—the extant letter—which intentionally abbreviated
    those matters, focusing instead on later events in Guangzhou.

     

What remains now is to show that Barros’ Decada 3, Livro 5, Capitulo 1
matches with those three points.

The first point (that the letter began in January 1520) is fairly simple
to prove. Barros’ chapter begins at that precise time:

  At the time they [i.e. Simão de Andrade and the other ships] arrived,
  Tomé Pires had still not yet departed. […] [He] departed in January
  1520, which was after the arrival of Simão de Andrade.^([686])

In both the lost first letter and Barros’ chapter, the starting date is
the same: January 1520. And the opening event is the same: Pires’
departure from Guangzhou.

Next, let us consider the second point: that the first letter ended on
August 7, 1521. Demonstrating this point is somewhat more difficult,
because while Barros’ summary of the first letter says the month and day
when it was sent (August 7), it does not say the year. The intuitive
interpretation would be that the letter was written on August 7, 1520.
However, this interpretation has a chronological problem: Vieira writes
that Diogo Calvo received the letter, but in August 1520, Calvo had not
yet arrived in China. From the summer of 1519 till roughly December
1520, it was Simão de Andrade who was captain major.^([687]) He would
have received the letter, and doubtless would have carried it back to
India, without Calvo ever seeing it.^([688]) Thus, for the 1520 dating
to be true, one must assume that Vieira wrote the name “Diogo Calvo” by
mistake. However, this is a strange mistake, since Vieira was sitting in
captivity alongside Diogo’s brother—Vasco Calvo, who would certainly
have known if his older brother had received such a letter or not. In
short, the intuitive interpretation—that Vieira’s lost letter was
written on August 7, 1520—is problematic.

Most likely, the letter was written on August 7, 1521. In that case,
Vieira would be correct in writing that Diogo Calvo received it, for
Calvo arrived in China around May 1521. What was the situation in August
1521? Given Vieira’s inaccurate calendar (see Part 2, above), “August 7”
in fact is probably closer to early July. The Zhengde Emperor had died
on April 20, 1521, and the following day, the embassy was dismissed from
the capital. Vieira’s second letter says that the embassy had a long
voyage, only arriving in Guangdong on September 22, 1521.^([689]) In
other words, Vieira probably wrote the letter while he was still on the
road from Beijing to Guangzhou.

Barros closes his chapter at that same time. He does not give a specific
date, but the events match. Namely, Decada 3, Livro 5, Capitulo 1 ends
with a description of how the Zhengde Emperor died, and how the “new
emperor”—that is to say, the Jiajing Emperor—issued orders to the
Portuguese embassy. The Jiajing Emperor was enthroned on May 27, 1521.
That means that both the lost letter and Barros’ chapter end at the same
time: during the summer of 1521.

The third point to prove is that the first letter focused on events in
Beijing, prior to August 1521. This is in contrast to the second letter,
which focused on events in Guangzhou, from September 1521 onward.

This brings us to a key challenge in analyzing Decada 3, Livro 5,
Capitulo 1: namely, that Barros not only had the lost first letter in
his hands, but also the extant second letter. He says so himself,
speaking of:

  …Two letters that our people received two or three years ago from
  these two men: Vasco Calvo, brother of Diogo Calvo, & Cristóvão
  Vieira, who were prisoners in Guangdong.^([690])

These are clearly references to the 1534 and 1536 letters from the two
captives—the extant letters. Accordingly, it is often assumed that the
second letter was the unique source for Barros’ chapter. The first
letter goes unnoticed, hidden in the shadows.

However, a careful comparison between Barros’ chapter, on the one hand,
with Vieira’s extant second letter, on the other, shows that clearly,
the former cannot be uniquely based on the latter. Indeed, when dealing
with events prior to August 1521, Barros' chapter is in fact more
detailed than Vieira's letter. For example, they both describe how the
Zhengde Emperor executed the Prince of Ning before entering Beijing.
Vieira’s extant letter describes this very briefly:

  The king arrived at a town that is two leagues from the city of
  Beijing in January of 1521. He was judging a relative of his who had
  revolted against him, and he ordered him to be burnt after being
  strangled.^([691])

Barros’ chapter describes the same event, but using more precise
details:

  Already in January of the following year of 1521, the king came, and
  before he entered the city, he held back in a place two leagues from
  there, to judge the affair of a relative of his, who had stirred up a
  province, revolting against him. And he was condemned to die in this
  way: first he was strangled under the proclamation of banditry, saying
  that he had revolted with other bandits to rob the land, and
  afterwards burned under the proclamation of treason, because this
  crime is punished with fire, so that no memory of the bones of the
  guilty remains on the earth.^([692])

In comparing the two documents, it is clear that they came from the same
person, for they tell the same story, often with the same vocabulary.
However, Barros’ chapter is much more detailed than Vieira’s letter. He
cannot simply be copying it. 

In fact, Barros’ chapter contains many passages that are unique,
passages that have no equivalent in Vieira’s extant letter. For example:

- The itinerary of the embassy’s voyage from Guangzhou to
  Nanjing.^([693])

- The descriptions of the three letters that Tomé Pires carried with him
  to present to the Emperor in Beijing (from Dom Manuel, from Fernão
  Peres de Andrade, and from the “governors” of Guangdong).^([694])

- The orders that the “new emperor” issued concerning the rejected
  Portuguese embassy.^([695])

Let us note that all of this unique material happened before August
1521.

Finally, if Barros were following Vieira’s second letter, then we would
expect his chapter to focus more on events in Guangzhou, after September
1521. But Decada 3, Livro 5, Capitulo 1 does not describe the embassy’s
later travails. Rather, it is the following chapter (Capitulo 2) which
mentions them, though only briefly.^([696])

As a final piece of evidence, let us note an interesting passage from
Vieira’s extant second letter in which he describes the embassy’s
transportation in Spring 1520:

  [The emperor] ordered us to be given the best boats that there are in
  the land of China […] with trade goods. He ordered us [to be
  transported] honorably, as I have already said.^([697]) (Emphasis
  added)

Vieira here mentions off-handedly that he had already described the
luxurious transportation, and apparently in even greater detail. But his
second letter contains no such description at all. Vieira is presumably
referring to his first letter.

Now, Barros’ chapter contains precisely such a description of the
embassy’s fine ships:

  …three ships with oars in the style of light ships (fustas) set up in
  our style, with flags and a silk canopy […] For just the honor of this
  kingdom, it carried the flags with the arms and the motto [of the
  kingdom].^([698])

This would seem to be the passage to which Vieira's second letter was
referring. It would seem to be evidence that Barros had the lost first
letter in his hands.

Part 4: The Captain of Melaka’s Lost Report on the Battle of Tamão

This book often speaks of a report that the captain of Melaka prepared
after the Battle of Tamão in 1521. This document is no longer extant,
but João de Barros had it and used it as his primary source for one of
his chapters (Decada 3, Livro 5, Capitulo 2). The following is the
evidence for believing this.

It is important to first establish the historical context. As discussed
in Chapter 14, after retreating from China, Diogo Calvo arrived back in
Melaka in October 1521. He handed Cristóvão Vieira’s lost letter to the
captain of the fortress, Garcia de Sá.^([699]) At that time, Sá was
preparing to finish his first term as captain, and was trying to clear
his name of any blemishes. For example, there is an extant report from
June 1522,^([700]) in which he justified himself from accusations made
by the previous Captain of Melaka, Afonso Lopes da Costa, who claimed
that Sá had stolen the position from him.^([701])

When Sá looked at Vieira’s letter, he must have worried. The letter
conveyed the Ming Emperor’s grievances against Portugal, making numerous
complaints about the merchants at Tamão.^([702]) Such accusations could
potentially harm Sá’s reputation, for they implied that he had failed to
control the casados, and that he had mishandled the precious China
trade. He certainly did not wish to be held responsible for such a
failure. Indeed, Dom Manuel had already taken note of the rich profits
coming from the Middle Kingdom.^([703])

It was in this context that Garcia de Sá prepared a report on the causes
of the Battle of Tamão. Castanheda describes this report:

  Our people escaped from this danger and gathered together on the way
  to Melaka, where they arrived at the end of October, 1521. And they
  gave news of the uprising of China. And an investigation was conducted
  on this, which was sealed and carried to the King of Portugal, in
  which were clarified some reasons for this uprising, which, like I
  said, I could not find out, and for this I do not tell about
  them.^([704])

What did the report say? It seems that Garcia de Sá’s goal was to reply
to the angry accusations of the Jiajing Emperor. At the end of Decada 3,
Livro 6, Capitulo 1, Barros gives a summary of the Emperor’s orders (as
described in Chapter 13). After finishing this summary, Barros then
switches to what seems to be the opening of Sá’s report:

  In order to see if the judgement of this new king was just or not, in
  regards to what it said about us, in this second chapter, we will
  write some of the things on which we have information as to what we
  did in the port of Tamão, [things] which are true.^([705])

That is to say, Sá wished to reply to the Emperor’s anti-Portuguese
accusations. This is apparently the meaning of Castanheda’s statement
that the report was meant to “clarify some reasons for the uprising.”
Clearly, the report had a rhetorical goal: to clear Sá of any possible
guilt.

Accordingly, the report (as summarized in Decada 3, Livro 6, Cap. 2) was
careful in distributing blame. It did not blame the casados of Melaka,
who were supporting Sá, nor did it blame Diogo Calvo, whom Sá had
dispatched to China. It pinpointed just one guilty party: Simão de
Andrade. The report (as summarized by Barros) contained a list of crimes
that the impetuous captain major had committed in Guangdong, crimes
which had angered the Chinese:

- He executed a sailor without consulting the Ming officials.^([706])

- He made a fortress “like someone who wanted to take possession of the
  land.”^([707])

- He impeded other ships from trading.^([708])

- He purchased Chinese children from “honorable” families.^([709])

That list of crimes matches with many of the accusations that the Ming
court had made against the Portuguese merchants:

- The Portuguese “had a fortress made of stone, covered with tiles and
  surrounded with artillery, and within, many weapons.”^([710])

- The Portuguese “took taxes from the Siamese and seized them and
  enslaved their junks and put guards in them and did not let them do
  trading.”^([711])

- The Portuguese “stole children and ate them roasted.”^([712])

The similarity between the two lists of accusations seems too close to
be coincidental. However, a key difference is that the Ming officials
denounced the Portuguese in general, whereas Sá’s report denounced
Andrade specifically. The report was meant to deflect blame away from
the casados of Melaka and away from Garcia de Sá, in order to place it
instead on a disliked fidalgo who had already returned to India. It
should also be noted that Andrade had been dispatched to China by the
previous Captain of Melaka, Afonso Lopes da Costa, who was Sá’s
enemy.^([713])

After discussing Andrade’s crimes, Barros’ chapter then describes the
1521 Battle of Tamão. This probably also follows the structure of Garcia
de Sá’s report. Barros’ narration consists of different testimonies from
casados of Melaka—much like the style of Sá’s aforementioned extant 1522
report. The chapter primarily gives the testimonies of Duarte Coelho and
Ambrósio do Rego, two Melakan captains.^([714]) It also pays special
hommage to the deceased Jorge Alvares, who had been another outstanding
member of the casado community.^([715])

Interestingly, Barros’ chapter lacks the perspective of Diogo Calvo
himself. As mentioned in Chapter 14, Calvo had a more positive view of
the Chinese officials, and a more negative view of his own Portuguese
peers. It seems that the enmity was mutual, and that the casados
disliked him as well. This can be perceived in the battle accounts of
Castanheda and Correa. For example, Castanheda wrote:

  In the defense of the ship, [Calvo] did it so weakly that when the
  Chinese came against him, [the ship] would have been taken, were it
  not for our people from the junks, who gathered to [his ship] and
  defended it valiantly.^([716])

That is to say, the successful defense of the ship is not credited to
Calvo, but rather to the casados. This criticism is in contrast to the
praise which Vasco Calvo made of his brother’s courageous fighting
ability:

  My brother was at this port encircled for three months […] without
  them being able to enter [his ship], because he showed them his
  teeth!^([717])

Presumably, following the advice of the casados, Garcia de Sá had
convinced Calvo to keep quiet and not give his own personal perspective
on the causes of the battle. Six years after the events, in 1527, Calvo
wrote another letter to the king in which he complained of being
silenced:

  Since giving Your highness the observations that you might by means of
  them question me on the affairs of China, regarding the uprising in
  the country, several persons knew of it, for which reason they begged
  me that if your Highness questioned me as to the riches of the country
  I should not declare all, and should hold my tongue, because if I lost
  the chance of favor with your highness they would do me a good
  turn.^([718])

Calvo does not want to say who these people are, but he implies that
they are traitors who coerced him to hide the truth from the king. They
are people who wish to keep the riches of China for themselves. They are
probably the casados of Melaka.

In short, Barros’ Decada 3, Livro 6, Cap 2 would seem to be a summary of
the Captain of Melaka’s report. As a form of negative proof, one can
note that Castanheda and Correa, who by their own admission did not have
access to the report, do not have any information on Simão de Andrade’s
crimes either.

Part 5: The Lost Letters of Tomé Pires

This book sometimes mentions lost letters from Tomé Pires. The following
explains what is known about them.

Actually, Pires must have written many letters to the fleet in
Guangdong, but only two of them can be guessed at with any certainty.
The first letter is mentioned by João de Barros. He says that after the
embassy arrived at the river pass over the Nanling mountains, presumably
at the city of Shaoguan (韶关), they wrote back to the fleet:

  One of those mountain passes is where Tomé Pires was taken. […] From
  which port Tomé Pires wrote to Simão de Andrade: how he had arrived
  there safely and that he considered the city of Guangzhou to be a
  small thing in respect to others that he had seen.^([719])

It seems unlikely that Barros had the original letter in his hands, for
his summary of it is extremely brief and laconic. Most likely, Barros
took this passage from Cristóvão Vieira’s first letter—the lost
letter.^([720]) This seems to indicate that Vieira knew the contents of
Pires’ letters, and that the two envoys perhaps coordinated with each
other, taking turns writing.

The second letter is mentioned by Martim Afonso de Melo.^([721]) Melo
met Simão de Andrade in Cochin in November 1521, and Andrade told him
about the embassy's situation. Melo gives what seems to be a summary of
a letter from Pires:

  He departed from there [i.e. Guangzhou] with his cavalries and
  authority, which he thought to be more than any man had ever had,
  going to the king of the Chinese. And it is certain that he spent over
  three months on the road, without any delay there other than the
  distance of the road […] He was very well received by the king and all
  his people, and he was given very unspeakable honor. […] Thus, he was
  waiting for his judgement, which might already be done, and his return
  also.^([722])

Tomé Pires clearly wrote this letter after he met with the Zhengde
Emperor in Nanjing, and before he met arrived in Beijing, where he
expected to receive a judgment. That would mean that the letter was
written around September 1520.

If Pires and Vieira did indeed take turns writing, then it would explain
one of the curious silences in Vieira’s letters: namely, that he never
wrote a detailed description of the experience in Nanjing. He mentions
it in a fragment from the second letter, but only briefly.^([723])
Perhaps he knew that Pires had already written about those events, and
so he felt no need to repeat.

Why were Pires’ two letters lost? The circumstances hint at one main
reason: both of them were received by Simão de Andrade. Andrade
apparently kept the letters with himself, for he was able to show the
second one to Melo. But that very fact means that he did not send them
to Lisbon, where they could be archived. Perhaps he considered them to
be his own personal letters rather than official correspondence. Or
perhaps he intended to personally deliver them to the king when he
returned to Portugal. But it seems that he died in India, for there is
no record of him ever returning. And thus the letters were lost.

Part 6: Liang Zhuo’s Biographies

The biography of Liang Zhuo (梁焯傳) is one of the most important
sources on the Portuguese embassy. However, its textual history is
complicated, and the version of it that is used in this book is little
known. The following thus gives more information about this key
document.

There are two manuscript branches of the biography. The better known
branch first appeared in the county-level gazetteer Nanhai
Xianzhi.^([724]) Afterward it was compiled into the province-level
gazetteer, the Jiajing-era Guangdong Tongzhi.^([725]) And from there, it
was recopied into various later works, such as the the Shuyu
zhouzilu,^([726]) the Mingshancang,^([727]) the Tianxia junguo libing
shu,^([728]) and the Mingshi.^([729]) Paul Pelliot famously noted that
many of these works make errors in copying the original,
causing confusion among historians.^([730]) This biography is brief and
concise, about 550 characters in length.

The other branch of the biography derives from a Liang Family Genealogy
(梁氏家谱). I have consulted the 1885 Foshan edition, which has
generously been digitized by the Foshan Museum.^([731]) This version is
much longer: roughly around 7000 characters in length. It contains an
inscription from a stone monument which was made posthumously for Liang
Zhuo in 1527^([732]) (but which is now lost), as well as copies of his
imperial promotion letters.^([733]) The inscription is clearly based
upon autobiographical materials which Zhuo himself must have written,
for it includes lively personal details, such as a story about how he
miraculously prayed for rain to stop,^([734]) and another story about
how he silenced noisy spirits in a Buddhist temple who were bothering
him while he tried to study, etc.^([735])

The genealogy biography had very little diffusion. The only later source
which copied it was the Foshan Zhongyi Xianzhi (佛山忠義鄉志), which
contains an abridged version.^([736]) Because of that, it is little
known to scholars, and has been almost completely neglected up to this
point.

There are many textual differences between the genealogy branch and the
Nanhai Xianzhi branch. They both recognizably tell the story of the same
Liang Zhuo. However, they each have different anecdotes about him.
Because of this, both versions are cited in this book, as appropriate.
When possible, the genealogy version is preferred, since it contains far
more details.

Part 7: The Mystery of Huozhe Yasan

The identity of Huozhe Yasan (火者亞三) has been a matter of debate for
almost a century.^([737]) Jin Guoping has proposed that he was a second
ambassador within the embassy, alongside Tomé Pires.^([738]) Geoff Wade
has suggested that he was the former naval admiral of the Sultanate of
Melaka.^([739]) Tien-Tse Chang argued that he was none other than Pires
himself.^([740]) Perhaps most daringly, Paul Pelliot argued that he did
not exist at all—that he was simply the product of a copyist’s
error.^([741]) In this book, I support the most mundane view, which says
that he was simply one of the embassy’s Chinese interpreters. This
section argues for that position.

There are many reasons to believe that Huozhe Yasan was just an
interpreter. First of all, in his extant letter, Cristóvão Vieira lists
the names of the eight official envoys of the Portuguese embassy, and he
does not mention the name “Yasan.”^([742]) Vieira also mentions that
there were five Chinese interpreters, but he does not give their
names.^([743]) It is reasonable to think that Huozhe Yasan was one of
these five.

Furthermore, Vieira reports the ultimate fate of the interpreters:

  The interpreters in Beijing were arrested and killed, and their
  servants were given as slaves to the officials […]. The chief
  interpreter died of sickness.^([744])

This matches with what Liang Zhuo’s biography says about the demise of
Huozhe Yasan: namely, that after the death of the Zhengde Emperor:

  Huozhe Yasan was then jailed. He said that he was originally a Chinese
  man, who served the foreign barbarians. […] Then, along with Xieyi
  Huxian, both were executed.^([745])

Given the above, the natural conclusion is that Yasan was simply an
interpreter.

However, there are many counterarguments against this idea. Let us
examine each of them in turn. First of all, there are some sources that
describe Yasan as the leader of the embassy^([746])—significant sources
such as the Shuyu zhouzilu,^([747]) the Mingshancang,^([748]) the
Tianxia junguo libing shu,^([749]) and the Mingshi.^([750]) However,
those documents are not reliable on this point. It must be noted that
they are all following one common source: the 1529 memorial of Supreme
Commander Lin Fu (林富). In that memorial, Fu wrote:

  The investigating Censor Qiu Daolong memorialized to immediately have
  the Grand Coordinator and Judicial Commission order the Naval officer
  to drive [the Portuguese] out of the borders and execute their leader
  Huozhe Yasan.^([751])

Where did Lin Fu get this information from? He never met Yasan himself.
Fu was not in Guangzhou in 1517–1521, nor in Nanjing or Beijing in 1520.
He was in Chuzhou (處州, today, Lishui 麗水) during that entire
time.^([752]) In fact, his statement contains many errors. He claims
that Qiu Daolong asked the navy to drive away the Portuguese, but that
is not true. Daolong only asked the court to reject their
tribute.^([753]) It was the Minister of War, Peng Ze, who gave orders to
the navy in August 1521.^([754]) Furthermore, neither Qiu Daolong nor
Peng Ze gave the order to execute Huozhe Yasan. As mentioned above, this
happened immediately after the Zhengde Emperor died, in April 1521. In
short, Lin Fu probably wrote those words casually, without checking his
facts.

Indeed, a more knowledgeable witness even corrected Lin Fu’s mistake.
This was Wang Hong, who fought against Diogo Calvo in 1521, and who
perhaps saw Yasan in person while he was serving as Surveillance
Commissioner in Guangdong.^([755]) In 1529, he wrote a response to Fu’s
memorial. He quoted it, and in doing so, he made a small but significant
alteration:

  After that, they investigated the rebellious foreigners, and executed
  their evil leaders, Jiabidanmo (加必丹末), Huozhe Yasan, etc, in order
  to stop the border trouble.^([756])

In contrast to Lin Fu, who called Yasan the leader, Wang Hong demoted
Yasan to second position. Instead, he says that the leader was
Jiabidanmo—that is to say, Tomé Pires.^([757])

The biography of Liang Zhuo is the most important source on Huozhe
Yasan. This work also recognizes that Jiabidanmo was the leader of the
embassy. It says:

  The envoy Jiabidanmo and thirty personnel reached the capital.^([758])

When the biography mentions Huozhe Yasan, it does not call him an
ambassador. Interestingly, it simply calls him by his name, without a
title. It gives one hint as to his status: when Liang Zhuo punished
Yasan with caning, he also yelled at him, calling him an “imperial
courtier without any name (無名姓天上人).”^([759]) The gist of the
insult is that Yasan did not deserve the favors that he was receiving
from the Son of Heaven: he neither came from a noble family, nor did he
have any formal office. This seems to confirm that he did not hold the
official position of ambassador. It also suggests that he was from a
lower social class.

Another counterargument against the idea that Yasan was simply an
interpreter is that some sources say that he was in fact an agent
pretending to be an envoy from Melaka.^([760]) This statement comes from
the Jiajing Guangdong Tongzhi, which says:

  The censors Qiu Daolong and He Ao one after the other memorialized:
  “Everyone says that [the Portuguese] are rebellious and domineering,
  that they drove away their own lord. It seems that they are rebellious
  vassals of [the nation of] Nanboli. In a previous year, they secretly
  sent Huozhe Yasan to pose as a Rites envoy sent from the Sultanate of
  Melaka.”^([761])

This passage does not give precise quotations of earlier sources, for
the words that it attributes to Qiu Daolong and He Ao are inaccurate to
say the least.^([762]) It only captures the general flavor of their
memorials. For that reason, when it claims that “Huozhe Yasan posed as a
Rites envoy sent from the Sultanate of Melaka,” we should not take these
precise words too seriously. As Chapter 11 of this book argued, the
long-standing accusation against the Portuguese ambassadors was
precisely that they were impostors, posing as Melakan envoys. The
Guangdong Tongzhi seems to express that general idea, even as it makes
numerous mistakes in the details.

Finally, some historians have doubted that a mere interpreter could be
the focus of so much attention in the Chinese sources. It is for this
reason that Tien-Tse Chang claimed that Yasan was in fact Tomé Pires
himself.^([763]) However, such concerns are unnecessary. The
interpreters were often the focus of scandals in Ming foreign
affairs.^([764]) Indeed, Yasan was not the only case in which an
interpreter was confused with an ambassador. In the late sixteenth
century, the famous scholar Yan Congjian (嚴從簡) complained:

  Greedy ruffians rebelliously follow the rich crowds of foreign
  countries, such as Song Suqing (宋素卿) of Japan, Xie Wenbin (謝文彬)
  of Siam, Huozhe Yasan of Portugal. People do not realize their
  motives. This ultimately allows them not only to trade and do petty
  tasks, but also to spy on the capital.^([765])

The case of Song Suqing is instructive. He was part of the Japanese
embassy which provoked the Battle of Ningbo in 1523.^([766]) After the
battle, the Ming officials conducted an investigation on him. They
discovered the following:

  In the Hongzhi years, he had secretly followed the Japanese envoy
  Tangsi Wulang (湯四五郎)^([767]) and fled away. The King [of Japan]
  loved and favored him, and granted him a position. […] And then he
  became chief ambassador (正使).^([768])

Was Song Suqing the chief ambassador of the Japanese embassy? The
Japanese sources make it clear that he was not. The chief ambassador was
Rankō Zuisa (鸞岡瑞佐).^([769]) The sources do not state Suqing’s status
in the embassy—he was perhaps an interpreter. In short, the confusion
between interpreters and ambassadors that occurred with Huozhe Yasan was
not uncommon.

As a final, somewhat tangential note, if the foregoing arguments are
valid, then most likely, the person whom our sources call “Huozhe Yasan”
was not in fact Huozhe Yasan. Huozhe Yasan was the name of the chief
interpreter of the Portuguese embassy. This would have been the Chinese
man from Jiangxi whom Gu Yingxiang met aboard Fernão Peres de Andrade’s
ship in 1517.^([770]) But Vieira’s extant letter says that the chief
interpreter died of disease during the voyage.^([771]) That is to say,
the “true” Huozhe Yasan passed away.

The person who arrived in Beijing bearing the name “Yasan” was probably
one of the assistant interpreters. Cristóvão Vieira reported that the
names within the embassy were fixed, regardless of personnel changes.
For example the government escorts insisted on calling Vieira “Tristão
de Pina,” because he had replaced Pina on the team, and the escorts did
not want to alter the roster.^([772]) In the same way, after the true
Yasan died, the escorts probably gave the name to his successor.

In any case, this name confusion doubtless made little difference to
Tomé Pires. In the Portuguese sources, the interpreters almost
invariably bear Christian names, such as Simão or João.^([773]) That is
to say, they had been baptized after the conquest of Melaka. Because of
this, Pires probably did not know that his interpreter was called “Sir
Hassan” in Chinese—he simply called him by his baptismal name.

Part 8: Mao Cheng’s 1521 Report on the Portuguese

The memorial that Mao Cheng presented on January 23, 1521 is not extant,
and determining its contents requires some reconstruction. It is the
focus of Chapter 12 of this book, but in order to make that chapter
easier to read, the heavy philological baggage has been moved here to
the appendix.

1.  a.

    The Sources

     

There are two major sources which give information about the memorial.
The most precise source is Cristóvão Vieira. From the accounts of other
foreigners who visited the Ming Court during this period, such as the
Joseon official Choe Bu, it is known that the court gave transcripts of
relevant diplomatic proceedings to ambassadors.^([774]) Presumably, Tomé
Pires was given a copy of Mao Cheng’s report, which the interpreters
then translated, and which Vieira in turn summarized.

Vieira’s extant second letter describes the overall structure of the
memorial:

  There were brought to him three letters against the Portuguese: one
  from two mandarins in Beijing, another from the mandarins of
  Guangdong, and another from the Malays.^([775])

In other words, the memorial had three main sections: (1) reports from
“two officials of Beijing” (2) a dossier of letters from Guangdong (3) A
letter from the Bintan embassy. Vieira’s letter gives details about
items 2 and 3. However, he does not describe the contents of item 1 (the
two Beijing officials).

The second major source that gives information about the memorial is the
Ming Court Records. The Court Records usually do not record the original
memorials verbatim, but only offer an abbreviated summary. That seems to
be true in this case. Fortunately, it gives a fairly detailed synopsis
of the memorials of Qiu Daolong and He Ao—that is to say, item 1 (the
“two officials of Beijing”). However, it only briefly mentions the
letters from Guangdong and from Bintan in the opening sentences.

Clearly, Vieira’s letters and the Court Records complement each other
well, for each fills in the gap left by the other: the Court Records
tell us about item 1, and Vieira tells us about items 2 and 3. Smaller
fragments of the memorial also appear in scattered Ming documents, which
will be cited below.

1.  b.

    The Contents of the Guangdong Letters

     

In regard to the dossier of letters from Guangdong (item 2), Vieira
gives a somewhat chaotic description. Sorting through his scattered
comments, roughly three documents can be perceived:

1.  1.

    The 1517 Memorial of Supreme Commander Chen Jin

     

Vieira describes a letter which says the following:

  [The officials] had heard that these people had seized Melaka, which
  was pillaged, and many people killed. [They said] that the king should
  not receive their gift, and that if he wished to receive it, that [the
  Portuguese] should say which kingdoms were adjacent to the kingdom of
  the Portuguese.^([776])

This matches fairly closely with what is known about Chen Jin’s 1517
memorial.^([777]) First, according to the Ministry of Rites, Chen Jin
reported that the Portuguese “relied on great strength to cut through
Melaka and Sumendala, pillaging everywhere.”^([778]) And then, according
to the Court Records, Chen Jin insisted on the geographical uncertainty
of the country: “Of the various foreign countries of the Southern Ocean
(海南), there is none called Portugal.”^([779]) He thus recommended to
reject the embassy: “they cannot be trusted.”

1.  2.

    The 1518 Letter from the “Governors of Guangdong,” Carried by Tomé
    Pires to the Court

     

Vieira describes another letter which says the following:

  A great mandarin said that in a letter, we asked for a residence or
  houses in Guangdong, which seemed bad to him, we being foreigners—that
  in place of obedience, we were asking for a residence in the
  land.^([780])

This matches a description from João de Barros (presumably, following
the lost letter of Cristóvão Vieira), which mentions a 1518 letter that
Tomé Pires received from the “governors of Guangdong.” Barros summarizes
the letter as follows:

  In addition, this letter said that we were asking for a house in the
  city of Guangzhou to have a trade factory there, and in addition, that
  we were people who were difficult to please, very presumptuous in
  matters of honor.^([781])

This letter is also mentioned in Liang Zhuo’s biography. The guard who
was escorting the Portuguese embassy to Beijing wrote that they had a
request:

  The foreigners […] want to buy a place in Nantou, in Dongguan County,
  in order to facilitate their comings and goings.^([782])

1.  3.

    A Memorial Written in Late 1520, Probably in Direct Response to Yang
    Tinghe’s Request for an Investigation

     

Vieira gives a confused description of this document. He seems to
mention three separate letters, but most likely, the first letter was
embedded within the second, and the second was embedded within the
third. In other words, there was probably only one, multilayered letter.
This tiered structure reflects the Ming administrative hierarchy, in
which higher officers summarized reports from their subordinates, who,
in turn, summarized reports from their own subordinates.

At the lowest level, Vieira describes a missive from a tax agent:

  Another mandarin said that in the year of 1520, on the Island of
  Trade, the Portuguese struck his hat and gave him blows and seized
  him, when he went to collect the taxes under the orders of the
  mandarins of Guangdong.^([783])

Certainly, this letter was embedded within another. The reason for
believing this is that it was written by a tax agent—that is to say, a
yahang (牙行). These were private merchants who were contracted by the
government.^([784]) Such people were technically not government
officials, and did not have the right to write directly to the Ministry
of Rites. Instead, they would have written to their immediate superiors:
the mid-level coastal officials.

The second letter, which embeds the first, was from the mid-level
coastal officials: either the Assistant Director of the Foreign Trade
Bureau or one of the naval officers, such as the Provincial Surveillance
Commissioner. Vieira does not say precisely who wrote it, simply
speaking of “the mandarins who go to the trade island.” He describes
their letter as follows:

  The Mandarins who went to the Island of trade to receive the taxes
  under the orders of the mandarins of Guangdong make it known to the
  king that they went in such a year and day, and that it was to collect
  taxes. There came foreign people with many weapons and artillery,
  strong people. And they did not pay the taxes according to tradition.
  And they are making forts.^([785])

This letter summarizes the tax agent’s letter, and also adds other
complaints about the Portuguese: that they were resisting taxes and
making a fortress. Perhaps these additional complaints came from other
tax agents who were also visiting Tamão.

Finally, there is a third letter which seems to embed the second. This
one is from the “mandarins of Guangdong,” that is to say, the
provincial-level officials, seated in Guangzhou. This would most likely
be the supreme commander or the administration commissioner. Vieira
describes it as follows:

  The letter of the mandarins of Guangdong said that the Portuguese did
  not want to pay taxes, and that they took taxes from the Siamese, and
  they took them and sealed their junks, and put guards in them and did
  not let them trade or pay taxes, and that they had a fortress made of
  stone, covered with tiles and circled with artillery, and inside, many
  weapons, and that they stole dogs [children]^([786]) and that they ate
  them roasted, and that they came into Guangzhou by force, and that
  they carried cannonballs in skiffs, discovering the rivers, that they
  shot cannonballs in front of the city and in other forbidden
  places.^([787])

This letter summarizes the complaints of the second letter (abusing a
tax agent, resisting taxes, building a fortress) and also adds other
complaints: harassing the Siamese, eating children, shooting cannons,
etc. It is the most comprehensive letter, coming from the highest
officials.

The comprehensive letter from the “mandarins of Guangdong” apparently
expressed the cumulated concerns about the Portuguese from the past
three years (1517–1520). The list of complaints includes very early
events. Namely, the accusation of “shooting cannonballs in front of the
city” is clearly a reference to Fernão Peres de Andrade’s gun salute in
October 1517. The list also includes very recent events. Vieira directly
says that the tax agent incident occurred in 1520, and that would
presumably be during the summer, the high trading season. The letter
most likely was prepared in direct response to Yang Tinghe’s request for
an investigation on the Portuguese in October 1520.^([788])

On a side note, the Ming official Wang Xiwen (王希文) perhaps had a copy
of this letter as well. In a 1529 memorial, he seems to quote it:

  [The Portuguese] presumptuously disobeyed the rules and did not pay
  taxes (抽分); they roasted and ate children; they kidnapped men and
  women; they built fences to strengthen themselves; they had cannons
  and ran unchecked across the open sea.^([789])

Xiwen was one of the leading literati of the county of Dongguan (where
the fortress of Nantou was located). He presumably had access to the
county archives.

1.  c.

    The Contents of the Bintan Embassy’s Letter

     

In regard to the letter from the Bintan embassy (item 3), it seems that
the letter was not written by the Sultan, but rather by the envoys
themselves. Vieira’s lost letter says quite precisely that the letter
was “from an ambassador named Tuam Mahamed.”^([790]) His extant letter
says more broadly that the letter was from “the Malays.”^([791])

In regard to the contents, Vieira first gives the following description:

  The Malays said that the ambassador from the King of Portugal who was
  in the land of China did not come in truth, that he had false come to
  the land of China in order to deceive, and that we would see lands and
  then afterwards come against them, and since we were placing a stone
  and had a house, afterwards we would have the land as our own, and
  that we had done so in Melaka and in other parts, that we were
  bandits.^([792])

In a later passage, he gives what seems to be another summary of
the same letter:

  The Portuguese, bandits with great hearts, came to Melaka with many
  people and took the land and destroyed it. They killed many people and
  pillaged it. And they captured another. And the other people remain
  below the justice of the Portuguese, for which the King who had been
  of Melaka has a sad and troubled heart. With great fear he took the
  seal of the King of China and fled to Bintam, where he is [now]. And
  my brothers and relatives fled to other lands. The ambassador of the
  King of Portugal who is in the land of China is false. He does not
  come in truth, that he comes to deceive the land of China. Since the
  King of China gives mercy to the King of Melaka, with a troubled heart
  he sends a present, asking for help and people in order that his land
  might be returned.^([793])

The biography of Wang Hong also has a passage which seems to correspond
to this letter:

  Like tigers they watch the corners of the sea. They ambition to
  swallow and conquer. They draw plans and set up stones. They direct
  and manage various foreigners.^([794])

Wang Hong was the naval surveillance commissioner at the time when the
Bintan embassy arrived, in 1519.^([795]) In this position, he greeted
foreign embassies. It is possible that he originally received the letter
from the Bintan envoys and then transmitted it to the court. He must
have made a copy of it before forwarding it on.

v. 1 (Minneapolis: University of Minnesota Press, 1977), 381.

Goes, Father Mergulhão (Vieira, 32–33).

refused to see the Portuguese on February 11, 1518 (Wu zong shi lu,
卷158, date: 正德十三年春正月壬寅).

正德九年夏四月己丑. Note that the reconstruction that this chapter
offers of these confusing events differs from that of Morris Rossabi:
See his “Ming China and Turfan, 1406–1517,” in From Yuan to Modern China
and Mongolia (Brill, 2014), 56; “Ming Foreign Policy: The Case of Hami.”
In From Yuan to Modern China and Mongolia, 17–37: Brill, 2014, 35.
Rossabi basically relies on the Ming Court Records (Wuzong Shilu
武宗實錄). But I consider Chen Hongmo to be a more reliable source,
since he clearly was closely involved in the events. Furthermore, in
contrast to the highly partisan Court Records, Chen Hongmo is equally
critical of both Peng Ze and Wang Qiong.

[529] The term for the “palace” given in this passage is curious. As
mentioned in Chapter 5, before departing for Nanjing, the Emperor made
an alter-ego for himself: 鎮國公朱壽 (Zhu Shou, the Lord who protects
the country). In this passage of Liang Zhuo’s biography, he says that
Yasan returned from 鎮國府. I have thus translated this as “the
emperor’s palace.” This is doubtless in the city of Tongzhou, where the
Emperor was residing at the time.

[609] While no records of the time directly say so, a much later court
memorial makes the following mysterious comment:
“遷設市舶提舉司、稅其貨。正德間、移泊高州電白縣.” The Portuguese and
Chinese sources make it clear that the branch of the Foreign Trade
Bureau was in the Fortress of Nantou, in the province of Dongguan,
throughout the Zhengde era. Thus, the move to Dianbai must have happened
at the very end of the era: in the early days after the Jiajing emperor
was enthroned. See Xi zong shi lu 熹宗實錄, [Veritable Records of the

[755] From October 1517-January 1520, Yasan stayed at Huaiyuan Station,
in the entourage of Tomé Pires. During that time, Wang Hong was the
Provincial Surveillance Commissioner. The Ming Court Records say that he
was finally promoted out of that position on March 4, 1523 (Shi zong shi
lu, 卷23, date: 嘉靖二年二月己丑). That means that between 1517–1520,
Huozhe Yasan and Wang Hong could have potentially seen each other at
various official events.
